322

Worldbreaking

WHERE WE WENT WRONG

One must always know the boundaries with great precision.

—FRIEDRICH AUGUST WOLF, ENCYCLOPÄDIE DER PHILOLOGIE1

WE  DO  NOT  NORMALLY  STUDY  or  teach  ancient  history  in  this  manner.  Why
not?  Had  certain  trends  continued,  we  might  have  embraced  a  globalizing
vision a long time ago. But as it happened, scholarship took a very different
turn, leading us farther and farther away from inclusive, humanity-centered
approaches.  The  world  the  worldbuilders  made  was  broken  up,  smashed
into pieces by their learned descendants.

The  story  of  how  this  unfolded  would  fill  a  much  longer  book.  It
certainly  deserves  one.  All  I  can  do  here  is  engage  with  traditions  of
scholarship  that  have  become  dominant  in  Western  countries.  I  focus  in
particular  on  developments  in  intellectual  and  academic  circles  in
eighteenth-  and  nineteenth-century  Germany,  which  proved
to  be
disproportionately influential. Just as contemporary universities around the
globe are profoundly indebted to the German model of organizing research
and  teaching,  so  are  the  ways  in  which  we  have  learned  to  deal  with  the
remains of the ancient world.

In  this  chapter,  I  ask  why  the  unprecedented  expansion  of  connectivity
on  a  planetary  scale  of  the  last  few  hundred  years,  and  the  equally
unprecedented  expansion  and  exchange  of  historical  knowledge  it  made
possible, did not foster a global perspective of the human past. I show how

professionalization,  nationalism,  and  racism  interacted  to  create  an
academic  landscape  carved  up  into  ever-smaller  parcels  that  became
separated  by  increasingly  impermeable  boundaries;  and  how  a  novel  form
of  worship  of  ancient  Greece  and  Rome  provided  a  model  of  conceptual
segregation  that  boosted  these  divisions  while  securing  an  exceptionally
privileged  position  for  those  two  cultures—so  privileged  in  fact  that  they
came  to  be  equated  with  “ancient  history”  as  such  while  broader
perspectives  fell  by  the  wayside.  I  move  back  and  forth  between  different
levels  of  resolution—the  study  of  Greece  and  Rome,  of  antiquity,  and  of
history  in  general—all  of  which  contributed  to  setting  the  course  for  our
engagement with the ancient past.

Even  though  my  survey  is  necessarily  highly  selective  (or  even
reductive,  not  to  mention  disrespectful),  my  main  point  should  not  be
controversial:  that  over  the  last  250  years,  academic  ideals  and  practices
have  made  it  ever  harder  for  us  to  explore,  understand,  and  publicize  our
shared  foundational  history.  In  an  apparent  paradox,  these  trends  gained
momentum  just  as  spectacular  advances  in  the  decipherment  of  ancient
scripts  and  languages  together  with  the  rise  of  archaeological  fieldwork
made such a foundational history feasible for the first time.

Matching our knowledge of that history with a vision that does it justice
remains  a  task  for  us  today  and  for  future  generations.  In  this  respect,  the
history of scholarship has an important role to play: by reminding us how
much scholarship has changed in the past, it gives us hope that it can do so
again. If we want to understand where we are, we must know how we got
here. That understanding is not merely an end in itself: it is both a necessary
step toward reform—to re-forming our habits of engagement and inquiry—
and a safeguard against repeating the mistakes of the past.

Bigger Histories for a Bigger World

I  began  the  introduction  to  this  book  by  pointing  out  how,  in  Western
countries,  the  term  “ancient  history”  tends  to  have  a  narrow  meaning,
commonly limited to the history of Greece and Rome with or without their
immediate  neighbors.  One  might  think  that  this  is  not  a  big  deal,  even
natural—why  wouldn’t  societies  privilege  the  early  history  of  their  own

corner  of  the  globe  and  their  cultural  forebears,  real  or  imagined?  And  of
course  historiography  has  always  been  mostly  local,  regional,  or  ethnic  in
focus. At the same time, however, ecumenically minded historians had long
tried to keep track of widening horizons. As connections between different
parts of the world expanded, so did historical vision.2

Experimentation  with  “global”  history  goes  back  to  antiquity  itself,  to
authors such as Herodotus and Pompeius Trogus in the Mediterranean and
Sima  Qian  in  Han  China.  Islamic  scholars  subsequently  took  a  lead
following  the  growth  of  their  religious  ecumene.  The  most  famous
examples are al-Masudi’s Meadows of Gold and Mines of Gems of the tenth
century,  a  universal  history  from  the  times  of  Abraham  that  ranged  from
Europe to China, and Rashid al-Din Fadlullah Hamadani’s Compendium of
Chronicles around 1300 that rivaled it in scope.

Locked  as  they  were  into  a  comparatively  smaller  world,  medieval
European  writers  produced  narrower  “world  histories.”  That  was  still  the
case  when  Sir  Walter  Raleigh  divided  his  Historie  of  the  World  in  Five
Bookes  of  1614  into  biblical  and  Greco-Roman  history.  But  before  long,
European  overseas  expansion  gave  rise  to  more  genuinely  universal
accounts, which swelled in number from the late fifteenth century onward.
This  became  a  global  fashion,  as  authors  based  in  East  Asia,  India,  the
Ottoman sphere, and the Americas benefited from the widening of horizons
in the sixteenth and seventeenth centuries.3

Meanwhile,  thanks  to  its  nodal  position  in  these  expansions,  Europe
moved to the forefront of this trend. In the third quarter of the seventeenth
century, Georg Horn, a German professor of history at Leiden (and one of
the  earliest  proponents  of  the  tripartite  scheme  of  ancient,  medieval,  and
modern),  showed  what  was  possible  by  including  China  and  Indigenous
American cultures in his world historical surveys.4

Some of these projects reveled in the accumulation of facts, an approach
that  culminated  in  the  outsized  multiauthored  Universal  History,  from  the
Earliest  Account  of  Time  to  the  Present  that  was  published  in  Britain
between 1736 and 1766 in fifty-one volumes (and in sixty-four volumes by
the  time  of  the  third  edition  of  1779–1784)  and  spawned  at  least  partial
translations  into  several  other  European  languages.  Comprehensive  global

coverage (from the vantage point of Europe’s fastest-rising power) was the
goal:  seven  volumes  were  dedicated  to  antiquity  and  one-half  each  of  the
other forty-four to later European history and to other continents.5

Other  efforts  adopted  a  more  philosophical  outlook,  going  back  to  the
French  bishop  Jacques  Bossuet’s  Discours  sur  l’histoire  universelle  of
1681.  Answering  the  challenge  of  the  Universal History,  which  had  been
translated  into  German  from  1744  onward  and  (even)  further  expanded  in
the process, fell to two historians at the University of Göttingen, founded in
1737  to  promote  Enlightenment  ideals  and  innovative  scholarship:  Johann
Christoph Gatterer and August Ludwig Schlözer.6

The  first  volume  of  Gatterer’s  universal  history,  published  in  1761,
provided  a  semiglobal  ancient  history  that  covered  North  Africa  and
Eurasia  from  the  Atlantic  to  India  up  to  500  CE,  followed  by  another
volume three years later that dealt with China, Korea, Tibet, and Japan from
ancient times to the present. Drawing heavily on building blocks furnished
by recent French historiography, Gatterer aimed to synthesize world history
in a more coherent fashion.7

Over several decades, multiple revisions covered the same ground over
and  over  again  as  Gatterer  struggled  to  optimize  his  presentation.  These
iterations  were  the  product  of  open-ended  experimentation  on  how  to
organize  world  history  on  a  planetary  scale—experimentation  worth
remembering  because  it  so  clearly  illuminates  a  road  not  taken  by  the
emerging historical profession, with consequences for our understanding of
the past that have made themselves felt ever since.8

Gatterer initially sought to order and present key facts in a compendium
that treated all of human history as a single unified and indivisible domain.
At  first,  he  relied  on  standardized  templates  for  conveying  information
about  particular  cultures  or  clusters  of  cultures,  constructing  rather  than
narrating or analyzing universal history. He then devised a different model,
prioritizing connections and interactions between nations in a synchronized
account.  National  histories  were  arranged  within  several  major  phases,
turning them into components of interactive systems.

This  elevated  change  and  systems  to  key  concepts,  increasing  the  need
for  commentary  and  posing  new  challenges:  tracking  interactions  was  a

more  complex  and  open-ended  task  than  adding  details  about  cases.  In  a
further step, Gatterer tried to integrate his two schemes by fusing national
units and systems, and upgraded the role of cultural development relative to
political conditions.

Going  forward,  Gatterer  further  emphasized  developments  relative  to
features,  as  well  as  interaction  and  interdependence.  Conditions  in  a
particular  period  rather  than  in  any  one  country  moved  to  the  center  of
attention,  a  shift  that  drew  more  attention  to  ideas,  economy,  technology,
and  daily  life.  In  that  regard,  he  was  in  agreement  with  his  colleague-
competitor  Schlözer,  who  in  his  shorter  introduction  to  universal  history
had  already  decreed  that  “the  invention  of  fire,  of  bread,  of  liquor  etc  are
facts as worthy of attention as” famous battles. As a result, Gatterer strove
for a total history that covered all aspects of the past, even as that objective
proved difficult to fulfill and mostly led to antiquarian accumulation.9

Undeterred, Gatterer continued his quest for totalizing integration. In his
final version of 1792, an “attempt” to cover the history of the world up to
1492 on a scale he deemed necessary to “use the term world history in its
true meaning,” he experimented with a hybrid format in which each period
was  covered  in  two  ways:  while  the  political  history  of  nations  was
presented  case  by  case,  the  “people’s  history”  of  commerce,  art,  science,
social conditions, and religion was discussed by period. This final endeavor
reflects  his  determination  to  combine  seemingly  incompatible  organizing
principles, “according to the chronological sequence of peoples and events,
and  according  to  their  synchronicity.”  Unsurprisingly,  this  ambition
exceeded  the  capacity  of  a  single  scholar:  the  “people’s  history”  ended  in
146 BCE, and South, Central, and East Asia took up only one-eighth of the
text.10

Meanwhile,  Schlözer  had  already  gestured  in  the  same  direction:  he
likewise distinguished between actual connections and mere synchronicity,
and emphasized the challenge of combining both, even as it was impossible
to accommodate them in equal measure. The overall goal was to understand
the path to the present: “We want to survey in their entirety the revolutions
of the earth that we inhabit and of the human race that we belong to, so as
to understand the current condition of both.”11

These efforts afford us a tantalizing glimpse of what might have been: a
commitment  to  global  perspectives  coupled  with  energetic  exploration  of
how  to  develop  and  employ  them—of  how  to  make  world  history  work.
Any  global  approach  to  the  past  today  grapples  with  the  same  issues  as
these  Göttingen  professors  had  done.  Instead  of  recovering  from  a
multigenerational  hiatus,  as  it  has  only  recently  managed  to  do,  debate
could  have  taken  off  in  earnest  more  than  200  years  ago,  shaping  modern
historiography in fruitful and unexpected ways.12

This  is  all the  more  true  given  that in  1764  Gatterer  sought  to  set  up a
historical  academy  that  was  meant  to  establish  the  study  of  history  as  a
separate discipline modeled on the exact sciences and to incorporate inputs
from natural history (such as Gatterer’s own work on climatic variation), a
push toward total history that faintly foreshadowed the “Annales school” of
the  twentieth  century.  However,  this  ambitious  holistic  approach  was
rebuffed by  the  emerging  local  academy  that  claimed  authority  over  pure
research.  Thus  limited  to  a  more  modest  scope,  the  Royal  Institute  of  the
Historical  Sciences—the  world’s  first  History  department—did  not  in  the
end survive its founder.13

Then  again,  with  the  benefit  of  hindsight,  failure  had  always  been  the
most likely outcome. While earlier Christian universal histories, predicated
on  the  premise  of  humanity’s  shared  descent  from  Noah,  had—however
imperfectly—sought  to  cover  the  whole  world  and  early  Enlightenment
historiography  had  embraced  East  Asia,  later  versions,  preoccupied  with
human  progress,  already  began  to  center  European  cultures  and  their
achievements.14

And  that  was  only  the  start:  in  Gatterer’s  days,  there  was  no  way  of
anticipating  just  how  nationalist  and  racist  much  of  nineteenth-century
academia  would  become.  All  that  might  have  been  feasible  would  have
been  a  warped  version  of  world  history—world  history  as  a  scorecard—
akin  to  some  early  anthropology  but  far  removed  from  any  universalizing
principles.  Nowhere  was  this  more  obvious  than  in  Göttingen  itself:  as
universal  history  declined,  fame  and  influence  flowed  instead  to  the  local
pioneers  of  “scientific”  racism,  the  polygenist  and  anti-Enlightenment

historian  Christoph  Meiners  and  the  equally  prominent  craniometrist
Johann Blumenbach.

Even  if  such  characters  had  not  appeared  on  the  scene,  neohumanistic
preferences were bound to push back against totalizing versions of history,
just  as  historicism  would  have  rejected  universalizing  templates.  And
science was not yet able to support transdisciplinary historiography. All in
all, the project was doomed. What academia got instead was a bifurcation
between  lofty  philosophical  histories  à  la  Herder,  Hegel,  and  Marx  that
harnessed history as a means to preordained ends on the one hand and ever-
narrowing  specialization  on  the  other.  Ancient  history,  which  had  been
integral to universal histories (especially as it proved more manageable than
later  periods)  and  was  claimed  by  Gatterer’s  institute,  was  ineluctably
caught up in this process.15

Reaction and Revelation

At the end of the day, the vision of a truly global historiography had been a
long  shot.  But  that  does  not  mean  that  intense  localism  was  the  only
plausible alternative. To  be sure,  in  Europe just as elsewhere,  engagement
with the past had always favored the closer and better known over the more
distant and obscure. Ancient Rome, and to a lesser degree ancient Greece,
had long occupied a particularly prominent position in European intellectual
circles.  Latin  and  Greek  languages  and  literatures  played  a  central  role  in
the  Christian  church,  medieval  scholasticism,  and  Renaissance  humanism,
as well as legal, political, medical, and scientific discourse.

Even so, other ancient cultures had received their share of attention and
respect,  most  notably  Egyptians  and  Hebrews,  which  were  considered  not
merely  older  but  sometimes  also  wiser  than  the  Greeks  and  Romans.  The
eminent  seventeenth-century  scholar  Joseph  Scaliger,  to  name  just  one
stellar  example,  took  a  unified  tradition  for  granted  as  he  studied  Hebrew
and tried to decipher Egyptian hieroglyphs alongside engaging with Greek
and Roman materials.16

How  did  modernizing  development  affect  engagement  with  antiquity?
Let  us  consider  this  question  from  first  principles.  Which  choices  were
realistically  available  in,  say,  Protestant  northwestern  Europe  in  the  mid-

eighteenth century? The boldest option would have been to let the dead past
bury  its  dead  and  move  on  into  a  mold-breaking  future.  But  that  is  not
something  any  actual  society  has  ever  been  prepared  to  do:  even  the
ultraradical Khmer Rouge cherished the Angkorian tradition.

Alternatively,  in  keeping  with  the  new  mantra  of  improvement  and
progress,  history  could  have  been  pressed  into  the  service  of  the
Enlightenment in an attempt to understand all of humankind and its gradual
if  uneven  embrace  of  reason.  The  Göttingen  professors  and  later
philosopher-historians tried but ultimately failed.

Enter  the  discontents.  Modernization  invited  pushback—a  response
rather than an embrace. There was growing demand for a counterweight  to
the perceived evils of rationalism and utilitarianism the Enlightenment had
unleashed,  to  modern  science,  technology,  and  capitalism.  The  emerging
Romantic  movement  offered  a  dramatic  rebuttal,  with  its  organicist  vision
of  human  development  that  challenged  mechanistic  principles  and  its
rejection  of  utilitarian  perspectives  in  favor  of  valuing  that  which  was
valuable in and of itself.

Complicating  matters,  Romanticism  was  not  merely  reactionary  in  its
yearning for an idealized past but also progressive in its opposition to an old
order  of  religious  orthodoxy  and  aristocratic  privilege,  and  in  its  push  for
upward  mobility  through  accessible  education  and  what  German  calls
“Bildung.” The latter—meaning “shaping” or “formation” and perhaps best
translated as comprehensive cultivation beyond mere education—became a
new ideal of aesthetic development and cultivation of the self.

Bildung was conceived of not as self-fashioning in response to external
expectations or for a particular useful purpose but as a process that allowed
one’s  innate  endowments  to  develop  in  a  way  that  ensured  harmony
between  that  process  and  those  properties.  Bildung  could  therefore  only
draw  out  into  the  open  what  was  already  contained  within,  as  an  organic
attribute  of  the  individual.  From  that  vantage  point,  as  the  Romantic  poet
and intellectual Friedrich Schlegel summed it up, “the highest good and the
only useful thing is Bildung.”17

This ideal encouraged the search for past counterworlds that by virtue of
their  inherent  excellence  could  guide  this  process  of  cultivating  the  inner

self. At that time, versions of this quest had already had a long pedigree and
have  survived  ever  since:  the  humanities  in  general  are  routinely  called
upon to confront assorted societal ills and improve young minds to avert the
end of civilization.18

The  Romantic  project,  however,  went  beyond  traditional  humanism  in
envisioning deep fractures that obstructed true Bildung. It cast the contrast
between  ancient  and  modern  as  one  between  harmony  and  its  loss.  It
pictured ancients who enjoyed true unity—of body and mind, of individual
and  collective—a  unity  captured  by  the  statues  of  impossibly  gorgeous
anthropomorphic gods that represented a unified ideal of human and divine.
In the Romantic version of events, Christianity first destroyed that unity
(alongside  that  of  mind  and  body)  and  modernity  subsequently  amplified
the problem by alienating the individual from society. Predating those falls,
the ancients had been pure: according to Friedrich Schiller, their poetry was
“naïve”  where  its  modern  counterpart  was  “sentimentalist”;  the  difference
was one between naturalness and artifice. Those lost unities, harmonies, and
purities had receded beyond our grasp: they could be articulated merely as a
“destination of yearning.” No matter: as Stefan Matuschek puts it in his new
history  of  Romanticism,  “A  merely  imagined  motherland  is  adequate.
Romanticism is the art of building metaphysical castles in the air. This art
works  even  when  one  knows  that  they  are  castles  in  the  air,  and  thus
illusions.”19

What  imagined  motherlands  were  at  hand?  The  reactive  nature  of  the
movement called for a past that was sufficiently different from the present
to  provide  a  suitable  counterweight,  and  one  that  could  plausibly  be
idealized  to  succeed  in  that  role.  Only  three  realistic  candidates  were  on
offer: the biblical tradition, ancient Greece and Rome, and the Middle Ages.
In  the  eyes  of  our  mid-eighteenth-century  northwestern  European
Protestant  observer,  the  medieval  world  would  have  been  handicapped  by
its  hegemonic  Catholicism  and  what  had  come  to  be  regarded  as
stereotypical backwardness remedied by Renaissance humanism. While the
biblical  tradition  was  more  respectable,  especially  in  an  environment  that
was  still  overtly  Christian,  it  likewise  suffered  from  serious  drawbacks:
religion  no  longer  provided  a  compelling  focal  point,  and  Christianity’s

origins  were  not  European  but  Semitic,  indeed  Jewish.  Ancient  Egypt,
meanwhile,  was  impracticable  even  as  an  exotic  option  because  it  was
simply too poorly known.

By  default,  Greeks  and  Romans,  their  traditions  already  deeply
embedded  in  the  cultural  consciousness,  remained  the  most  attractive
choice.  Then  again,  that  embeddedness  cannot  by  itself  explain  the  sheer
scale,  intensity,  and  exclusionarity  of  the  resultant  zeal  and  the  outsized
mark  it  would  leave  on  educational  curricula  and  the  configuration  of
academic scholarship.

Ancient  Rome  had  long  been  the  eight-hundred-pound  gorilla  of  that
canonical  pair:  the  educated  read  Latin,  were  taught  to  imitate  Latin
authors,  and  at  university  wrote  their  dissertations  in  Latin.  Yet  this
profound familiarity had now become a weakness. There was little novelty
value to be had in doubling down on studying the Romans and their Latin;
that would have been just more of the same.

In Germany and among its northern neighbors, additional concerns came
into  play.  As  Romance-language  speakers,  the  Italians  and  French  had  a
stronger  prima  facie  claim  to  proximity  to  Roman  culture.  Its  association
with  France  was  particularly  unpalatable:  as  Europe’s  greatest  political,
military, and cultural force  at  the  time,  that country  was  an object  of both
envy and loathing in Germany’s fractured society. To make matters worse,
French  scholarship  dominated  the  study  of  ancient  Rome,  and  expended
great effort on the Latin rhetorical tradition.

That left the Greeks, not merely by dint of elimination but also because
of  their  convenient  virtues.  Ancient  Greece  may  have  been  perceived  as
more remote and less familiar than Rome, yet was sufficiently well known
and accessible to be studied in depth. Western Europe lacked nations with
special  connections  to  its  legacies:  contemporary  Greece  was  under
Ottoman  rule  and  excluded  from  Europe’s  concert  of  powers;  and  the
Orthodox  Church  barely  registered.  Mastering  the  finer  points  of  ancient
Greek  served  as  a  novel  challenge  for  a  Latin-soaked  intelligentsia.  It
created  a  new  elite  at  an  affordable  rate.  The  ancient  Roman  embrace  of
Greek  culture  offered  a  venerable  precedent,  a  model  for  the  present.
Greece itself was farther away than Italy, yet still part of Europe. All in all,

ancient Greece was the goldilocks option: distant but not too remote, novel
but not too alien, accessible but not undemanding.

And even that was not all. Unlike the mature Roman empire, pre-Roman
Greece  was  safely  detached  from  Christianity.  Just  like  Germany,  it  had
been highly fragmented—very much unlike France, which could be likened
to the Roman empire in both heft and regime. Last but not least, tastes in art
also  played  a  role.  The  artificial  and  courtly  was  out;  the  natural  and
demotic  was  in,  which  once  again  favored  the  Greeks.  Their  art,  as
presented  at  the  time,  was  as  un-Baroque  as  it  could  be.  As  the  Baroque
style,  associated  as  it  was  with  the  Counter-Reformation  and  absolutism,
was falling out of fashion, the time had come for a new ideal of beauty and
purity:  one  of  “noble  simplicity  and  calm  grandeur,”  as  Johann  Joachim
Winckelmann famously described ancient Greek sculpture.20

In view of all that, it is hardly an exaggeration to say that if people at the
time had tried to invent an idealizable ancient past from scratch, they could
hardly  have  done  a  better  job.  The  ancient  Greeks  were  in  a  sense  almost
too good to be true, which is exactly what was required of them: remote and
beautiful  yet  approachable,  a  ready-made  object  of  veneration  and  desire,
and  a  potent  means  of  cleansing  minds  contaminated  by  rationalism  and
modernity—even  as  that  cleansing,  in  one  of  many  paradoxes  we  will
encounter in this chapter, embraced modernizing expectations of academic
professionalism and expertise.

Winckelmann, a Prussian scholar who had risen from modest circumstances
and  supported  himself  as  a  librarian,  famously  provided  major  impetus.
Even before he first arrived in Italy in late 1755 to view Roman copies of
Greek art, he had already formed firm beliefs. The ancient Greeks were the
best. They were the first to generate what made Europe great, and they did
so  on  their  own  even  when  they  drew  on  antecedents.  The  opening  of  his
first pamphlet sums it all up: “The good taste, which is spreading more and
more  through  the  world,  first  began  to  form  under  the  Greek  sky.  All

inventions  of  foreign  peoples  came  as  it  were  only  as  the  first  seed  to
Greece, and assumed a different nature and form in that country.”21

The  Greeks  managed  to  accomplish  that  thanks  to  their  considerable
physical beauty (and their related propensity to run around naked) and their
freedom,  which  allowed  that  beauty  (and  nudity)  to  feed  into  artistic
production. Others were not so lucky: “But in a country in which nature’s
influence was held back by harsh laws, like in Egypt—the given fatherland
of arts and sciences—, nature’s most perfect creatures would have become
only partially and imperfectly known to artists. In Greece, however, where
one dedicated oneself from one’s youth to delight and joy, where a certain
modern  bourgeois  prosperity  never  curtailed  the  freedom  of  manners,  the
beautiful  nature  showed  itself  unconcealed  for  the  great  education  of
artists.”  Thus,  “[t]he  only  way  for  us  to  become  great  and  indeed,  if  it  is
possible, inimitable, is the imitation of the ancients.”22

Given  the  axiomatic  nature  of  these  views,  actual  study  of  ancient
remains  could  only  confirm  them,  as  it  duly  did  in  Winckelmann’s
landmark history of ancient art in 1764. In a nod to the existing tradition of
ancient  world  history,  which  was  still  deeply  entrenched  at  the  time,  he
covered  Egyptians,  Phoenicians,  Persians,  Etruscans,  and  other  Italians
before moving on to Greece and Rome.

Yet these other cultures primarily served as a foil to Hellenic greatness.
Winckelmann began his survey of Egyptian art with the observation that it
failed to evolve or to rise to Greek levels: “the reason may be sought partly
in their physical properties, partly in their way of thinking, and not least in
their customs and laws—and especially their religious ones; and also in the
estimation  and  skills  of  their  artists.”  Held  back  by  bodily  defects,
despotism, xenophobia, and other ills, “the history of Egyptian art is, akin
to the nature of the country, like a large arid plain.”

Meanwhile, the paucity of evidence allowed him to sideline Phoenicians
and  Persians,  secure  in  the  knowledge  that  no  major  discoveries  of  their
artifacts  were  to  be  expected  in  the  future.  Ancient  Hebrews  basically
lacked  art.  Their  mentality  prevented  the  Etruscans  from  attaining  Greek
levels  of  artistry.  By  contrast,  the  Greek  environment  produced  superior
bodies,  a  blessing  of  which  the  Greeks  were  happily  aware.  Another  big

advantage was their freedom, “out of which rose, like a noble branch out of
a healthy trunk, the thought of the entire people.” Owing to these qualities
and  their  felicitous  consequences,  others  did  not  stand  a  chance:  “such
advantages  did  Greek  art  enjoy  compared  to  other  peoples,  and  such
magnificent fruit could grow on such a soil.”23

The  main  pillars  of  the  Hellenophilic  canon  were  all  in  place:  racial
superiority,  cultural  purity,  and  freedom  as  opposed  to  stifling  despotism
elsewhere.  That  Winckelmann  somehow  already  knew  all  this  before  he
even  got  to  work  on  ancient  artifacts  was  not  lost  on  his  admirers;  it  was
considered a virtue. Noting that Winckelmann’s inaugural pamphlet of 1755
predated his first journey to Italy, the philosopher Johann Gottfried Herder,
in his eulogy of 1778, made that perfectly clear: “Rome and Greece already
live in his heart: his ideal of art and antiquity is complete, and may merely
be  applied,  explained  and  reinforced  in  particular  instances.”  After  all,
Winckelmann himself was quite forthright about this when he advised those
encountering Greek artworks “to approach them in a partial manner, for in
the conviction that beauty is to be found, they will seek it, and some of it
will  reveal  itself  to  them.  One  must  return  until  one  has  found  it:  for  it  is
there.”24

Herder’s sympathy did not come out of nowhere. When he first tried his
hand at philosophical history in 1774, the Greeks were already on top: “In
the  history  of  humankind,  Greece  will  forever  remain  the  place  where  it
experienced  its  most  beautiful  youth  and  bridal  bloom.…  Greece  was  the
cradle of humanity, of the peoples’ love, of beautiful legislation, of the most
pleasant  in  religion,  manners,  literary  style,  poetry,  customs,  and  arts.…
Greece! Archetype and model of all beauty, grace and simplicity! Virtue’s
bloom of the human race—if only it could have lasted forever!”25

In  his  telling,  the  origins  of  this  cultural  miracle  were  ostensibly  more
complex  than  Winckelmann  had  allowed  for,  rooted  in  a  mixture  of
Phoenician  and  Egyptian  modes  of  thought—a  remnant  of  what  Martin
Bernal  labeled  the  “Oriental  model”  that  credited  them  with  foundational
influences  on  Greek  development.  But  Herder  took  pains  to  undercut  it
straight  away:  “I  consider  it  undeniable  that  Greece  received  seeds  of
culture, language, arts, and sciences from elsewhere, and this can clearly be

shown  for  some  areas—sculpture,  architecture,  mythology,  literature.  But
that the Greeks might just as well not have received all that, that they gave
all that an entirely new character, so that in every way the ‘beautiful,’ in the
true  sense  of  the  word,  was  without  doubt  their  own  creation.…  Nothing
Oriental, Phoenician, and Egyptian retained its nature: it became Greek and
in  some  ways  they  [i.e.,  the  Greeks]  were  almost  too  much  originals  who
re-dressed and dressed up everything according to their own nature.”26

This marks an inflection point at which external influences could still be
acknowledged  even  as  they  were  on  their  way  out.  In  a  more  expansive
iteration  of  his  philosophy  of  history  thirteen  years  later,  Herder’s  Greeks
were already pure and had developed autonomously, more fully than others:
“Not  only  were  the  Greeks  free  from  admixture  by  alien  nations  and
remained  true  to  themselves  throughout  their  development:  they  also
experienced  the  periods  of  their  development  more  thoroughly  and
traversed the entire path of that development from the smallest beginnings
more fully than any other people in history. The inland nations were either
stalled  at  the  earliest  beginnings  of  culture  and  unnaturally  perpetuated
them in law and custom, or they succumbed to conquest before they could
mature: the flower was cut down before it bloomed.”27

Just as in the writings of Winckelmann and those of later scholars, such
unfavorable comparisons sat uneasily with the dearth of information about
other  early  cultures,  even  as  Herder  confronted  it  head-on.  In  Egypt  and
West Asia “everything appears to us as a ruin or a vanished dream.… The
archives of Babylon, Phoenicia, and Carthage are no more: Egypt was in its
decline,  almost  before  a  single  Greek  visited  its  interior.  Everything  has
been  shrunk  down  to  a  few  faded  pages  that  contain  fables  of  fables,
fragments of history, a dream of the prehistorical world.” Yet the underlying
paradox did not deter: the Greeks were best even though little was known
about the others—except that one could be sure they were less impressive.
the  guiding  principle  for  an  exclusivist
This  was  fast  becoming
preoccupation with Greece and Rome.28

More  generally,  engagement  with  antiquity  was  entering  a  path  toward
hierarchy  and  compartmentalization.  While  Greece  and  Rome  were
separated  from  the  biblical  “Orient”  and  its  neighbors,  Greeks  and  Greek

were upgraded relative to the previously dominant Romans and their Latin.
French cultural hegemony shared the Roman’s fate. In his poem on the “two
fevers,” Schiller poked fun at these shifts:

Scarce has the fever so chilly of Gallomania departed,
When a more burning attack in Grecomania breaks out.29

Winckelmann owed his influence on this process above all to being at the
right  place  at  the  right  time,  providing  a  concrete  destination  for  “soulful
yearning”  as  Germany  was  approaching  peak  Romanticism.  At  the  same
time, outright imitation—a hallmark of earlier humanism and pedagogy and
still prominently invoked by Winckelmann himself in 1755—was losing its
appeal.  The  newly  constructed  ancient  Greece  formed  a  complete  and
completed unity; so did its art and thought, which had been spawned by an
irreproducibly unique physical and sociopolitical environment. The ancient
Greeks  had  been  both  natural  and  free  in  ways  no  modern  observer  could
hope  to  be.  As  a  result,  essential  Greek  qualities  could  not  be  plausibly
recreated, and imitation was beside the point. The best one could hope to do
was to commune with the Greek spirit in ways that were appropriate to the
present.30

The Greeks’ job was not to inspire emulation but to open modern eyes to
true  human  nature  and  to  fill  souls  with  images  of  an  ideal  humanity.
Herder  again:  “Out  of  the  works  of  the  Greeks  the  spirit  of  human  nature
can speak purely and intelligibly to us: for we will listen compassionately,
sympathetically.”31

Because the Greek holistic unity of spirit and culture pervaded all it had
produced,  everything  that  had  survived  was  intrinsically  valuable  and
worthy  of  close  scrutiny.  It  was  scarcely  by  accident  that  this  attitude
resembled  traditional  reverence  for  and  deep  immersion  in  Scripture.
Approaching Greece (and if need be, Rome) in the same manner improved
the  mind  and  spirit  of  acolytes  who  sought  a  deeper  and  more  accurate
understanding of revelatory texts and images.

As  Friedrich  Paulsen,  a  later  chronicler  and  critic  of  Hellenomania,
acerbically remarked, “One might even say: the Greeks became the object
of  a  religious  cult,  namely  the  Greeks  in  contrast  to  Christianity:  the
naturalistic-thisworldly
and
transcendent.”  Just  as  God  had  been  made  flesh  in  Jesus  Christ,  the  very
idea of Man had been made flesh in the Greeks.32

supranaturalistic

contrast

the

to

in

Playful  allusions  to  pagan  leanings  were  not  uncommon  in  Romantic
circles. Philologists were to serve as the priests of this new creed. Life-long
“sacrifice,” as the philologist Georg Friedrich Creuzer emphasized in 1807,
was the philologist’s lot in his holy mission—“appointed as the guardian of
a  divine  heritage  that  belongs  to  all  humankind”—to  restore  and
disseminate “the image of a more divine humankind in every aspect of their
action and thought,” and to never tire in letting shine “the divine light that
radiates  from  those  texts,  and  to  live  and  die  immersed  in  contemplation,
self-denying  and  innocent  in  the  way  in  which  those  divine  spirits
accomplished  their  works.”  Communion  with  a  lost  pagan  world  was
crucial:  the  modern  student  of  that  period  was  to  be  “transferred  into  a
higher  world,  where  ideas  of  the  eternally  true,  good  and  beautiful  are
simpler  and  more  clearly  formed  than  in  contemporary  writings,  and  he
receives the vision of a more divine humankind from a time when the gods
were more human.”33

Thanks  to  their  presumed  affinity  to  the  ancient  Greeks,  modern
Germans were uniquely well equipped to “enter into the entire worship of
the  inspiring  gods.”  Unlike  Christianity,  however,  this  ersatz  religion  was
not really meant for the many. Johann Wolfgang von Goethe opined  more
than once that only the study of Greek and Roman antiquity made us feel
what it was to be human. Yet he also felt that this benefit was limited to the
very best: while small minds stood nothing to gain, “only a noble person …
will develop in the most magnificent manner through contact and intimate
engagement with the majestic character of the Greek and Roman past, and
will day by day grow to a similar stature.” It was those students of antiquity
who reaped the rewards: “by dealing only with the best which the world has
produced … their knowledge gains such an abundance, their judgment such

certitude,  their  taste  such  consistency,  that  they  appear  within  their  circles
as astonishingly, even amazingly educated.”34

Disciplinization

“Amazing  education”  brings  us  to  our  main  theme,  how  this  new  vision
played  out  in  academia.  At  university,  Romantic  inspiration  or  quasi-
religious  fervor  were  not  quite  enough.  Academics  were  unable  to  escape
the  Enlightenment:  holistic  engagement  with  the  Greeks  (and  Romans)
required  serious  scholarship,  indeed  its  own  discipline,  and  ideally  even  a
proper science. In all this, mastery of Greek and Latin took center stage.35

The quest for precision in the study of ancient texts was to parallel that
in the exact sciences, such as astronomy: restoring the original wording was
not  unlike  determining  the  true  position  of  a  celestial  body.  Textual
criticism—the  means  of  reconstructing  the  “urtext”—was  emblematic  of
the  commitment  to  objective  results.  Encyclopedic  study  compensated  for
the lack of replicable experiments.

For the philological enterprise to become as rigorous as a “hard” science,
it needed to acquire attributes that would put it on a par with the study of
nature:  it  had  to  feature  clear  methods  and  boundaries,  and  reveal  truth.
Long after German scholars had taken a lead in this process, it fell to Ernest
Renan, an early French convert to their model, to spell this out most lucidly:
“Philology  is  the  exact  science  of  the  matters  of  the  spirit,”  just  as
chemistry and physics were for the material world.36

In yet another paradox, a new discipline was crafted from fusing modern
science  and  antimodern  Graecomania.  This  required  a  double  squaring  of
the  circle:  elevated  to  a  remedy  against  the  modern  pursuit  of  ostensibly
useful things, the study of Greece and Rome also had to be useful in its own
way,  by  improving  the  minds  of  its  students,  as  well  as  modern,  by
submitting to  the  same  principles  as the  study  of useful things undertaken
by scientists.

To  make  all  this  possible,  increasing  emphasis  came  to  be  placed  on
than  mere
advanced
knowledge. University seminars played a critical role in forging a path to a
science of the ancient past. As it labored to establish praxis, the philological

that  rated  expertise  higher

technical

training

seminar emerged as an engine of discipline formation—a process, in Glenn
Most’s  definition,  that  “denotes  the  establishment  of  a  defined  field  of
objects  of  knowledge  and  of  a  set  of  methods  designed  to  study  it  in  a
regulated and inter-subjectively verifiable manner, in such a way as both to
create a stable body of information which can be exchanged, examined, and
modified synchronically among contemporary researchers on that field and
to insure diachronic transmission to future generations of researchers.”37

At  German  universities,  seminars  as  sites  of  academic  training  dated
back to the sixteenth century. While they traditionally focused on theology
and propedeutic “pedagogy,” the study of Greece and Rome began to come
into  its  own  right  once  a  philological  seminar  was  set  up  at  Göttingen  in
1738 under the professor of eloquence Johann Matthias Gesner. Despite its
name,  instruction  went  well  beyond  Greek  and  Latin  to  include  religion,
pedagogy,  history,  geography,  mathematics,  astronomy,  and  natural
sciences.  Attention  to  ancient  culture  signaled  a  decisive  shift  from
language  acquisition  for  the  limited  purposes  of  reading,  writing,  and
imitation  of  classical  models  to  a  broader  understanding  of  content  and
context that was meant to develop students’ taste, mind, and character.38

This  approach  continued  under  Gesner’s  successor,  Christian  Gottlob
Heyne, who ran the seminar from 1763 to 1809 and turned it into a widely
recognized center of the nascent academic discipline of Greek and Roman
philology.  Instruction  no  longer  sought  to  produce  mere  schoolteachers  or
future  clergymen  but  genuine  scholars.  Heyne  largely  purged  coverage  of
nonancient materials; depth replaced breadth. Venturing beyond occasional
antecedents, this immersive approach became a new norm. The mission was
comprehensive  study  of  all  things  Greek  and  Roman:  history,  art,  and
archaeology  all  received  due  weight,  even  as  mastery  of  language  and
engagement with literature retained its pivotal position.39

This  confluence  of  inclusion  and  hierarchy  is  tellingly  illustrated  by
Heyne’s 1778 eulogy of Winckelmann, in which the author labored to coopt
the  Great  Man  retrospectively  into  the  emerging  field  of  the  “science  of
antiquity”—a  project  meant  to  remedy  the  fact  that  thus  far  “the  study  of
antiquity has been handled in a way that prevented the whole from taking
shape.” Heyne did so by elevating Winckelmann above mere antiquarians—

lesser  beings  who  did  not  know  enough  language  or  the  historical
background  “to  enter  the  mind  of  the  ancient  authors  or  the  spirit  of  the
modern artworks”—onto the more rarefied level of true philology.40

What  raised  Winckelmann  above  the  usual  antiquarians  were  his
superior  language  skills:  “He  read  the  best  authors  of  antiquity,  fashioned
his  taste  after  those  great  models  from  Greece,  nourished  his  imagination
with
images  from  Homer  and  Plato,  and  harvested  a  wealth  of
mythological, historical and poetical knowledge well before he considered
the use he was destined to make of all that in studying the ancient works of
art.”41

One could not ask for a clearer expression of the foundational principle
of  the  field:  language  and  literature  were  to  enjoy  primacy  over  art  and
archaeology.  The  formative  “images”  Heyne  invokes  were  not  visuals  of
actual  objects  but  figurative  images  conveyed  by  the  writings  of  premier
Greek  authors.  No  matter  how  wide  the  scope  of  remains  marshalled  to
access  the  ancient  spirit,  there  was  no  path  to  a  proper  appreciation  of
Greece (or Rome)  other  than  prior  immersion  in  and  mastery  of  language
and literature. Holistic as the field was supposed to be, not all its elements
were  created  equal.  That  premise—conveniently  exemplified  in  Heyne’s
reading  by  none  less  than  the  pioneer  of  ancient  art  history  himself—has
lingered ever since.42

Heyne  embodied  the  archetype  of  the  long-term  seminar  director  who
managed  to  reconcile  a  distinctive  personal  agenda  with  somewhat
conflicting  external  expectations.  The  authorities  that  funded  and  oversaw
his program expected it above all to prepare schoolteachers. Yet while the
seminar  benefited  from  public  funding  and  made  sure  to  meet  reporting
requirements, it effectively evolved into a vehicle of Heyne’s own interest
in  conducting  research  and  training  future  generations  of  like-minded
scholars.  Within  the  university,  the  intense  and  regimented  nature  of  this
training  encouraged  fragmentation  as  it  formally  compartmentalized
processes of teaching and research and fostered growing “disciplinary self-
consciousness in the seminarists.”43

Having  established  these  intellectual  and  organizational  templates,
Heyne’s  seminar  graduated  more  than  three  hundred  men  while  his
encyclopedic survey lectures drew large general audiences. The new model
spread,  initially  replicated  by  alumni  or  other  former  members  of  the
Göttingen  seminar  and  before  long  through  imitation  by  others.  Founders
generally  managed  to  copy  the  practice  of  obtaining  state  funding  for
positions, stipends, and other resources.44

The  model’s  growing  success  materially  affected  the  study  of  ancient
history. Whereas scholars who were interested in antiquity had traditionally
been free to roam far and wide, engagement with Greek and Roman history
began to feel the gravitational pull of the proliferating philology seminars.
The  tightly  circumscribed  discipline  they  championed  sought  to  conscript
that  history  into  the  overarching  mission  of  achieving  a  comprehensive
understanding  of  the  organic  unity  of  the  Greco-Roman  world  in  all  its
aspects,  and  in  the  process  to  subordinate  it  to  the  study  (and  scientific
restoration) of Greek and Latin literature. Just as philology had previously
been  regarded  as  auxiliary  to  theology  and  law,  the  study  of  Greek  and
Roman  history  was  to  occupy  a  similar  position  relative  to  (classical)
philology.

It  is  not  without  irony  that  this  process  should  have  been  given  major
impetus  at  Göttingen  at  the  exact  same  time  when  Gatterer  and  Schlözer
were  grappling  with  the  challenges  of  devising  a  global,  universalizing
approach to history, of which Greece and Rome were to be an integral part.
What  is  even  more  ironic  is  that  Heyne  himself  became  involved  in  the
business of universal history when in 1756 he (albeit briefly) served as the
inaugural  editor  of  the  German  translation  of  a  British  abridgment  of  the
original Universal History  series.  However,  thanks  in  no  small  part  to  the
precocious  institutionalization  of  the  new  Greco-Roman  studies  template
that turned organicist ideal into concrete praxis, the compartmentalizers of
historical studies were swiftly gaining the upper hand.45

But not always and everywhere. Scholars outside the philology seminars
continued  to  lay  claim  to  the  most  prominent  component  of  Greek  and
Roman  history,  the  study  of  states  and  politics.  Such  divisions  arose  from
than  common  consent.
convention  and  personal  preference  rather

Philologists sometimes acquiesced: in his program for the new philological
seminar  in  Heidelberg,  Creuzer  excluded  world  history  and  the  political
history  of  nations  from  its  scope—as  matters  to  be  left  to  the  “teacher  of
history”—but claimed Greek and Roman legal, juridical, and administrative
history  alongside  that  of  religion  and  private  life.  Those  were  the
beginnings of an enduring to and fro: scholars of Greek and Roman history
found  themselves  torn  between  seminars  of  philology  and  history,  never
fully  at  home  in  either.  As  we  shall  see,  this  unstable  positioning  has
continued to the present day.46

Yet  for  a  while  it  seemed  as  if  the  holistic  approach  to  Greece  and  Rome
was  going  to  sweep  everything  before  it. It  is  to  two students  of Heyne’s,
Wilhelm von Humboldt and Friedrich August Wolf, that we owe the boldest
blueprints  of  a  program  that  fused  Romantic  excitement  with  disciplined
professionalization.

Humboldt,  a  well-connected  scion  of  the  Prussian  titled  gentry,  was  an
enthusiastic Hellenophile who had audited Heyne’s classes. Wolf embarked
on an academic career, ardently committed to the new model of philology
for  philology’s  sake:  as  an  incoming  student  he  had  already  taken  the
emancipatory principle to new heights by insisting on matriculating only as
a philologist, which even Heyne deemed reckless. Following through, Wolf
went on to found a copycat seminar in Halle in 1787.47

Soon after befriending Wolf on a visit to Halle in 1792, Humboldt sent
him a letter in which he outlined his views of the benefits of studying the
Greeks: “Alongside all particular studies and educations of a person there is
a very specific one, which ties the whole person together as it were, which
makes  him  not  only  more  capable,  stronger,  better  in  this  and  that  regard,
but  makes  him  a  greater  and  nobler  person.…  This  education  has  been
diminishing and was present to a very high degree among the Greeks. There
is, in my view, no better way to promote it than through the study of great
and  especially  in  this  respect  admirable  people  or,  to  say  it  in  one  word,

through the study of the Greeks.” For in the end, “no other people combined
so much simplicity and nature with so much culture.”48

This education was to be accomplished by applying the holistic model,
which Humboldt laid out in a manifesto he shared with Wolf in early 1793.
Humboldt’s  goal  was  a  comprehensive  “biography”  of  a  “nation”  that
covered all its attributes, analogous in a sense to Giambattista Vico’s earlier
idea of the unity of the whole being reflected in a people’s spirit. Imitation
was  out,  totalizing  academic  study  was  in;  the  ideal  nature  of  the  subject
matter was to ensure the student’s personal improvement. Thus, Humboldt
held  “that  it  is  the  study  of  the  ancient  nations  that  offers  the
aforementioned  benefit  of  knowing  and  educating  humans  in  the  greatest
abundance.—By ‘ancients’ I mean exclusively the Greeks, and among them
often just the Athenians.”49

Here we encounter a radical narrowing that anticipates a new hierarchy
that puts Athens on top and finds the Romans not even worth mentioning,
seeking  to  subsume  them  under  the  Greeks  when  they  do  come  up.  As
Humboldt put it in a letter he wrote while he was preparing his manifesto,
“The ancients are all authors of just two nations and, if we are precise, of
only one, the Greeks, since the Roman authors must at the end of the day be
called  Greeks.  By  studying  them  one  studies  a  nation,  not  books,  but
people.” Both the “ancient” world and the approved manner of approaching
it shrink before our eyes.50

Humboldt’s  views  mattered  for  two  reasons.  One  is  his  influence  on
Wolf.  Although  the  latter’s  reaction  is  not  recorded—and  seems  to  have
been  sufficiently  cool  to  discourage  publication  of  the  manifesto—in  the
end, he took it seriously enough to quote lengthy passages from it (though
curiously without attribution and misdated) in his own later and much more
comprehensive exposition of the scope and organization of the field. More
specifically,  Wolf’s  claims  regarding  the  benefits  of  philological  study
beyond academic knowledge production—its capacity to improve people—
may well have been greatly indebted to Humboldt’s grandiose vision.51

The  other  and  more  important  reason  is  to  be  found  in  Humboldt’s
subsequent prominence in the education sector. As we will see, in the early
years of the nineteenth century he played a crucial role in establishing the

University of Berlin (which is now named after him) and in reforming the
Prussian  education  system—with  far-reaching  consequences  for
the
positioning  of  Greco-Roman  studies  and  thus  also  for  the  direction  of
Ancient History.

But  before  we  turn  to  academic  politics,  we  need  to  take  a  look  at  the
disciplinary  program  developed  and  promoted  by  Wolf  and  like-minded
contemporaries. Wolf’s own vision of the nature and purposes of the field
reflected  humanistic  as  well  as  protohistoricist  objectives—“Bildung”  and
objective
investigation  of  historically  unique  conditions—and  more
specifically  merged  Heyne’s  pursuit  of  comprehensive  coverage  with
Humboldt’s ideal of Greek-inspired mind-fashioning.52

the

Wolf  mapped  out  his  position  in  lectures  on  the  “encyclopedia  of
philology”  for  a general student  audience that he delivered  eighteen  times
from 1795 onward, first in Halle and later in Berlin. Two versions of later
lecture notes as well as his own published distillation from 1807 allow us to
trace the evolution of his views.53
latter

the  new  discipline
“Altertumswissenschaft,”  a  term  that  is  difficult  to  render  into  English
because  “Science of Antiquity”  carries  associations  of the  “hard”  sciences
that  are  too  narrow  to  do  justice  to  the  German  expression,  even  though
such  a  dimension  was  indeed  present.  After  all,  Wolf  had  to  defend  the
study  of  antiquity  against  modernizers,  whom  he  hoped  to  impress  by
invoking  scientific  principles.  The  most  precise  rendition  might  be
something  like  “Antiquityknowledgecraft”;  I  will  retain  the  German  term
instead.54

the  word
“Altertumswissenschaft” in his eulogy on Winckelmann, in which he called
for the “whole” of the field to “take shape.” It was only gradually adopted
by  Wolf,  who  initially  referred  to  “Alte  Litteratur”  (“Old,”  i.e.,  “Ancient
Literature”)  to  describe  the  same  agenda.  This  is  not  a  trivial  detail,  as  it
highlights the text-centered character of a field that its champions liked to
present  as  comprehensively  holistic—a  feature  that  the  field,  including  its
Anglo twin “Classics,” has never been fully able (or willing) to abandon.55

Wolf penned a concise adaptation of these lectures at the encouragement
of Goethe, to whose circle Humboldt had previously introduced him and to
whom  this  “presentation”  was  dedicated.  Never  fully  translated  into
English,  Wolf’s  rationale  for  narrowly  demarcating  the  study  of  the
“ancient” world deserves to be quoted in some detail, charting as it does the
course of Greco-Roman “Ancient History.”56

the

to  determine

subject  matter

In  order
science
[“Wissenschaft”], we take a look back in time from the great migrations
of the peoples, which initiated the transformation of our part of the world
to  the  Middle  Ages—a  cleavage  as  it  were  between  older  and  newer
civilization—to  the  course  of  the  preceding  centuries.  There  we  see  in
the  most  beautiful  regions  of  the  ancient  world  a  series  of  peoples
appear,  one  after  the  other  and  side  by  side,  peoples  that  even  today
advertise  their  past  lives  and  actions  through  their  remains.  One  would
like  to  gather  all  these  peoples  into  a  single  field  of  study.  However,
many reasons make a separation necessary and do not allow us to place
Egyptians, Hebrews, Persians and other nations of the Orient next to the
Greeks and Romans. One of the most important differences between the
former and the latter nations is this: that the former advanced not at all or
only a few steps above the kind of culture [“Bildung”] which we should
call policed civility [“bürgerliche Policirung”] or civilization, in contrast
to  true  higher  intellectual  culture  [“höhere  eigentliche  Geistescultur”].
The  former  type  of culture—the  terminology  itself is  irrelevant  as long
as  we  differentiate  its  character  more  precisely  than  usual—is  keenly
preoccupied with the conditions required for a life of security, order, and
comfort.  For  that,  it  employs  certain  worthier  inventions  and  skills,
which  were,  however,  mostly  obtained  by  unscientific  means—as  in
some  cases  by  Egyptians  and  other  ancient  peoples—and  should  never
have prompted claims to majestic wisdom. It neither requires nor creates
a  literature,  that  is,  a  stock  of  writings  made  up  not  of  the  records
specific castes produced for official purposes but of the contributions to
the  enlightenment  of  his  contemporaries  made  by  any  member  of  the
nation  who  was  confident  in  his  own  insights.  The  latter—which  in  a

felicitously organized people may commence earlier than order and calm
in  its  outer  life—had  never  occurred  among  any  people  before  the
Greeks, and none of them acquired that higher culture before them, be it
intellectual or literary. We need not worry that the peoples of the Orient
are being judged below their merit: further elaboration of my statement
would  readily  dispel  any  such  suspicion.  Among  all  Orientals,  the
merely  native  beginnings  of  intellectual  formation,  which  barely
advanced to prose presentations—preserved above all in the holy books
of the Hebrews—are  of a character  strikingly  different from that of the
Greeks. Likewise, the remains of artworks from those regions—prior to
Hellenization—display a style that so deviates from European tastes that
such  remains,  were  they  joined  together  with  those  left  by  Greece  and
Rome, would add up only to a very heterogeneous whole. Thirdly, when
it  comes  to  all  those  other  peoples,  we  are  deterred  by  the  extremely
small  number  of  surviving  works,  which  permit  at  most  a  rudimentary
comprehension of their languages but not a deep understanding of their
intellectual organization and all their peculiarities. Thus, we are left with
just  two  nations  of  antiquity  the  knowledge  of  which  can  support  a
consistent science [“Wissenschaft”], namely Greeks and Romans. Asians
and  Africans,  as  literarily  uncultivated,  merely  civilized  peoples,  are
unhesitatingly  excluded  from  our  borders;  likewise  the  Arabs,  who
became  important  later,  even  though  they—just  as  the  Romans  before
them—reached a certain degree of scholarly education with the help of
the Greeks. Accordingly, literature that is fragmentary or still hidden in
book-dungeons, and even more that of the most distant peoples of Asia,
is left to Orientalists, who divide themselves into several fields. Indeed,
in the spirit of the ancients, who proudly looked down on the barbari as
ignoble species, we are permitted to restrict even the term antiquity in an
exclusionary  manner  to  the  two  peoples  that  were  refined  by  their
intellectual culture, learning and art.57

The  main  points  are  clear:  (1)  Greeks  and  Romans  were  the  only  ones
endowed  with  true  higher  culture,  which  manifested  itself  above  all  in
literature; (2) other cultures were too alien (both at the time and for modern

observers)  to  be  covered  alongside  Greece  and  Rome;  (3)  other  early
cultures were (too) poorly known.

According to early fragments of his lectures from 1785, Wolf had at first
merely  elevated  Greeks  and  Romans  without  turning  them  into  the  sole
representatives of “antiquity”; the latter position appeared in the 1790s. His
blending of normative claims (about intrinsic differences in cultural quality)
and  ostensibly  pragmatic  concerns  (about  the  imbalance  of  evidence)
reveals his eagerness to present a watertight case. What we encounter in this
passage  is  the  tripartite  scheme  of  Greco-Roman  “ancient”  (European)  vs
“oriental” vs “modern” (European). Also embraced by other philologists at
the time, it was to structure scholarly engagement with the world’s history
for many generations to come.58

A  fourth  claim—already  familiar  from  Winckelmann  and  Herder—
followed:  “It  is  possible,  and  indeed  made  likely  by  the  historical  record,
that  the  Greeks  initially  obtained  some  elementary  concepts  of  their
sciences  and  some  technical  norms  for  the  arts  from  an  already  civilized
their  earliest  political  arrangements  and
Orient.  Nevertheless,
constitutions, in their manners, in their language, in everything that makes a
people  distinctive,  they  were  by  nature  as  original  as  any  people  has  ever
been;  and  they  knew  how  to  stamp  such  foreign  borrowings  with  their
genius,  and  to  enrich  and  fertilize  them,  so  that  everything  soon  became
their own.”59

in

Moreover,  leaving  aside  the  uneven  state  of  knowledge  about  different
early  cultures,  Wolf  insisted  that  they  be  studied  separately  as  a  matter  of
principle:  “In  terms  of  their  origins,  existence,  and  fall,  the  peoples
themselves  need  to  be  studied  individually,  not  after  the  fashion  of  some
new  dramatizing  universal  histories  but  as  an  assemblage  of  peoples’
histories that are lined up next to and after one another.”60

Romantic preferences shone through. In opposing rationalist confidence
in  the  shared  features  of  all  human  beings  that  was  grounded  in
Enlightenment  notions  of  human  equality,  Romanticism  downplayed
commonalities  by  emphasizing,  essentializing,  and  celebrating  difference.
This perspective called for cultures to be understood primarily on their own
terms; Enlightenment-style systematization was passé. It  would  be hard to

exaggerate  the  degree  to  which  the  tension  between  these  two  approaches
has continued to shape our interpretation of the past.61

In the Wolfian project, exclusion of the “other” had to be balanced by its
inverse, the inclusion of every single shred of information that pertained to
the  chosen  people.  Wolf  insisted  that  while  the  “aesthetically  beautiful”
remained rare, every single artifact, no matter how lowly, was worthy of our
attention:  all  of  them  had  historical  value  and  could  contribute  to  our
understanding  of  the  culture.  This  would  make  it  a  mistake  “to  single  out
to
only
antiquarians.”62

the  classical  and  beautiful  but  surrender  everything  else

Everything that was truly “ancient” deserved to be covered: this was the
guiding principle behind Wolf’s division of the discipline into no fewer than
twenty-four  different  branches,  ranging  from  grammar  to  the  study  of  art
and  geography.  That  alone  made  a  wider  scope  beyond  Greece  and  Rome
impractical.  Mastery  of  language  performed  a  similar  function.  For  all  its
holistic  reach,  the  discipline  rested  on  philological  excellence:  as  “written
works  naturally  enjoy  the  first  rank  among  all”  ancient  remains,  “a  most
perfect understanding of the authors … counts as one of the most essential
requirements.”63

For  all  these  reasons,  all  other  cultures  needed  to  be  summarily
“excluded”  from  the  field  and  left  to  area  specialists.  Genuine  “antiquity”
was  to  be limited to  Greece and Rome,  and to  be investigated  from every
conceivable  angle:  the  goal  of  Altertumswissenschaft  “is  nothing  else  but
the understanding of ancient humankind itself, an understanding that arises
from the observation—facilitated by the study of the ancient remains—of an
organically unfolding momentous national ‘Bildung.’ ”64

This understanding, and its expansive benefits, could only be attained if
this  true  antiquity  was  kept  apart  from  everything  else:  “Our  antiquity,
conceived of as a whole, is as it were a world closed unto itself. As such, it
touches  every  observer  in  its  own  way,  and  offers  different  things  to
different  people,  so  as  to  develop  and  train  their  faculties,  to  expand  their
knowledge  with  things  worth  knowing,  to  sharpen  their  sense  of  truth,  to
refine their judgment about beauty, to rein in their imagination, to wake all

the powers of the soul with attractive tasks and methods and to mold them
in a balanced fashion.”65

to

Due

their  assorted  deficiencies,  no  other  cultures  promised
comparable  rewards.  Not  even  the  Romans  emerged  unscathed:  “For  our
study,  among  the  ancient  nations,  the  Romans  already  offer  no  desirable
material; because right from the beginning they followed some of the one-
sided trajectories that some of the most estimable peoples have adopted in
recent  centuries.  Only  in  ancient  Greece  do  we  find  what  we  look  for  in
vain  almost  everywhere  else:  peoples  and  states  which  by  virtue  of  their
nature  possessed  most  of  the  qualities  that  laid  the  foundations  for  a
character that is perfected to full humanity.”66

Indeed, Greek development served as a “culture meter” for the “course
of purely human development as such.” Thus, “of all the nations none is as
important, and I daresay holy, for the student of human history as that of the
Greeks.” This adds Wolf’s two final claims: that studying the Greeks makes
us better people, and, thanks to its tight focus, offers unrivaled insights into
human nature as such.67

The  resultant  nine-headed  hydra  laid  the  foundations  of  the  discipline:
from  others  necessitated  by
Greco-Roman  superiority;  separation
intercultural incommensurability; lack of knowledge of other early cultures;
Greek purity; national specificity; primacy of linguistic excellence coupled
with  comprehensiveness  of  coverage  within  the  approved  limits;  self-
improvement  through  immersive  study;  and  the  universal  relevance  of  the
singular.

These  premises  involved  more  than  their  fair  share  of  paradoxes.  How
could Wolf so confidently make the first two points in the face of the third?
His assertion that other early cultures were poorly documented sat uneasily
with  even more  strident judgments in  his  lectures,  such as his  verdict  that
“[t]he  Hebrews  did  not  build  themselves  up  to  a  level  of  culture  at  which
we would have to regard them as a learned cultivated people. They do not
even  have  prose,  only  partial  poetry.  Their  historians  are  just  miserable

chroniclers.  They  never  managed  to  write  meter;  that  was  a  Greek
invention.”  By  the  same  token,  “we  must  deny  that  the  Egyptians  had  a
learned culture,” or the Chinese.68

Such sweeping dismissals already troubled Wolf’s contemporaries. In the
late  eighteenth  century,  European  scholars  had  begun
to  produce
translations  of  Sanskrit  works  from  ancient  India,  such  as  the  Bhagavad
Gītā,  some  Upanishads  and  the  Laws  of  Manu,  as  well  as  a  translation  of
the  Pahlavi  version  of  parts  of  the  Zoroastrian  Avesta,  all  of  which  were
generally well received.69

The  first  translation  of  the  ancient  Indian  poet  Kālidāsa’s  play  “The
Recognition  of  Śakuntalā”  into  English  in  1789,  followed  by  a  German
translation two years later, generated particular attention. Enthusiasm about
its artfulness swept across Europe, prompting Goethe himself to rhapsodize

If you want to capture Heaven and Earth in one single name,
Then I name you, Sakontala, and everything is said.70

Yet the same Goethe raised no objections to Wolf’s position, styling him the
“progenitor of a great scientific idea” and praising without any evident sign
of irony the breadth of his vision when his exposition of 1807 came out.71

Breakthroughs in the decipherment of Egyptian hieroglyphs in the 1820s
and of Old Persian cuneiform in the 1820s and 1830s raised the pressure, as
did  further  translations  of  Sanskrit  texts.  Thus,  nine  years  after  Wolf’s
death, the editor of his collected papers felt compelled to insert a footnote
on  Wolf’s  assertion  of  other  cultures’  inferiority:  “Regarding  Wolf’s
assessment of the intellectual culture of oriental peoples, which might seem
unjust  to  some,  one  must  take  account  of  his  viewpoint,  and  one  will
consider  his  verdict—albeit  motivated  [sic]  only  by  cutting  hints—
nonetheless  thought-through  and  just.  It  is  true  that  since  Wolf  wrote  this
account, scholarly research has greatly illuminated the languages of Asian
peoples.  Even  so,  time  and  again  this  research  offers  new  evidence  that
among  them—except  perhaps  for  the  Indians—only civilization  reached  a
certain  level  whereas  true  and  widespread  intellectual  culture  did  not
prevail,  and  that  therefore  an  important  difference  existed  between  them
and the Greeks and Romans.”72

In  other  words,  further  research  was  futile  because  the  verdict  had
already  been  reached  and  could  not  be  disconfirmed,  except  perhaps  for
fellow Aryans in South Asia. Yet even the study of Sanskrit, as we will see
in a moment, needed to be kept at arm’s length in order to maintain Greco-
Roman  exclusivity.  Wolf  had  already  anticipated  this  necessity  when  he
noted that even if other cultures had produced literature, they still could not
be included in his discipline: homogeneity was a decisive criterion.73

But  not  the  only  one.  Wolf’s  reasoning  also  anticipated  the  British
historian Thomas Macaulay’s (in)famous observation in 1835: having read
translations of Sanskrit and Arabic classics and consulted with orientalists,
Macaulay had “never found … among” the latter anyone “who could deny
that a single shelf of a good European library was worth the whole native
literature of India and Arabia.”74

Returning  to  paradoxes,  Wolf’s  second  and  fifth  points  seem  to  clash
with his seventh: if all nations were different and incommensurable and the
study  of Greece and Rome  conceived of “a world closed  into itself,”  how
could  it  generate  universally  valid  insights  about  humanity?  Wolf  “had  to
reconcile  a  radical  claim  to  uniqueness  with  an  equally  radical  claim  to
universality.”  A  paradox  that  lay  at  the  heart  of  the  Romantic  project  had
now become enshrined in a rising academic discipline.75

Lesser  paradoxes  pale  beside  this,  such  as  Wolf’s  insistence  on  the
distinctiveness and therefore separate study of individual nations and one of
his  student’s takeaways  that “[w]ith respect to  Bildung, the  Romans  come
closest to the Greeks. They merge into a single whole.” No longer are only
their literatures joined together, as Humboldt would have it; so are the two
peoples as such. This special dispensation from the mandate to essentialize
discrete  “nations”  finally  allowed  the  subject  of  the  new  discipline  to  be
constituted  as  what  Joy  Connolly  calls  “GreeceandRome.”  There  was  not
(yet)  a  need  to  call  this  construct  “classical”:  as  long  as  GreeceandRome
represented  the  only  genuine  antiquity,  no  qualifying  adjectives  were
required.76

That  narrowing  and  fusing  went  hand  in  hand  with  exclusion  of  the
Hebrew tradition, downgraded to that of an uncultured people, and even of
the  New  Testament.  Wolf  at  one  point  deemed  the  latter  to  have  resulted

from Jewish recognition and embrace of Greek superiority that resulted in a
text of “Greek morals, mixed with Jewish ideas,” composed in Greek that
was “revolting” and “barbaric …, full of Hebraisms and Judaizing turns of
phrase.”77

While Wolf’s banishment of early Judaism from his new field arguably
reflected  his  desire  to  rid  the  study  of  antiquity  from  the  influence  of
theology, such rhetoric went far beyond what might have been required to
achieve this goal. Endowed with a well-known language and literature, the
ancient Hebrews more than any other culture had to be cut down to size in
order  to  justify  the  separatist  elevation  of  GreeceandRome.  There  is  some
bitter irony in the fact that all of this took place at the University of Halle,
where  August  Hermann  Francke  in  the  opening  years  of  the  eighteenth
century had established not only a conventional pedagogical seminar for the
teaching  of  Greek  and  Latin  but  also  a  high-profile  collegium  orientale
theologicum  for  the  study  of  biblical  and  rabbinical  Hebrew,  Aramaic,
Samaritan, Syriac, Arabic, and Ethiopian.78

For  Wolf  himself,  the  hardening  of  the  GreeceandRome  template
required rejection of comparative questions—an implicit repudiation of his
own  earlier  comparative  work  on  Greek  and  biblical  philology  for  his
masterpiece Prolegomena ad Homerum of 1795. Disciplinization demanded
self-disciplining.79

The  task  at  hand  was  to  “elevate”  comprehensive  engagement  with
Greece  and  Rome—the  commitment  “to  get  to  know  it  in  all  possible
respects”—“to  the  eminence  of  a  well-ordered  philosophical-historical
science.” For that, “[m]ethod is key. If it is correctly established, there is no
field  in  which,  as  in  ours,  all  powers  of  the  soul  are  cultivated  in  a  more
balanced manner, and many elements of our field engage all these powers
of the soul conjointly.”80

However the discipline’s many branches were to be configured, language
skills were always central. Wolf made sure to impress this on his students,
not  just  future  scholars  in  the  seminar  but  also  his  more  diverse  lecture
audience: “We can never fully get to know the true state of a nation without
reading its authors in the original. For language represents a key element of

the state of a nation and belongs to the whole, which the study of antiquity
searches for.”81

Excellent  language  skills  were  essential  not  merely  to  access  ancient
sources but because of the intrinsic equivalence of language and nation. As
Humboldt  later  proclaimed  in  his  final  opus  magnum,  summing  up  long-
held  convictions,  “language  is  as  it  were  the  external  manifestation  of  the
spirit  of  peoples;  their  language  is  their  spirit  and  their  spirit  is  their
language; it is impossible to exaggerate the degree of identity between the
two.”82

This  mattered  because  Altertumswissenschaft  defined  itself  “not  by
appropriating a particular method, but … in terms of its historical object.”
Idealization  of  the  subject  matter  was  necessary  to  allow  philological
endeavor to progress from the production of knowledge to the practitioners’
personal  improvement.  In  the  end,  the  field  itself  was  legitimated  less  by
skill or process than by the special properties of the true ancients.83

The presumed naïve unities of the Greeks (or GreeksandRomans) required a
very  peculiar  approach.  Joseph  Farrell,  addressing  a  convention  of
professional  classical  scholars  in  2019,  captured  it  well.  In  the  Romantic
vision,  “every  aspect  of  ancient  Greek  and  Roman  civilization  …  was
related  to  every  other  aspect  in  a  way  that  they  thought  simply  untrue  of
other historical periods, and that could never be true of our own, radically
heterogeneous  modern  world.…  This  unified,  highly  coherent  period,  of
which  every  morsel  apparently  stands  in  something  like  a  fractal  or
Anaxagorean  relationship  to  the  whole,  is  the  twin  of  Schiller’s  simple,
organically  unified,  objective  world  of  naïve  literature,  and  is  everything
that  his  complex,  disunified,  subjectively  distorted  modern  world  is  not.
What that means is that the founders of our discipline also constructed our
object of study as something that is the opposite of the modern world, and
as  something  that  possessed  characteristics  different  from  those  that  any
other period of human existence has ever known.”84

James Porter gets to the bottom of the underlying yearning: “Ideals have
a  way  of  seducing  us  with  their  very  non-locatability.  This  is  their  chief
source of power, which is purely ideological: they present the requirement,

and illusion, of consensus.… Classicism’s construction of the classical ideal
is shaped in response to the very impossibility of discovering it in the works
that are beheld.… Belief in the classical ideal can be self-willed, and it can
even be denied in the way that it commonly is in romantic discourse … as
long  as  we  continue  to  act  as  if  we  believe  in  the  ideal,  in  the  face  of  its
sheer impossibility.”85

This  particular  “metaphysical castle  in  the  air”—GreeceandRome—had
been  built  to  last;  and  last  it  did.  Wolf’s  exposition  proved  to  be
paradigmatic, not so much because of the published version of 1807 (in an
obscure  and  short-lived  journal  he  had  founded  for  the  occasion)  but
because of the reach and success of his lectures, which had already spawned
epigonal publications by members of the audience.86

Yet  even  the  lectures,  popular  as  they  were,  were  just  opium  for  the
masses.  It  was  Wolf’s  seminar  that  created  future  professionals,  and  was
thus the main source of his impact. Its members were an elite group, as only
the  most  gifted  were  able  to  join  (and  often  received  stipends).  Students
were  barred  from  belonging  to  more  than  one  seminar,  a  restriction  that
encouraged  specialization  and  discipline  formation.  According  to  one
contemporary  observer,  seminarists  signaled  their  commitment  not  merely
by  “slavish  studying”  but  by  adopting  a  dress  code  so  distinctive  that  it
made  them  recognizable  from  far  away.  Those  were  the  shock  troops
charged with conquering the true antiquity.87

The Prussian government had approved the establishment of the seminar
on the understanding that a good teacher would benefit from research skills.
To  this  end,  the  curriculum  was  supposed  to  cover  “interpretation”  (i.e.,
lexicography,  grammar,  and  textual  criticism)  and  “disputation”  (based  on
written  homework  on  topics  often  bereft  of  relevance  for  later  teaching
careers) as well  as “pedagogy.” Wolf,  however,  prioritized the  first  two to
the  extent  that  the  third  was  even  omitted  from  his  own  outline  of  the
seminar rules. Producing research papers was to be a central element of the
philologist’s training. Foregrounding research at the expense of pedagogical
preparation while upholding the strict disciplinary boundaries that Wolf had
set  out  in  his  exposition,  this  template  was  adopted  across  the  philology

seminars  of  the  nineteenth  century.  What  is  more,  this  seminar  praxis
helped shape the modus operandi of the entire university.88
