What  accounts  for  the  success  of  this  type  of  seminar  and  the  rigorous
approach to the study of Greece and Rome that it promoted? The reasons lie
in  a  mixture  of  zeitgeist—others  held  similar  views;  replication—via
academic  genealogies;  and  political  power—which  allowed  a  particular
vision to capture the relevant institutions. I briefly touch on all three in turn.
Friedrich Ast, founder of the philological seminar in Landshut in Bavaria
the  nineteenth  century,  provides  a  striking
the  beginning  of
at
contemporaneous
to  Wolf’s.
Comprehensiveness was a key element of Ast’s program: “Philology is the
study  of  the  classical  world  in  its  complete  artistic,  scientific,  public  and
peculiar existence. The center of this study is the spirit of antiquity, which is
reflected  most  purely  in  the  works  of  ancient  authors,  but  is  likewise
reflected in the outer and peculiar life of the classical peoples.… Only the
harmonious  fusion  of  all  elements  of  Alterthumswissenschaft  can  bring
forth  true  philology,  because  only  the  comprehensive  appreciation  and
understanding of antiquity reveals the spirit of the classical world.”89

example  of

a  vision

analogous

The  motifs  of  superiority  and  universal  relevance  likewise  took  center
stage: “Everything Greek-made is therefore imbued with the characteristics
of  highest  and  purest  perfection  and—as  though  it  had  been  shaped  by
eternal,  immutable  nature  itself—manifests  itself  in  the  most  admirable
quality,  in  the  most  independent  beauty.”  “[I]n  its  autonomous  and  freely
developed, harmonious and universal existence, antiquity serves as the ideal
of  the  education  of  humankind.…  [W]e  consider  the  development  of  the
classical world as the model of human development in general.”90

The motif of improvement completed the familiar trio: “The philosophy
or the idealism of our own time can only be perfected by the poetry or the
realism  of  antiquity;  and  in  their  mutual  interpenetration  it  will  become
clear  that  both,  just  like  mind  and  body,  unity  and  plurality,  are  originally
one.… The beauty that will spring from the harmony of the ancient and the

modern world will be the unity of inner and outer humanity, the revelation
of a life that unfolds from the innermost depth and freedom of the spirit.”
Conversely, modern “creativity would gradually perish amid mere research
unless reawakened and nourished by the exhilarating beauty of Greek art.”91
In  short,  the  philologists  who  were  busy  populating  the  academic
landscape  with  new  seminars  were  setting  up  a  broad  front.  As  for  the
second factor, replication, Wolfian disciplinary framing persisted even as it
was updated, most notably by his star student August Böckh, who went on
to teach generations of students at the increasingly prominent University of
Berlin. Just like his mentor, Böckh insisted on organicist holism and thus on
the  inclusion  of  historical  context,  a  position  he  defended  against
proponents  of  a  narrower  focus  on  language  and  literature.  On  the  whole,
the  holistic  approach  prevailed,  even  as  deep  language  skills  continued  to
be  considered  the  “main  key”  for  unlocking  the  treasure  troves  of
antiquity.92

Böckh  delivered  his  influential  lectures  on  the  “encyclopedia  of
philology”—the same title Wolf had used—no fewer than twenty-six times
between 1809 and 1865. He began by expounding the “concept, scope, and
highest  purpose  of  philology.”  Albeit  universal  in  nature—as  “a  renewed
recognition  and  description  of  all  existing  human  knowledge”—philology
must  de  facto  be  organized  in  smaller  segments:  in  keeping  with  Wolfian
doctrine, Böckh preferred separation by time and space—ancient, modern,
East/West,  Greek,  Roman,  Hebrew,  Indian—over  separation  by  subject,
such as language or literature.93

The  chosen  mode  of  demarcation  meshed  well  with  the  nationalist
template, making it possible to survey the totality of a people by bundling
together all strands of development and approaching them from the center,
which  was  embodied  by  that  people’s  spirit:  for  “it  is  imperative  that  we
find something common, within which all particulars are contained”; that is,
“the principle of a people or era …, the innermost core of its overall being;
it  cannot  be  anything  else;  for  anything  else  would  be  alien,  introduced
from the outside.”94

Although  the  philology  of Greece and Rome  was  in  principle one only
among  many,  Böckh  deemed  it  special  after  all.  The  completeness  of

antiquity—finished,  distant,  and  foreign—turned  it  into  a  natural  unit  of
engagement,  worthy  of  our  attention  due  to  its  magnificence  and  impact:
“the philology of classical antiquity is a natural section because the classical
is  eminently  worth  knowing  and  the  culture  of  the  Greeks  and  Romans  is
the foundation of our entire Bildung.”95

This,  according  to  Böckh,  justified  elevating  the  study  of  Greece  and
Rome  into  a  kind  of  paradigm  of  philology  as  such:  “As  we  explicitly
recognize  the  other  branches  of  philology  as  equals,  we  accept  the
limitation  to  classical  antiquity,  which  is  pragmatically  justified  yet
coincidental  as  such,  while  being  aware  that  it  is  a  limitation.  Within  that
limitation [viz., to classical antiquity], however, we embrace the unlimited
concept  [viz.,  of  philology],  which  alone  determines  method  and
construction.”  Thus,  in  an  elegant  squaring  of  the  circle,  Greco-Roman
philology was one of many philologies yet superior to them. This was as it
had to be: after all, understanding Greece and Rome “is no base matter: for
it  is  the  understanding  of  the  noblest  that  the  human  spirit  has  produced
over  thousands  of  years  …;  one  learns  about  the  entire  process  of  human
understanding and of human conditions in an area that is bereft of passion
because it lies deep in the past and therefore permits impartial judgment.”96
Böckh  expected  scholars  to  devote  their  efforts  to  demonstrating
connections within the Greco-Roman world—that is, to empirically validate
the  organicist  premise—and  to  strive  for  precision  even  in  the  smallest
matters—“for in philology one must to some extent be pedantic.” Rigorous
and immersive engagement cleansed the mind: “Ancient ideas must and can
be  vividly  related  to  modern  thought  and  have  a  purifying  effect  on  the
latter.” This “purifying effect” issued from superiority: “the poetic works of
antiquity  are  still  of  higher  quality  than  any  others  …;  there  is  still  no
higher spirit than that which pervaded antiquity.” By conveying “noble and
majestic matters,” antiquity supplied “truly human, impartial, intellectual …
views and makes people free and liberal.”97

Except  for  the  explicit  downgrading  of  other  cultures,  all  the  principal
elements  of  the  Wolfian  agenda  were  thus  faithfully  preserved  and
energetically  disseminated  for  another  sixty  years:  the  presumption  of
organic  national unity, which called  for  scholarship  to  recover  it;  exacting

attention  to  detail;  and  the  superior  quality  of  that  specific  subject  that
promised personal improvement.98

The customary claim to purity received attention in Böckh’s address to a
convention of German philologists, schoolteachers, and orientalists (in that
presumably  hierarchical  sequence)  in  1850.  Conceding  certain  “points  of
contact” between “oriental and classical peoples” and even musing that “a
comparative cultural history of all of antiquity might eventually turn out to
be  the  principal  objective  of  philological  science,”  he  reminded  his
audience  that  “confessors  of  classical  philology”—note  the  religious
phrasing—dealt with a people “in which a purely national culture had fully
formed  that  was  as  independent  from  foreign  influences  as  possible,  and
that as swiftly as possible transformed all foreign elements it had absorbed
into its own being and its own flesh and blood.”99

In

the  end,

there  was  no  meaningful  bridge

to  be  built:
compartmentalization  ruled  supreme, and hierarchies among  cultures  were
not to be doubted. The averred primacy of Greece and Rome did not even
allow for genuine historicism. It thus hardly mattered that Böckh sought to
replace his “master” Wolf’s proposed formal organization of the field with
his  own  scheme.  The  “science”  of  an  essentialized,  nationalized  Greco-
Roman  antiquity  was  confirmed  as  the  basic  (and  pivotal)  unit  of  study,
blocking any hope of cross-cultural history.100

Manufacturing an Academic-Pedagogical Complex

Before  we  move  on  to  the  third  pillar  of  success—political  power—let  us
very briefly take stock of the new rules of the game. The complete toolbox
was now in place. Greeks were on top, especially Athenians, and at any rate
Greeks before the age of Alexander the Great, when they were still classical
and  pure.  Wolf,  in  Gibbonian  fashion,  had  laid  claim  to  the  Byzantine
period  as  well—and  rightly  so,  given  that  its  fusion  of  Greek  and  Roman
ought to have been every classicist’s wet dream. Yet that was not to be, just
as the logic of the purist-nationalist model (coupled more specifically with
apprehension about Greeks mingling with “Orientals”) militated against full
inclusion of the yet-to-be conceptualized “Hellenistic” period.101

Under such constraints, other cultures, even neighboring ones, were out;
so were Christian Greeks and Romans. While imitation of the ancients was
passé,  meticulous  study  had  become  de  rigueur:  as  “scientific”  and
comprehensive  as  possible,  it  had  to  be  both  specialized  and  holistic  at
once,  which  inevitably  turned  out  to  be  impossible.  It  had  to  prioritize
scholarship  at  the  expense  of  pedagogy  yet  fill  teaching  positions  in
secondary  schools  with  its  products,  with  predictable  consequences.  No
matter:  this  study  was  not  only  valued  in  and  of  itself  but  also  meant  to
improve the student.

Last  but  by  no  means  least,  this  model  had  to  generate  funding,
employment, and status for the elect. Thus, while its success “rest[ed] on a
philosophically  based  reconstruction  of  the  mind  of  the  classical  world  in
the  totality  of  its  historical  manifestations,”  it  also  needed  to  be
“institutionally anchored” in order to thrive.102

How  was  all  that  to  be  sustained?  Let  us  return  to  Humboldt.  More
charitable than his friend Goethe, he envisioned much more widely diffused
rewards: “In order to yield the described benefit to the utmost, the study of
antiquity  requires  the  greatest,  broadest,  and  most  meticulous  erudition,
which  of  course  only  very  few  can  command.  Even  so,  that  benefit  is
always present, albeit to a lesser extent, wherever one engages in this study
at  all,  even  if  it  is  with  less  striving  for  thoroughness.  It  even  reaches  all
those to whom this study remains completely alien. For in the context of a
highly civilized society an individual’s knowledge can reasonably be called
a possession of all.”103

An  ingenious  twist  on  Goethe’s  more  elitist  stance,  this  became  the
fulcrum  of  the  Humboldtian  project  of  educational  reform  centered  on
Greece  and  Rome:  even  if  only  the  most  erudite  could  reap  full  benefits,
everybody stood to gain, including those who did not participate at all. The
vision  is  one  of  a  civilizing  mission  whereby  those  at  the  top—university
philologists—train  teachers  who  in  turn  spread  the  word  in  ways  that
ultimately  raise  up  society  as  a  whole.  Those  who  know  best  must  not  be
questioned  because  everyone  somehow  wins.  Presented  as  axiomatic,  this
premise was of vital importance in securing financial support.

This  is  where  power  comes  in.  Wolf’s  increasingly  exalted  status
generated  soft  power.  Humboldt  had  introduced  him  to  Goethe’s  circle  in
1795,  and  before  long  his  fame  reached  the  top  echelons  of  the
establishment: according to Humboldt, a Prussian minister of justice and a
personal  acquaintance  of  Wolf’s  “had  openly  said  several  times  that  one
would  like  to  kneel  down  before  and  venerate”  Wolf’s  exposition  of
1807.104

But  the  hard  work  was  done  at  the  levers  of  administrative  power.  In
1809,  Humboldt  was  appointed  as  head  of  the  section  of  culture  and
education  in  the  Prussian  ministry  of  the  interior,  alongside  two  state
councilors: the Schiller and Wolf student and former philology professor in
Königsberg Johann Süvern (later replaced by yet another former student of
Wolf’s),  and  Georg  Nicolovius,  a  friend  of  Kant’s  who  had  married
Goethe’s  niece.  Other  close  associates,  such  as  Wilhelm  Uhden  and
Johannes Schulze, had likewise studied with Heyne or Wolf.105

Such  well-placed  like-minded  insiders  played  an  essential  role  in
steering resources toward the study of Greece and Rome. Their mission was
driven  by  a  sense  of  urgency:  sweeping  educational  reforms  sought  to
counter the threat posed by France, its former cultural hegemony made ever
more unpalatable by Napoleon’s relentless campaigns. The focus on Greece
and  Rome  was  intended  to  help  fashion  a  collective  identity  that  did  not
depend  on  political  unity—absent  from  Germany  at  the  time—but
championed  cultural  unity  modeled  on  that  ascribed  to  the  ancient
Greeks.106

This program satisfied bourgeois desire for education and perfectability
in a sufficiently elitist format. Those without means were largely excluded
from  this  project,  which  balanced  notions  of  liberating  cultivation  of  the
self  with  a  timocratic  conservatism  that  in  turn  made  it  appealing  to  state
authorities  wary  of  revolution.  “Bildung”  involved  submission  to  models,
and  in  this  case  opinion  leaders  had  long  been  championing  a  particular
one. Everything fell into place. As Suzanne Marchand summarizes it, “The
aristocratic,  secular,  aestheticized  wisdom  of  the  Greeks  became  the  basis
for Bildung;  the  knowledge  of  Latin  and  Greek  was  understood  to  be  the
sine qua non of the educated man.”107

As a result, money flowed freely. The field had come a long way from
Heyne’s  advising  young  Wolf  against  studying  philology  instead  of
theology because there were no jobs and even those few who obtained them
were  consigned  to  lives  of  poverty.  In  the  early  nineteenth  century,  in  a
marked  shift  away  from  earlier  traditions,  academic  positions  in  the
humanities took the lead in both prestige and pay, and among them Greco-
Roman  philology  was  the  envy  of  the  others.  Following  Göttingen’s
precocious lead in 1738, no fewer than twenty-two philology seminars were
established  between  1777  and  1838.  They  became  the  seeds  of  the
academic  institute  system,  shaping  practice  especially  for  departments
devoted to history and modern philology.108

When  the  University  of  Berlin,  the  epitome  of  Prussian  academic
ambition, opened in 1810, it was dominated by speculative philosophy and
neohumanistic  philology,  and  hosted  a  well-funded  philology  seminar  that
became  Böckh’s  exceptionally
influential  platform.  Meanwhile,  his
colleague  Georg  Wilhelm  Friedrich  Hegel,  one  of  the  century’s  most
prominent  philosophers,  in  his  lectures  on  the  history  of  philosophy  not
only  turned  Greeks  into  the  first  people  with  a  consciousness  of  freedom
but  endorsed  the  tropes  of  Greek-German  affinity  and  the  Greeks’  purity
achieved  by  their  having  “excised,  transformed,  worked  over,  inverted”
alien—Asian, Syrian, and Egyptian—influences to such an extent that they
were  “turned  into  something  else,”  which  is  what  we  now  “value,
recognize, love.”109

committee

As  top  intellectuals  presented  a  united  front,  it  mattered  little  that  the
founding figures soon fell by the wayside. Wolf, furnished with a chair in
Berlin at twice the going salary, repaid the favor by occasionally lecturing
in  Latin  to  deter  students,  falling  out  with  most  of  his  colleagues  and
refusing
assignments.  Humboldt,  meanwhile,  quickly
relinquished  his  ministerial  assignment  and  escaped  into  comparative
linguistics  by  studying  Basque,  Native  American,  Egyptian,  and  finally
Javanese languages, even as he kept insisting on the superiority of Greek.110
Once fired up, the philology train chugged on. Some 1,600 men attended
Böckh’s seminar alone, alongside the untold thousands who passed through
his encyclopedia lectures for more than half a century. As Berlin’s star kept

rising  and  other  universities  took  the  same  line,  Altertumswissenschaft
triumphed. With time, the model spread as academics in other countries also
took note.111

Nevertheless,  cracks  appeared  from  the  very  beginning.  Even  as  Böckh’s
generously supported seminar attracted growing numbers of students, depth
trumped  breadth.  In  1816,  Böckh  unsuccessfully  proposed  to  make  the
principal philology lectures mandatory—an easy fix for the lack of student
take-up  of  unloved  humanities  offerings  that  has  kept  resurfacing  to  the
present  day.  Other  institutions  faced  similar  problems.  Berlin’s  academic
leadership  even  felt  compelled  to  design  a  recommendation  for  incoming
students that promoted the study of antiquity above all other subjects.112

Considerable  tension  existed  between  university-oriented  training  that
favored early specialization and emphasis on research and was embraced by
students who modeled their preferences on those of their professors and the
preparation—or rather the lack thereof—for secondary school teaching that
awaited  many  of  the  graduates.  Moreover,  future  teachers  had  to  be
thoroughly professionalized as budding philology experts in order to satisfy
continuing  research  requirements.  Teachers  of  Greek  and  Latin  were
expected, and for a long time even obliged, to publish: over the course of a
century, an estimated 25,000 of their treatises filled the annual reports put
out by gymnasiums.113

The  mismatch  between  what  seminars  did  and  expected  and  what
schools needed was amplified by the former’s turn away from occupational
realities and toward study for the sake of study, which constituted a striking
inversion  of  earlier,  eighteenth-century  norms  and  practices.  Even  as
seminar  statutes  officially  recognized  the  need  to  train  generalists  and
teachers,  pedagogical  preparation  was  generally  sidelined  in  favor  of
relying  on
for
progressive pedagogy faded as philologists took a lead in denigrating such
efforts.114

learning  by  doing.  Eighteenth-century  enthusiasm

More specifically, the upgrading of Greece relative to Rome went hand
in  hand  with  the  repudiation  of  instruction  based  on  Latin-centered
formalism  in  favor  of  appreciation  of  the  essence  of  the  spirit  of  the

idealized Greeks—and grasping that essence called for specialized research.
This led to yet another paradox, given that the holistic philology movement,
via  its  Romantic  roots,  was  also  inspired  by  antimodernist  sentiments  that
disapproved  of  narrow  specialization.  The  hope  that  specialism  would
somehow  transcend  itself  unsurprisingly  failed  to  come  true.  Instead,
increasingly  compartmentalized  research  took  over  while  idealization
became a smoke screen to impress funding bodies and sweeten the pill for
parents and students asked to accept philological instruction as the primary
gatekeeping device for university access.115

The  goal  was  to  reconcile  aesthetic  ideals  with  bureaucratic  structures
that  controlled  public-sector  employment  and  the  professional  standing  of
teachers and the teachers’ teachers, while giving that last group ample room
to focus on personal research interests. As a consequence, the cleansing of
the  modern  mind  that  had  initially  been  expected  to  spring  from  intimate
communion  with  the  most  excellent  subject  matter  was  increasingly
supposed to be brought about by a rigorous academic regimen of technical
research.116

The  end  product  was  what  one  might  call  an  academic-pedagogical
complex.  Research-oriented  seminars  graduated  well-disciplined  and
research-oriented classicists who could make a living teaching in secondary
schools  that  produced  recruits  for  the  next  generation  of  well-disciplined
and  research-oriented  classicists.  The  challenge  in  all  of  this  was  to
maintain  the  perception—dare  one  say,  the  illusion—that  this  system
operated in order to improve and strengthen the nation rather than to satisfy
the  professional  aspirations  of  academics  whose  work  was  increasingly
detached  from  real-world  concerns.  What  is  remarkable  is  how  long  this
worked.

The  initial  aspirations  were  clear:  to  reform  the  school  systems  in  the
context of the Napoleonic Wars in ways that awakened minds and nurtured
capabilities, which was to be achieved in no small measure by mobilizing

engagement with an idealized ancient Greece as an optimal instrument for
perfecting modern society.

Selling this idea at the secondary education level called for a further step
up  from  the  rhapsodizing  we  encounter  in  lectures  and  seminar  programs
aimed  at  academic  audiences.  Friedrich  Jacobs’s  inaugural  lecture  at  the
lyceum  in  Munich  in  1807  is  a  classic  example.  A  former  student  of
Heyne’s, he faithfully laid out the key points of the emerging doctrine. His
audience  was  treated  to  separation  born  of  superiority—“antiquity  in  its
most  dignified  form  as  a  closed  world  of  the  noblest  and  most  beautiful
which the human spirit has created under the most favorable circumstances
and with youthful strength and manly rigorousness, as a world of nature and
art,  in  which  everything  that  can  raise,  cleanse,  and  fertilize  the  human
spirit reveals itself in the most varied and perfected characters.”

It  also  got  a dose of fetishization  of ancient  languages “as a wonderful
and  almost  sacred  work  of  nature  and  art,”  and  of  Greek’s  purity  as  “a
language  which  …  formed  itself—by  its  own  power  and  autonomously—
into  an  admirable  plant;  …  for  hundreds  of  years  and  under  all  kinds  of
circumstances,  always  free  from  foreign  influences.”  Consequently,  the
mastery  of  those  wondrous  tongues  counts  as  one  of  “the  principal
conditions for the consecration that unlocks the inner sanctum of the ancient
world.”  Likewise,  listeners  were  instructed  about  organicism  and  self-
improvement: language skills were not enough, for knowledge of all aspects
of  ancient  life  was  equally  essential  to  create  a  field  of  study  where  “the
individual elements are arranged into an inspired whole, which—worthy by
themselves of deep contemplation—in their conjuncture reveal a perfection
of humanity that had not appeared before and has not appeared since.”117

Was it truly worth it, though, Jacobs asked—“why lead the youth on an
arduous and thorny march to the dark land of an extinct people, and torture
them  for  years  with  study  of  an  extinct  language?”  That,  he  readily
conceded,  might  not  have  made  much  sense  in  the  past,  during  the  dark
days before the advent of Altertumswissenschaft when language study was
an end in itself; but those errors had since been remedied:118

With  renewed  love  we  returned  to  antiquity.  The  holy  fire  had  not  yet
been extinguished under the overturned altars; the columns of its temples
still  stood  fast.  From  everywhere  priests  and  disciples  streamed  toward
them.  Antiquity’s  magnificence  was  worshipped  with  greater  passion
than  ever  before,  each  of  its  remnants  was  brought  to  light,  its  depths
were  explored  and  illuminated.  Just  as  in  the  sciences  in  general,  here
also  we  observe  the  most  gratifying  efforts,  and  under  the  pressure  of
hostile  conditions  the  strength  of  the  German  people  in  its  striving  for
the noblest goals has gloriously prevailed. The great events of our time
have brought us closer to antiquity; its authors are more avidly read and
better  understood;  and  there  is  hardly  any  part  of  the  wide  field  of  the
classical world that has not been illuminated by new and consequential
research. More than ever are our minds enthralled by their greatness and
dignity;  more  than  ever  do  we  recognize  the  childlike  meaning  of  its
works;  more  than  ever  do  we  feel  the  noble  simplicity,  which  is
imprinted on their entire existence. Already we can see in the products of
our own time the impact of such a glorious passion. What is base retreats
further and further; what is noble takes its place, and everything that can
raise  up  receptive  minds  is  being  cultivated  with  excellent  love  and
felicitous success. The genius of culture and the muse of science advance
rapidly; their domain grows by the day, and their boundaries expand. No
pause, no standing still are allowed.119

In  other  words,  just  as  armies  the  size  of  which  had  never  been  seen
before  ransacked  Europe,  a  new  Golden  Age,  inspired  by  proper
understanding  of  the  glories  of  Greece  and  Rome,  had  commenced.
Whatever  contemporaries  might  have  made  of  this  idea,  war  certainly
concentrated minds and made it easier to tighten reins. From 1812, Prussia
imposed  final  school  exams  loaded  with  Greek  and  Latin  as  a  conduit  to
university,  and  over  time  any  remaining  loopholes  were  closed  all  over
Germany.  It  became  effectively  impossible  to  work  as  a  civil  servant,
lawyer, or physician without first submitting to this routine. An implacable
sorting mechanism had been born.

Gatekeeping  was  made  particularly  oppressive  by  the  sheer  heft  of
expectations. The Prussian curricular recommendations from 1812 assigned
40  percent  of  all  lesson  hours  at  the  secondary  level  to  Greek  and  Latin.
(For good measure, French was eliminated as mandatory subject: out with
the archenemy, in with the ancients.) They discouraged separate lessons on
literature or “realia” lest pupils turn to compendiums instead of deriving all
necessary  information  directly  from  ancient  texts.  Expectations  were
extravagant—error-free  Latin  composition;  discussion  of  class  material  in
spoken  Latin;  translation  from  German  into  ancient  Greek  with  Latin
commentary.120

Wolf, who refused to head the academic deputation for this process, was
sardonic in his comments on these proposals, which left “an indescribable
impression on anyone who knows the world the way it is.” In his view, the
curriculum  prescribed  way  too  many  ancient  authors,  writing  assignments
were  excessive,  and  expectations  unrealistic—“Spoken  Latin  too?  At  the
most famous universities not even three scholars can do that, and often not
the  Professor  of  Eloquence,  and  barely  6  of  every  100
even
schoolteachers!”121

No matter: mission creep proved unstoppable. Like a cancer, lofty norms
designed for small elite seminars spread into the wider world of secondary
education. Critically, schoolmen at prominent places, who were themselves
among  the  most  successful  products  of  those  seminars,  keenly  embraced
this  implausible  agenda.  Inevitably  defeated  by  the  mismatch  between
expectations and feasibility, instruction turned into drilling grammar instead
of  self-improvement,  serving  credentialing  instead  of  transformative
engagement.  As  Friedrich  Nietzsche,  Germany’s  most  famous  philology
dropout, satirized the situation, the philologist “teaches cramming.”122

This experience inevitably raised doubts about how well this curriculum
prepared the young for life and work. While this routine became ever more
irrelevant  to  a  changing  society,  classicists  had  little  to  offer  in  response
beyond  doubling  down  on  the  article  of  faith  that  close  encounters  with
ancient texts were of the utmost value. Historians of scholarship have yet to
just  how  much
address
opportunistic cynicism was involved in defenses of the model: the glaring

if  unappealing  question  of

important

the

gap  between  heady  promises  of  improvement  and  actual  work  practices
both at universities and in secondary schools suggests this is more than just
idle conjecture.123

The

triggered  criticism  about

initial  school  reforms  soon

the
disproportionate weight of ancient language instruction. Greek, which was
never  used  again  by  most  graduates,  was  the  most  popular  target:  in  that
respect,
to  be  particularly
counterproductive.  At  the  same  time,  the  seminars  complained  about  the
inadequate  language  skills  of  incoming  students.  The  guardians  of  the
complex  did  their  best  to  hold  the  line:  in  the  1840s  classical  instruction
even gained further ground in the Prussian school system.124

the  Hellenophilic

turned  out

ideal

All in all, it took half a century for the tide finally to turn. Between 1867
and  1900,  universities  began  to  drop  the  requirement  that  doctoral
dissertations  be  written  in  Latin;  consecutive  curriculum  reforms  reduced
the role of Greek and Latin, and Greek was partly replaced by English; the
ability to write Latin was abandoned as a learning goal in gymnasiums; and
neohumanistic  and  science-oriented  gymnasiums  were  granted  equal
status.125

Right  from  the  start,  just  as  Wolf  had  predicted,  high  expectations  of
teaching  standards  and
learning  outcomes  had  never  been  met.
Nevertheless,  the  whole  system  lumbered  on  for  generations.  And  for  the
top  echelon  of  the  purveyors  of  epiphanous  philology,  the  academic-
pedagogical complex certainly delivered the goods on an astonishing scale.
At  German  universities  in  1910,  almost  three  out  of  every  ten  full
professors in what we now call the humanities concerned themselves with
some  aspect  of  the  Greco-Roman  world.  Even  more  strikingly,  the  same
was  true  of  one  out  of  every  twelve  full  professors  across  all  academic
fields.  One  hundred  two  classicists  held  their  own  against  155  full
professors  of  law,  180  in  theology,  and  245  in  medicine,  and  easily
outnumbered  75  social  scientists  at  that  rank.  There  were  more  classical
philology  chairs  than  for  German  and  all  Romance  languages  taken
together,  more  for  classical  archaeology  than  for  art  history,  and  more  for
ancient  history  than  for  modern  history.  Even  though  this  dominance  was

somewhat less pronounced among more junior ranks, that by itself merely
underscored the extent of privilege.126

Germany was not alone. As secondary education expanded all over Western
Europe,  Greek  and  Latin  claimed  a  large  slice  of  the  pie  everywhere.  In
England, where university scholars of Greece and Rome were disinclined to
adopt  the  holistic  German  template  (see  below)  and  the  secondary  school
system  was  not  subject  to  Prussian-style  regimentation,  the  upper  tiers  of
the education sector nonetheless embraced the study of Greek and Latin as
“a central resource for the self-recognition and social closure practiced by
an assimilated noble-bourgeois élite  which dominated a market  of cultural
goods relatively unhindered by state intervention.”127

Just as academics and other intellectuals shifted their favor from Rome
to  Greece,  ancient  Greek  became  an  additional  hoop  to  jump  through  as
ruling  class  and  bourgeoisie  alike  pursued  elitist  forms  of  education  as  a
marker  of  distinction  and  social  exclusion.  “The  learning  of  Latin  and
Greek  by  peer-groups  of  boys  in  boarding  schools  led  to  a  shared
knowledge of classics which underpinned the self-images and solidarity of
educated  adults.”  Greco-Roman  studies  served  in  the  first  instance  as  a
marker and vehicle of privilege and status, even as members of the working
classes of Britain and Ireland sought to engage with the same subject.128

Long-term  outcomes  resembled  those  in  Germany:  Classics-heavy
grammar school curricula came under fire from the mid-nineteenth century
onward; at Oxbridge Greek was abolished as a university entry requirement
after the First World War and Latin by 1960.129

Building  on  its  elite’s  strong  appreciation  of  classical  antiquity  during
the  eighteenth  century,  the  United  States  was  likewise  receptive  to
philhellenism.  The  craze  for  matters  Greek  and  Roman  peaked  from  the
1810s to the Civil War, reflected not just in language study but also by the
proliferation  of  neoclassicist  architecture.  Much  as  in  Britain,  familiarity
with the ancient classics was considered the mark of the gentleman.130

In the academic sphere, traditionally influenced by Oxbridge, individual
Americans  began  to  study  philology  at  German  universities  and  imported
current  models  of  Greek  perfection.  Edward  Everett  was  a  pioneer  in  the
1810s:  after  earning  a  doctorate  at  the  Göttingen  seminar  then  run  by
former students of Heyne’s, he went on to teach Greek language and culture
at  Harvard.  However,  the  new  model  had  only  limited  success:  a  push  to
prioritize  Greek  over  Latin  failed  in  the  1820s  and  1830s,  and  due  to  the
paucity  of  institutional  infrastructure  the  German  model  could  only  be
partially implemented.131

As  elsewhere,  the  secondary  school  system  bore  the  brunt  of  language
instruction:  up  to  the  1860s  Greek  and  Latin  were  entry  requirements  for
most  colleges,  and  college  instruction  heavily  indulged  in  the  classics.
Greco-Roman  “Ancient  History”  was  covered  in  schools  to  illuminate  the
context of the ancient texts. According to one survey, around 1860 a quarter
of  the  space  in  113  general  history  textbooks  was  devoted  to  Greece  and
Rome.132

Gatekeeping eroded earlier than in Europe; the Civil War was a turning
point. From then on, one or both ancient languages could be dropped, and
classical  studies  at  top  places  such  as  Harvard  and  Yale  greatly  declined
once philological entry requirements were abolished. On the eve of the First
World  War,  two-thirds  of  American  colleges  no  longer  required  either
Greek  or  Latin.  Given  that  demand  for  this  material  had  been  driven
primarily by middle-class aspirants competing for entry, it faded as access
to college widened.133

Before long, the classical template was in headlong retreat everywhere.
But this did not result in a renewed broadening of studying and teaching the
ancient  world.  By  then  the  damage  had  long  been  done—not  just  to
generations  of  students  condemned  to  cramming  but  to  the  public
perception  of  antiquity,  which  had  been  distorted  by  several  generations’
worth of heightened emphasis on Greece and Rome.

The Great Dismembering

What  did  that  bias  mean  for  the  rest  of  the  ancient  world?  Defying  the
scarcity of adequate evidence, humanists and universal historians had long

sought  to  cover  regions  outside  Europe.  New  overseas  connections
increased the flow of information, creating new opportunities.134

In the early modern period, missions, trade, and colonization boosted so-
called Oriental Studies. Even as China initially posed a challenge because it
could not be accommodated within the Christian salvation narrative, it later
appealed  to  Enlightenment  tastes.  Sustained  by  dozens  of  volumes  on
Chinese  affairs  produced  by  learned  Jesuits  for  much  of  the  eighteenth
century,  admiration  for  China’s  high  culture  and  moral  order  could  be
instrumentalized  as  a  counterpoint  to  European  absolutism,  aristocratic
privilege,  and  popery.  More  generally,  the  global  balance  of  power  still
encouraged  Europeans  to  view  Asian  societies  as  distant  neighbors  with
whom fruitful dialogue was possible.135

This changed from the 1780s onward, as the prevailing image of China
mutated from praiseworthy utopia into that of an inferior culture held back
by  stifling  despotism  and,  with  the  rise  of  race  theories,  by  innate
shortcomings.  The  Chinese  were  recast  as  a  kind  of  anti-Greeks—unfree,
culturally  mummified,  bereft  of  any  genuine  historical  development,  and
saddled with a language that was detrimental to thought.136

Thus,  by  the  early  nineteenth  century,  if  anyone  wished  to  learn  more
about  early  China  they  would  need  to  do  so  at  a  safe  distance  from  the
hallowed  study  of  Greece  and  Rome;  there  was  no  common  ground.  It  is
not without irony that Western European elites began to denigrate Chinese
culture  at  the  same  time  as  they  were  eagerly  embracing  classics-based
selection  mechanisms  not  wholly  dissimilar  to  the  Chinese  examination
system  that  Enlightenment  intellectuals  such  as  Voltaire  had  found  so
commendable.

For a while, the China craze was replaced by a wave of Indophilia. As I
already mentioned, early translations of Sanskrit texts that began to appear
in  the  late  eighteenth  century  were  popular.  India  also  appealed  to  the
Romantic gaze, which allowed its ancient denizens to be imagined as “lost
in contemplation of the spiritual nature of the world, the migration of souls,
and the recurring incarnations of the gods.”137

Then  again,  the  features  that  made  early  India  appealing  also  turned  it
into  a  potential  competitor.  Once  Greco-Roman  philology  had  hardened

into an exclusivist pursuit, its newly institutionalized position and attendant
privilege had to be defended against all comers. When Sir William Jones, in
his  third  anniversary  discourse  as  president  of  the  Asiatick  Society  in
Calcutta, not only drew attention to the connections among Indo-European
languages but proclaimed that “the Sanscrit language … is of a wonderful
structure;  more  perfect  than  the  Greek,  more  copious  than  the  Latin,  and
more  exquisitely  refined  than  either,”  any  self-respecting  classicist  would
have taken those as fighting words.138

Sporadic attempts to shift the balance of admiration in favor of ancient
India  were  consequently  met  with  growing  hostility  in  the  Greco-Roman
camp.  In  1808,  the  otherwise  philhellenic  Romantic  poet  and  intellectual
Friedrich  Schlegel  pivoted  in  that  direction  in  his  book  On  the  Language
and Wisdom of the Indians, throwing down the gauntlet by subtitling it “a
contribution to the establishment of Alterthumskunde” in a bid to broaden
the  scope  of  a  term  that  had  just  been  claimed  for  Greece  and  Rome
alone.139

Even  without  naming  names,  Schlegel’s  polemic  against  the  Wolfian
exorcism of all things “Oriental” pulled no punches: “A prejudice, which in
this respect has done great harm and still does, is the separation between the
study and the spirit of the Orient and [the study and the spirit] of the Greeks
—a  complete  difference  that  was  to  a  greater  extent  imagined  and
arbitrarily  assumed  than  it  was  based  on  reality.  In  the  history  of
humankind, the inhabitants of Asia and the Europeans are to be regarded as
members of one family, whose history must absolutely not be divided if we
want to understand the whole. And what the literature commonly refers to
as  the  Oriental  style  and  spirit  is  derived  from  just  a  few  Asiatic  peoples,
especially  the  Arabs  and  Persians,  and  from  some  texts  of  the  Old
Testament, insofar as they are judged as poetry; but to several other peoples
this  does  not  apply  at  all.”  As  counterexamples,  he  invoked  the  “prosaic
dryness  of  Chinese  books”  and  the  “beautiful  simplicity  of  the  Indian
style.”  Hard  pressed  to  make  its  case  on  substance,  the  Protestant
intelligentsia  responded  with  ad  hominem  attacks  colored  by  aversion  to
Schlegel’s conversion to Catholicism (which the sainted Winckelmann had
once been forgiven without much ado).140

Even  as  the  ties  among  Greek,  Latin,  and  Sanskrit  were  established  in
detail, India was mooted as a possible ancestral homeland, and the study of
Sanskrit became firmly established at universities, ancient South Asia was
not to be admitted to the inner circle of Altertumswissenschaft or Classics.
Likewise, Friedrich Creuzer’s attempt in the early 1810s—just a few years
after he had waxed lyrical about the divinity of the Greeks—to emphasize
the  contribution  of  Egypt  to  later  myth  and  symbolism  violated  the  hard-
liners’  claims  to  Hellenic  purity  and  absorptive  capacity  and  ended  in
failure and recrimination.141

As  a  result,  true  “Ancient  History”  was  fenced  off  from  South  or  East
Asia,  just  as  it  was  to  be  from  West  Asia  or  North  Africa.  This  of  course
fully  conformed  to  the  zeitgeist,  as  attitudes  toward  Asia  in  particular
shifted in the wake of growing power asymmetries between “the West and
the  rest.”  At  the  same  time,  anxieties  that  non-European  peoples  might
organize politically to throw off European colonial power—such as in Haiti
—discouraged more thorough engagement.142

To  say  that  Europe  and  its  colonial  offshoots  provided  an  ideal
environment for the cult of Greece and Rome to thrive and for other ancient
cultures  to  be  kept  at  arm’s  length  would  be  an  understatement.  For  one,
growing specialization helped preserve boundaries once drawn. For another,
peak  colonialism  and  the  steady  advance  of  scientific  racism  sheltered
classicists and their academic-pedagogical complex from the rising tide of
new knowledge about the bulk of the world’s ancient history.

The  combination  of  these  factors  ensured  not  just  separation  but
privilege.  No  matter  how  much  else  was  being  discovered,  true  “Ancient
History”  remained  Greco-Roman.  With  few  exceptions,  its  practitioners
showed little interest in their chosen subjects’ neighbors and peers even as
others were rescuing them from oblivion.

There is no denying that the effects of the principal driving forces—the
logic  of  disciplinary  specialization  on  the  one  hand,  and  the  impact  of
geopolitics  and  ideology  on  the  other—are  very  difficult  to  disentangle.
Martin Bernal, in the opening volume of his Black Athena series, famously
ascribed  the  growing  isolationism  of  Greco-Roman  studies—exemplified
by the increasingly strident downgrading of Egyptians and Phoenicians—to

intensifying  racism  and  anti-Semitism  in  the  academic  establishment,
echoing that of the wider cultural environment. While it would seem hard to
take issue with the overall thrust of his argument, his claim that such shifts
were  “entirely  the  result  of  social  forces”  untouched  by  the  massive
expansion  of  empirical  knowledge  inevitably  raised  hackles,  fueling
criticism  of  a  perspective  that  was  bound  to  be  resisted  within  a  field  so
deeply  rooted  in  the  elitist  construction  and  valorization  of  Greece  and
Rome  and so dependent  on the  prestige  and claim to  resources they  (used
to) confer.143

As Anthony Grafton and Suzanne Marchand observed in their critique of
Bernal and his critics, specializing narrowing need not have been prompted
primarily  by  racism  and  other  external  factors,  but  rather  by  growing
division  of  labor  in  response  to  the  proliferation  of  new  information
elsewhere—due  above  all  to  decipherments  and  excavations  in  Egypt  and
West Asia, alongside better access to South and East Asia—and the demand
for  relevant  expertise.  Thus,  a  process  of  fragmentation  that  had
commenced in  the  first  half  of  the  nineteenth  century  gathered  speed  and
became  more  systematic  and  intentional  in  the  second:  new  labels,
professional  associations,  and  academic  positions  raised  up  walls  as
specialization  generated  “a  feedback  loop  fostering  more  and  more  self-
isolation.”144

But once again that was only part of the story. James Turner reminds us
that  it  is  hard  fully  to  account  for  this  splintering.  There  had  always  been
too  much  to  know,  and  hyperspecialization  was  not  the  only  strategy  to
cope;  nor were  rigid  boundaries between fields.  Disciplines do not  simply
help  manage  information  but  serve  as  filters  and  markers  of  academic
identity;  they  actively  splinter  learning  und  understanding.  In  as  much  as
advanced  learning  led  to  disciplinization,  the  philology  seminar  had
pioneered  that  process.  As  seminar  praxis  sharply  demarcated  a  field  and
turned it into a discipline, it set the tone for others.145

More  specifically,  there  was  no  discernible  interest  in  consolidating
various  surfacing  specialisms  into  more  comprehensive  forms  of  ancient
studies—in  contrast  to  the  emergence  of  comparative  linguistics  or
comparative  religion,  not  to  mention  anthropology.  Instead,  as  “the  one

great  continent  of  baroque  philology  fell  apart  into  an  archipelago  of
stubbornly  independent  disciplines,”  space  and  language  rather  than  time
and  development  became  key  criteria  for  disagglomeration.  Rather  than
treating  this  as  a  natural  outcome  of  evidentiary  heterogeneity,  one  must
wonder  to  what  extent  this  move  was  encouraged  by  the  precociously
mature model of the philological seminar template that actively discouraged
alternative formats or outreach.146

In practical terms, that template—not least thanks to its enviable success
—inspired scholars of the ancient “Orient” to combine the words and things
of  particular  peoples  (or  “nations”)  into  their  own  copycat  versions  of
Greco-Roman  Altertumswissenschaft.  Much  like  the  latter  had  done,  the
study  of  ancient  Egypt  and  West  Asia  not  only  emancipated  itself  from
theology  but  also  embraced  narrowing  philologization  and  regionalization
at the expense of broader historical perspectives.147

Even so, asymmetries persisted. When interest first in ancient India and
then in ancient Egypt and West Asia (the “Ancient Near East”) grew in the
course  of  the  nineteenth  century  and  began  to  be  accommodated  by
university  curricula,  individual  faculty  positions  for  covering  these  areas
were  the  norm.  Seminars  or  institutes—usually  for  “Oriental  Studies,”  an
enormous  remit—were  late  in  coming  and  only  in  the  twentieth  century
spawned  discrete  antiquity-focused  subunits.  In  terms  of  institutionalized
engagement,  Greece  and  Rome  maintained  a  comfortable  lead,  as  well  as
the hierarchical positioning this entailed.148

Finally,  even  though  in  principle  neither  specialization  nor  formal
disciplinization  forestalled  bridge-building  between  Greco-Roman  studies
and  its  budding  neighbors,  on  the  whole  it  remained  the  exception.  Given
persistent  power  imbalances,  such  bridges  would  have  had  to  be  built  by
those on top, the classicists, who for the most part could not have cared less.
Against  the  backdrop  of  nineteenth-century  nationalism  and  racism,  the
of
and
aggressively
Altertumswissenschaft  (or  Classics)  played  a  major  role  in  ensuring  the
severe fragmentation of the study of the ancient world.149

essentializing

exclusionary

model

The  alternative—to  reposition  Greco-Roman  studies  as  the  core  of  an
expanding global Altertumswissenschaft—was never on the table. Even as

the lack of evidence from elsewhere could no longer be invoked to justify
separation, as Herder and Wolf had done, their and their peers’ insistence on
endured.  Twinned
incommensurability,  homogeneity,
commitments  to  philological  expertise  and  to  the  essentializing  of  the
“nation”  lent  intellectual  respectability  to  this  stance.  Non-European
antiquity  was  firmly  placed  out  of  bounds  by  a  European/non-European
divide  that  not  only  arose  from  general  cultural  shifts  but  had  more
specifically been modeled and formalized by the disciplinization of Greco-
Roman studies.150

and  purity

To the extent that racial politics contributed to this process, it had done
so  from  the  beginning.  Jürgen  Osterhammel  aptly  calls  racism  “the  dark
twin,  as  it  were,  of  emphatic  Romanticism.”  Indelibly  scarred  by  its
eighteenth-century
incapable  of
embracing one without the other.151

roots,  Altertumswissenschaft  was

The  new  model  of  Greco-Roman  studies  was  fundamentally  incompatible
with older traditions of universal history, such as that defined by the biblical
model  of  a  succession  of  empires.  In  the  1730s,  the  French  historian
Charles  Rollin’s  fourteen-volume  Histoire  ancienne  that  covered  Egypt,
Mesopotamia,  Iran,  Carthage,  Greeks,  and  Macedonians  was  extremely
popular: it went through multiple posthumous editions; was translated into
English, German, and several other languages; and was widely read in the
United States.152

Sixty years later, Arnold Ludwig Heeren, who lectured on ancient world
history  in  Göttingen  alongside  his  more  senior  colleagues  Gatterer  and
Schlözer, opted for similar breadth. His two volumes on ancient history of
1793  and  1796  ranged  from  Egypt,  Ethiopia,  and  Carthage  to  West  Asia,
Scythia,  and  India,  extending  beyond  Hebrew,  Greek,  and  Roman  sources
to  early  research  on  cuneiform  inscriptions  and  the  first  translations  of
Zoroastrian  texts.  Fame  and  translations  followed.  Heeren’s  focus  on
“politics, commerce and trade” was promising in principle, especially with
respect to the latter two: whereas there was no point in redoing all of world

history over and over again, more thematically focused treatments offered a
way forward.153

But  when  he  released  a  third  volume  on  Greece  in  1812,  it  turned  out
that time was up. The trailblazing Roman historian Barthold Georg Niebuhr
savaged  it  in  extraordinary  detail,  complaining  about  Heeren’s  lack  of
philological  expertise  and  precision  and  inadequate  coverage  of  modern
scholarship, and for good measure also about the weight given to culture as
opposed  to  political  affairs—in  other  words,  about  Heeren’s  failure  to
conform to the professional ideals of impeccable mastery of language and
bibliography that defined the emergent expert class. The subtext, albeit not
spelled  out,  was  crystal-clear:  broader  history  was  out,  replaced  by
specialist study of the nation. What is more, as Niebuhr and others applied
Wolf’s  philological  method  to  the  study  of  ancient  history,  the  latter  once
again became ancillary to language-centered philology.154

Much  had  changed  between  1796  and  1812.  The  high  priests  of  the
newly disciplined exclusionary Altertumswissenschaft had no patience with
rival  approaches,  especially  when  they  were  applied  to  sacrosanct  Greeks
rather than mere Africans or Asians. The lesson did not fall on deaf ears. In
the  following  decades,  open  dissent  was  hard  to  find.  The  main  debate
within  classical  studies,  between  a  narrow  philology  focused  on  language
and  texts  and  a  broader  version  that  paid  proper  attention  to  context  and
objects,  did  not  challenge  the  overall  frame  of  reference—the  field’s
restriction  to  Greece  and  Rome—but  merely  disagreed  over  the  scope  of
engagement.

Karl  Heinrich  Milhauser,  unsuccessful  at  the  time  and  soon  forgotten,
merits notice as a rare if quixotic challenger of the frame itself. A graduate
of  the  philology  seminar  in  Leipzig,  in  1837  he  published  a  short  book
devoted  to  a  critique  of  Wolf’s  concept  of  Altertumswissenschaft.  He
objected to the fragmentation caused by the construction of putatively self-
contained  spatiotemporal  units.  In  his  view,  time  and  space  were  not  the
proper  criteria  for  such  demarcations:  within  human  history,  antiquity
represented  “only  a  single  period  of  its  gradual  development.”  Better  to
structure “[t]he science of human life” into six separate domains, “based on
the  different  natures  of  the  subject  matter”:  the  history  of  languages

(linguistics), the history of states, the history of religions, the history of arts,
the history of intellectual endeavors and enlightenment (mostly captured by
the history of literature and philosophy), and the history of what he quaintly
labeled “luxury and fashion” but is best rendered as sociocultural history.155
Unlike periods or nations, these categories formed “six organic wholes,”
each of them spanning “the unbroken chain from the beginning of the world
to the present.” Failing to appreciate that order, “Wolf, however, turned one
part  of  that  life,  demarcated  by  means  of  an  empty  notion  of  time,  into  a
closed-off whole.” Wolf had been misled by his enthusiasm for Greece and
Rome without considering the logical implications of his approach: “Just as
Wolf  established  the  Antiquity-Science,  an  admirer  of  the  Middle  Ages
might  establish  a  separate  Middle-Age-Science,  or  an  admirer  of  the
modern age might establish a Modernity-Science. If we were to consistently
implement  Wolf’s  theory,  those  would  be  the  only  possible  structuring
principle of the main science of historical human life.”156

In  his  efforts,  Wolf  “had  killed,  as  it  were,  history,  and  the  science
alongside it.” His cardinal error had been to elevate his version of antiquity
to  “an  eternally  unchanging  idea,  to  an  abstractum,”  whose  special  status
was  moreover  entirely  unwarranted  and  unjust:  “For  science,  however,  all
peoples,  as  parts  of  humankind,  are  of  equal  importance.”  In  the  end,
Milhauser concluded, “there is no Alterthumswissenschaft.”157

This radical critique is worth remembering not merely because it was so
uncommon or, in my view, right on target. It also matters because it sought
to  open  up  space  for  a  revival  of  broader,  even  global  approaches  to  the
study  of  humankind,  in  the  process  liberating  the  study  of  ancient  history
from the iron grip of Altertumswissenschaft and reconnecting it to the study
of cognate times, places, and phases of development. It is a testimony to the
power  of  the  dominant  paradigm  that  this  proposal  sank  without  a  trace.
Five  years  later,  Milhauser  dropped  out  of  academia.  For  the  time  being,
Altertumswissenschaft lumbered on. Nor, as we shall see, were historians of
other  times  and  places  more  favorably  disposed  toward  anything  even
vaguely comparable to Milhauser’s vision.

Milhauser’s counterfactual of a “Middle-Age-Science” raised a valid point.
In  theory,  there  was  nothing  to  keep  Latinate  Catholic  Europe  of  the
medieval  period  from  being  designated  the  subject  of  yet  another  holistic
area  studies  field  modeled  on  Greco-Roman  studies.  In  practice,  however,
there were strong reasons for not going down that road. Medieval Europe,
even  if  narrowly  defined,  was  multinational  and  too  directly  connected  to
later periods to be conceptualized as a completed past world akin to that of
Greece  and  Rome.  As  such,  it  always  belonged  to  History  as  the  latter
evolved into a distinctive academic discipline. By the same token, parochial
medieval  language  and  literature  came  to  fall  within  the  purview  of
seminars of German and Romance philology, which likewise covered later
periods as well.158

As  a  nascent  discipline,  History  moved  in  a  different  direction  from
Altertumswissenschaft:  whereas  the  latter  was  at  least  nominally  holistic,
the  former  narrowed  its  thematic  scope  even  as  the  knowledge  base  kept
expanding.  The  field  became  increasingly  preoccupied  with  documents,
faintly echoing the philological turn toward rigorousness.159

The  History  seminars  that  were  set  up  at  German  universities  from  the
1830s modeled themselves on the Greco-Roman philological seminars and
adopted  their  methods—source  criticism,  lectures,  student  presentations—
as well as their desire for autonomy: the goal was to study history for the
sake of understanding history, and not merely to support philology (just as
philology  had  once  been  meant  to  support  theology).  Initially  the  new
seminars  also  strove  to  perfect  scholars  as  human  beings,  by  means  of
exacting academic work. The official rationale—not least in order to secure
funding—was the same as for philology, namely the training of secondary
schoolteachers. Yet just as with philology, training for research gained the
upper  hand,  resulting  in  the  same  mismatch  between  supply  (of  technical
skills) and demand (for pedagogy).160

The  appearance  of  separate  History  seminars  pulled  Greco-Roman
“Ancient  History”  in  different  directions.  Whereas  philology  seminars
effectively  subordinated  it  to  the  study  of  language  and  literature,  History
seminars  and  institutes  recognized  it  as  a  subject  in  its  own  right.  This
affected the positioning of “Ancient History” in ways that have endured to

the  present  day,  not  only  in  Germany  but  wherever  such  parallel  tracks
came into existence.

Yet  neither  one  of  these  options  encouraged  spatial  or  temporal
broadening.  Altertumswissenschaft  embraced  closedness  as  a  foundational
principle.  Meanwhile,  History  as  a  field  was  profoundly  shaped  by  what
Sebastian Conrad has called the “two ‘birth defects’ of the modern sciences
and  humanities  [that]  hinder  our  ability  to  achieve  a  systematic  grasp  of
processes  that  span  the  world,”  namely  fixation  on  the  nation  state  and
Eurocentrism.161

As  for  the  first  of  these,  professionalized  historians  were  increasingly
expected  to  focus  on  bounded  national  histories.  The  specificities  of
individual  cultures  and  their  states  took  center  stage  while  cross-cultural
engagement was to be limited to cases of close interconnectivity, which was
regarded  as  a  modern  phenomenon.  As  Leopold  Ranke,  the  future  patron
saint  of  the  emerging  profession,  declared  in  lectures  he  delivered  upon
assuming his perch at Berlin at the tender age of thirty, “it is beneficial to
consider different peoples in their concurrent development only if their lives
are connected, as is the case in recent times; but where this is not the case,
as in antiquity, one breaks the thread that holds together the parts that form
the history of a single nation, and heaps fragment upon fragment.”162

As  for  the  second  defect,  the  question  of  how  to  pursue  world  history
across  disparate  cultures  and  types  of  evidence  that  had  exercised
eighteenth-century  universal  historians  could  be dodged by excising  much
of  the  world  from  the  domain  of  academic  History.  This  strategy
conveniently  allowed  historians  to  apply  a  single  set  of  new  and  exacting
standards  for  mastering  evidence  that  combined  advanced  language  skills
with  painstaking  archival  research  and  a  focus  on  authority,  authenticity,
and the state without having to worry about how to approach less accessible
cases.  “Nations”  outside  Europe  entered  history  proper  only  via
international commerce or colonization. Everything prior remained outside,
consigned to experts in “Oriental Studies” and later also to anthropologists.
As  a  result,  as  Franz  Fillafer  puts  it,  “History  dwindled,  becoming  a
relevant past that was filtered through western European records,” a process
that extended to the Greco-Roman world as the first act of that story.163

As  History  joined  Altertumswissenschaft  in  exclusionary  othering,  the
European/non-European  divide  became  a  decisive  marker.  Within  and
without Europe the template of the “nation” could now be upheld, even if
only  nations  within  were  deemed  to  be  endowed  with  true  history.  At  the
same  time,  a  new  divide  opened  up.  Whereas  the  postancient  nations  of
Europe  were  not  normally  to  be  studied  in  a  holistic  manner  but  by
discipline (such as History, Literature, and Art History), scholars of ancient
Greece  and  Rome  might  adopt  either  approach  depending  on  their
institutional  affiliation  or  personal  preferences.  Cut  off  from  the  wider
ancient,  precolonial  world,  Greco-Roman  history  fell  between  the  two
stools of Altertumswissenschaft and History.

How did practitioners handle this situation? The Wolfian model obviated
the  need  for  formal  individuation  of  “Ancient  History,”  considering  it  an
integral  and  inseparable  element  of  holistic  Greco-Roman  philology.  But
this  template  prevailed  only  at  some  institutions.  At  others,  Greek  and
Roman history fell into the remit of History seminars. Earlier traditions of
greater spatiotemporal breadth only survived among the latter, at least for a
while.164

Thus, for the first two-thirds of the nineteenth century, ancient historians
outside  philology  seminars  often  escaped  narrow  specialization  as  they
covered other periods as well. Greco-Roman specialists, meanwhile, found
a  natural  habitat  in  philology  seminars  that  offered  a  growing  number  of
positions.165

Even  so,  the  latter  model  became  increasingly  unstable.  Over  time,
Altertumswissenschaft differentiated into branches that strained against the
overarching  template:  dedicated  chairs  for  Classical  Archaeology  and
Ancient History began to appear in the 1840s and 1860s, respectively. The
advance  of  compartmentalization  fed  on  the  expansion  of  funding  and
enrollment  in  the  late  nineteenth  century,  precipitating  the  separation  of
Greek from Latin philology and, around the turn of the century, leading to
the  creation  of  the  first  chairs  for  Byzantine  Studies  and  medieval  Latin.
This  process  proved  unstoppable  as  ever  more  stringent  norms  of
professionalism  took  hold,  undermining  an  organicist  ideal  of  unity  that
even as it was nominally upheld was being dismantled in practice.166

However, while classical philology and archaeology managed to acquire
clear  profiles  as  fields  that  dealt  with  texts  or  artifacts,  Greco-Roman
“Ancient  History”  remained  less  well  defined:  its  single  most  famous
practitioner,  Theodor  Mommsen,  did  not  even  consider  it  a  meaningful
academic  field.  Reflecting  this  ambiguity,  there  was  no  obvious  home  for
the  twenty-one  Ancient  History  chairs  created  between  1869  and  1904:
sometimes  they  were  attached  to  Altertumswissenschaft  and  sometimes
they were not. The one thing that united them was a hardened commitment
to Greece and Rome and exclusion of everything else.167

Although  academic  institutions  in  Britain  and  the  United  States  were
lagging behind German reforms, they eventually absorbed critical changes
that  resulted  in  an  analogous  fusion  of  language,  culture,  and  history  into
what became known as “Classics.”168

long  been  unimpressed  by

Invested as they were in maintaining a relatively narrow focus on textual
criticism and linguistic questions, scholars of Greece and Rome at Oxford
and  Cambridge  had
the  model  of
Altertumswissenschaft.  More  holistic  approaches  attracted  interest  mostly
among  outsiders  while  attempts  to  promote  them  within  academia  failed.
Multiple  obstacles  converged:  disdain  for  German  influence,  the  religious
character  of  the  Oxbridge  colleges,  a  tutorial  system  geared  toward
language instruction, and entrenched scholarly preferences.169

For  a  while,  this  ensured  independence  for  practitioners  of  Greek  and
Roman  history,  maintaining  a  tradition  going  back  to  Gibbon  and
documented  in  the  mid-nineteenth  century by Connop  Thirlwall’s massive
Greek History  alongside  George  Grote’s  more  famous  counterpart.  Yet  in
the end, the German model proved hard to ignore, and although it had been
premature  as  well  as  hyperbolic  for  an  anonymous  reviewer  in  1834  to
proclaim that German scholarship on Greek and Latin had advanced to such
an  extent  that  other  European  nations  were  “satisfied  to  follow  as  mere
imitators  of  their  triumphant  career,”  it  did  leave  a  growing  mark  on  the
British academic landscape as well. Thus, despite the prolonged dominance

of textual work, engagement with Greece and Rome eventually broadened
by absorbing realia from history to archaeology, establishing a more holistic
field.170

At the same time, specialization proceeded apace. The Camden chair in
Ancient  History  had  been  set  up  in  Oxford  in  1622.  The  earliest
chairholders  worked  on  everything  under  the  sun,  with  some  preference
given to church history. Later on, Thomas Warton (1785–1790) wrote about
English  literature  while  Thomas  Winstanley  (1790–1823)  was  also
professor of Arabic. Edward Cardwell’s (1825–1861) main field was church
history, and George Rawlinson (1861–1889) published books on Egyptian,
Mesopotamian, Phoenician, Parthian, and Sasanian history. Only afterward
did Greece and Rome rule supreme, until the position was limited to Roman
history  in  1910  following  the  creation  of  the  Wykeham  chair  for  Greek
history. The inevitable narrowing is less telling than its final destination.

Teaching  arrangements  reflected  both  the  emergence  of  the  holistic
template  and  its  absorption  of  Greek  and  Roman  history,  shorn  of  that  of
other  parts  of  the  ancient  world.  In  Oxford,  from  the  early  nineteenth
century,  a  bachelor’s  degree  in  the  arts  involved  exams  in  Literae
Humaniores that required knowledge of Greek and Latin and of rhetoric and
moral  philosophy  drawn  from  ancient  authors,  as  well  as  facility  in  Latin
composition. At the time there was still room for Hebrew.

By  the  late  1830s,  “ancient”  history  had  become  central  to  the  final
exams,  more  so  than  modern  history.  In  1850  a  two-part  model  was
introduced for the Literae Humaniores or Classics degree, comprising a first
part  on  Greek  and  Latin  language  and  literature  and  a  second  one  on
philosophy  and  history.  In  1872,  the  Lit.  Hum.  Board  established  Ancient
History  as  a  separate  field,  wholly  Greco-Roman  in  nature  and  based  on
prescribed Greek and Roman authors. An Oxford Ancient History Society,
set  up  in  1884  to  encourage  research  by  undergraduates,  was  soon  folded
into  the  Oxford  Philological  Society,  whereas  the  subfaculty  of  Ancient
History,  launched  as  a  dining  club  in  1903,  was  formalized  in  1914.  The
embrace by Classics was complete.171

But  the  Oxford  model  was  never  exported  to  other  institutions.  Even
though it took longer, the Cambridge version of Classics became the norm.

In Cambridge, the traditional dominance of Greek and Latin language and
literature  had  continued  for  much  longer.  A  classical  exam  structure
(“tripos”)  was  set  up  in  the  1820s,  open  only  to  those  who  had  scored
highly  in  mathematics.  Proposals  to  include  Greek  and  Roman  history
encountered  resistance:  although  an  Ancient  History  paper  had  been  an
option since 1849, it was deemed inferior, and much debate was required to
install  it  as  a  regular  subject.  One  critic  in  1866  fretted  about  Ancient
History  introducing  “an  alien  and  disturbing  element  into  our  great
Classical  examination,”  even  as  others  who  stressed  the  inseparability  of
language and content prevailed.172

tradition  and  as  a  concession

The  Cambridge  Tripos  reform  of  1882  was  a  compromise  struck
between traditionalists keen on language and reformers keen on upgrading
the  role  of  content,  context,  and  culture.  In  the  new  two-part  examination
structure,  the  first  part  was  limited  to  language  and  literature,  in  keeping
with
that  ensured
gatekeeping, whereas the second part covered not just more of the same but
also  history,  philosophy,  and  archaeology.  Ironically,  this  broadening  also
resulted in a narrowing, as inclusion of Sanskrit, which would have found a
place in a more language-focused curriculum, was rejected. As a further sop
to conservatives, it was possible to receive an honors degree by completing
only part 1. Part 2 and hence Ancient History remained optional until it was
made  mandatory  in  1918.  Meanwhile,  the  first  Ancient  History  chair  in
Cambridge did not materialize until 1898.173

to  conservatives

The  conventional  markers  of  professionalization,  such  as  societies  and
journals,  were  late  in  coming.  By  the  same  token,  research  training  in
Classics  and  cognate  fields  received  only  limited  support.  At  Cambridge
and Oxford, senior doctorates in the arts appeared only near the end of the
nineteenth century; at the latter, a minimum of thirteen years of study acted
as  a  formidable  hurdle.  A  practicable  PhD  format  was  finally  introduced
after the First World War.174

As  a result,  developments in  Germany  and Britain  were  separated  by a
century and more. It was not until the 1920s that Classics had become fully
established  in  ways  comparable  to  German  Altertumswissenschaft.  But
once this model had finally taken over, it displayed extraordinary resilience

even  as  it  crumbled  elsewhere,  most  notably  in  its  native  Germany.  The
integrated  Cantabrigian  version  of  Classics  has  survived  ever  since.  Only
University College London placed its Ancient History chair, created in the
early twentieth century, in the History department.

Divisions  into  Classics,  History,  and  Oriental  Studies  echoed  those  in
Germany. In the 1880s, the Oxford Faculty of Arts admitted a faculty board
of Oriental Languages alongside those for Literae Humaniores and Modern
History, which in due course evolved into separate units. In Cambridge, by
contrast,  Oriental  Studies  never  absorbed  the  study  of  non-Greco-Roman
ancient  cultures:  instead,  they  were  hived  off  to  anthropology  and
archaeology, to be ever further removed from Greco-Roman affairs.

In sum, in both teaching and research, the history of Greece and Rome
was firmly separated not only from later periods of history but also from the
history of other ancient societies.

In the United States, the lack of library resources slowed down the adoption
of  the  ambitious  template  of  Altertumswissenschaft.  It  was  only  in  the
second half of the nineteenth century that field formation took off. The idea
that Classics could be considered a science (as Wolf had envisioned) gained
ground.  The  American  Philological  Association  was  instrumental  in  the
process of disciplinization: founded in 1869 to cover a much wider range of
traditions, it was gradually taken over by scholars specializing in the study
of Greece and Rome.175

While the field expanded from teaching Greek and Latin to coverage of
content  and  context,  language  instruction  remained  central.  Classical
archaeology  was  effectively  coopted  in  the  late  nineteenth  century.  The
American Institute of Archaeology, founded in 1879 “to quicken interest in
classical  and  Biblical  Studies,  to  promote  an  acquaintance  with  the
prehistoric  antiquities  of  our  own  country,”  also  fell  under  the  sway  of
Greco-Roman Classics; it had privileged classical Greece from the start. By
the turn of the century, the fusion of different specialisms into a full-service
version of Altertumswissenschaft had been completed, and universities had
begun to house Classics departments and graduate programs.176

The  case  of  Charles  Waldstein  is  emblematic  of  the  success  of  the
Wolfian  model  across  different  countries.  Born  into  a  Jewish  family  of
Austrian origin in New York City in 1856, he earned degrees at Columbia
and  at  Heidelberg  and  subsequently  ran  the  Fitzwilliam  Museum  in
Cambridge  and the  American School  in  Athens. As  he explained  in  1885,
the “science of classical antiquity” was based on three principal sources—
words,  monuments,  and  events—that  were  to  be  studied  by  philology  “in
the  widest  sense,”  archaeology, and history. History, which as he allowed,
“might comprise the whole of the knowledge concerning the past; but it is
here used in the most restricted acceptation of the term,” that is, “Greek and
Roman  history.”  Although  Wolf  might  have  disapproved  of  Waldstein’s
constricting use of the  term  “philology,” he would  have been intrigued by
his even narrower definition of history; just as Winckelmann and Humboldt
would have been pleased by his parroting their explanation for the artistic
qualities of the Greeks, rooted in physical terrain and freedom.177

Just  as  in  Germany,  Greco-Roman  “Ancient  History”  lacked  a  fixed
academic  home.  In  some  university  settings,  it  was  absorbed  into  the
holistic  Classics  model  and  thereby  detached  from  other  (Western)
histories;  yet  it  was  just  as  likely  to  find  itself  attached  to  History.
Outcomes  were  not  necessarily  determined  by  rarefied
intellectual
considerations:  when  the  Classics  and  History  departments  at  Harvard  in
the  1890s fought  over control  of Ancient  History, they  were  motivated  by
concerns about enrollment and staffing.178

No  single  hegemonic  paradigm  emerged:  unlike  in  Germany,  where
Altertumswissenschaft  died  a  slow  death  and  delivered  Greco-Roman
“Ancient  History”  to  History  programs,  or  in  the  United  Kingdom,  where
individual  American
Classics  swept  (almost)  everything  before
universities  idiosyncratically  followed  one  path  or  the  other,  creating  a
hodgepodge that persists to the present day. Insofar as this pluralism yielded
a  benefit,  it  was  to  showcase  alternative  arrangements  and  their  trade-offs
side by side. Yet the drawback was the same: both arrangements ensured the
isolation  of  Greek  and  Roman  history  from  other  ancient  histories  as  area
studies programs or anthropology claimed the latter.179

it,

Lack  of  space  prevents  discussion  of  the  French  and  Italian  traditions.
Suffice  it  to  say  that  despite  valiant  resistance,  a  humanistic  tradition  that
emphasized  engagement  with  antiquity  as  an  art  and  source  of  pleasure
rather  than  a  science  was  in  the  end  supplanted  by  German-inspired
principles  of  research-oriented  professionalism.  The  first  academic  units
based  on  the  philology  seminar  template  were  established  in  France  from
the  1860s  onward  while  the  German  model  became  the  ideal  in  Italian
academia.  By  the  end  of  the  nineteenth  century,  it  had  come  to  dominate
much of Europe and its settler offshoots.180

In  Germany  itself,  meanwhile,  efforts  either  to  preserve  a  unitary
Altertumswissenschaft  or  to  open  “Ancient  History”  to  ancient  Egypt  and
West Asia both failed. Just as in the late eighteenth century Göttingen had
been  an  incubator  of  initiatives  that  pulled  historians  in  wildly  different
directions—exemplified by Heyne, Gatterer, and Meiners—around 1900 the
University  of  Berlin  played  the  same  role,  this  time  with  the  classical
philologist  Ulrich  von  Wilamowitz-Moellendorff  and  the  ancient  historian
Eduard Meyer taking the lead.

In  a  nod  to  the  Wolfian  vision,  Wilamowitz  labored  to  create  a  unified
institute for all matters Greek and Roman. For some time, Berlin’s Greco-
Roman  historians  had  run  their  own  institute  alongside  the  original
philology seminar. They were coopted into the latter by 1907, followed five
this
later  by  classical  archaeology.  Wilamowitz  considered
years
arrangement  “perfect,  in  some  sense  a  model.”  Nonetheless,  the  study  of
both  ancient  history  and  archaeology  had  reached  such  a  degree  of
independence  that  this  (re)unification  was  more  apparent  than  real.
Likewise, classical philology—now more modestly defined as the study of
Greek and Latin language and literature—had already begun to be accepted
as  a  separate  discipline.  Altertumswissenschaft  had  irremediably  fractured
and no amount of organizational legerdemain could turn back the clock.181

At the same time, each of these subfields continued to keep the ancient
world beyond Greece and Rome at bay. When Berlin’s preeminent ancient

historian Eduard Meyer attempted to break down these barriers, his efforts
were  custom-tailored  to  his  own  polymathic  capabilities  and  therefore
doomed to failure.

After  studying  Greek,  Latin,  Hebrew,  and  Arabic  in  secondary  school,
Meyer added Persian, Turkish, Egyptian, Syriac, and Sanskrit at university
and  later  ventured  into  cuneiform  texts  and  Hittite.  Drawing  on  this
unparalleled  skill  set  and  encyclopedic  knowledge,  he  published
monographs  on  the  origins  of  the  Jews,  early  Christianity,  the  end  of  the
Roman republic, the history of dynastic Egypt, the Hittites, and much else
besides.  His  main  project  was  a  history  of  antiquity  that  covered  the
Mediterranean,  Egypt,  and  West  Asia  from  the  beginnings  (prefaced  by  a
substantial  survey  of  general  anthropology)  until  the  late  fourth  century
BCE,  and  since  1884  appeared  in  multiple  volumes  and  editions.  The
objective  was  to  produce  a  unified  historical  account  supported  by
specialist-level research and expertise.182

Meyer  was  not  alone  in  Berlin  in  his  ambition  to  erase  the  divide
between  Greece  and  Rome  and  its  antecedents  and  neighbors.  The  local
Oriental  languages  scholar  Hugo  Winckler  observed  that  “only  narrow-
minded philistines could look down on the ancient Orient, from which they
had  received  all  cultural  achievements,  and  that  we  cannot  understand
antiquity if we know only a small part of it,” and insisted that “we should
finally  universally  acknowledge  that  there  must  not  be  a  barrier  between
research on classical antiquity and research on the ‘barbarians,’ just as there
had never been a barrier between the intellectual and cultural lives of those
two areas.” But only Meyer made the colossal effort this agenda required.183
In  his  treatise  on  the  philosophy  of  history  published  in  1902,  Meyer
rejected  the  dominant  structuring  of  history  by  nations  and  stressed  the
