primacy of universal history. In this, echoing Ranke, he put his emphasis on
connectivity: “all peoples, which were politically and culturally lastingly in
contact,  form  an  indissoluble  unit  for  history  until  that  connection  was
dissolved  in  the  course  of  historical  development.”  Yet  unlike  for  Ranke
and  his  epigones,  this  approach  was  to  include  premodern  cases  well
beyond Europe.184

Accepting  that  concrete  studies  could  not  always  cast  their  nets  wide,
Meyer  argued—in  terms  that  foreshadowed  current  approaches  to  global
history (see chapter 3)—that even specialized work needed to be framed in
global  terms:  “The  histories  of  the  individual  peoples,  states,  nations  are
merely  parts  of  that  general  history;  one  may  make  those  the  subject  of
separate  accounts,  but  can  never  view  them  in  isolation  all  by  themselves
and  without  continuous  consideration  of  the  universal  context.  The
foundation and objective of all historical study and of all historical research
even on the most minute details can only ever be universal history.”185

Most importantly for the issue at hand, Meyer drew a contrast between
history and philology, expressly rejecting Wolfian conceptualization: when
it comes to the study of antiquity, “the amalgamation of both, their merger
under the heading of ‘Altertumswissenschaft,’ is unjustified and confusing,
and has become  quite detrimental  to  the  treatment of ancient  history. This
has  artificially  promoted  the  shredding  of  what  belongs  together  and  the
isolation of particular elements of a field, as well as specialization and the
much-hyped  academic  division  of  labor,  on  which  our  era  prides  itself  as
much  as  any  people  or  any  person  can  pride  themselves  on  their
weaknesses.  The  history  of  antiquity  is  and  must  never  be  anything  other
than part of a single, general history, and both parties—ancient and modern
historical research—must never forget that.” Meyer consequently criticized
the  fact  that  in  some  universities,  ancient  historians  were  not  based  in
historical seminars  (alongside  practitioners  of modern  history)  but  lumped
together  with  philologists  in  units  devoted  to  Altertumswissenschaft—a
transparent criticism of his colleague Wilamowitz’s reorganization of their
Berlin institute.186

In  addition  to  resistance  rooted  in  the  hardening  of  disciplinary
boundaries  and  traditional  preference  for  pairing  Greece  and  Rome,
Meyer’s initiative encountered three major problems. Feasibility was one of
them: only a polymath could hope to master all the necessary skills. Then
again, for true experts even that would never be good enough: even though
back  in  the  gymnasium  Meyer  had  translated  the  notoriously  challenging
Latin  author  Tacitus  into  Greek,  Wilamowitz  nevertheless  doubted  his
control  of  the  minutiae  of  Greek  alongside  his  command  of  specialized

scholarship. The second problem was that Meyer’s approach ran counter to
the  zeitgeist  of  the  first  half  of  the  twentieth  century,  which  drove
nationalism to unprecedented heights.187

Finally,  his  initiative  did  not  call  for  genuine  world  history:  happy  to
assert  the  putative  centrality  of  Greek  culture,  Meyer  conceived  of  the
premodern  world  as  a  series  of  separate  clusters  of  sustained  interaction.
Defined by a rather limited notion of connectivity that separated South Asia
from  Iran,  Meyer’s  engagement  could  not  be  separated  from  his  own
portfolio of language skills. In fact, when an attempt was made in 1909 to
establish comparative history as a field at the University of Berlin, Meyer
penned  the  official  memorandum  that  rejected  this  proposal,  expressing
concern  that  students  of  such  a  subject  might  end  up  “half-educated”  and
bereft  of  proper  language  skills,  and  objecting  to  the  study  of  historical
societies alongside “cultureless prehistorical peoples.”188

Meyer  was  unable  to  offer  a  recipe  beyond  individual  genius-level
polymathy.  And  even  that  rare  quality  imposed  iron  limits  that  made  true
globalization  impossible:  later  generations  rightly  rejected  Meyer’s  choice
of scope as the creation of a “pseudouniversality” that in the hands of any
one scholar could necessarily never expand to include South and East Asia,
the Americas, and Africa.189

Fossilization

Yet  even  as  Meyer  brilliantly  failed,  the  Altertumswissenschaft  of  Greece
and Rome was already on its way out. In a sense, the holistic principle had
been a lost cause from the very start. In 1807, Wolf had already castigated
the “endless striving, regarding all of antiquity’s remains and especially the
languages,  to  collect  particulars  as  particulars  without  drawing  on  solid
general principles, without having any idea of the spirit that fashions all the
particulars into a harmonious whole.”190

His  elaborate  taxonomy  was  supposed  to  fix  that  problem,  as  was
Böckh’s revised version. Yet the latter’s hope that “the building blocks we
gather  will  gradually  join  together  into  a  unified  work  of  art  and
magnificent edifice of human knowledge” never came true. Scholarship that
was  more  and  more  specialized—the  “endless  striving”—carried  the  day,

making  a  mockery  of  the  holistic  ideal.  From  the  mid-nineteenth  century,
“the  great  creation  of  Humboldt  and  Wolf  looked  very  much  like  a  self-
consuming artifact.”191

them

At the same time, the arbitrary demarcations that defined the field ceased
to  be  sacrosanct.  The  eminent  philologist  and  scholar  of  comparative
in  1882:  “A
religion  Hermann  Usener  mercilessly  ridiculed
construction  of  philology  such  as  Boeckh’s  is  impossible  today.  It  is  as  if
one  cut  out  of  a  book  all  the  passages  that  deal  with  a  particular  people,
unconcerned  about  the  specific  context,  and  lined  them  up  side  by  side.
These days even a student knows from hundredfold experience that we can
often  gain  even  the  most  immediate  understanding  of  a  concrete
phenomenon  only  once we  have taken  off  the  blinkers  that the  philologist
likes to attach to the left and right of his eyes.” Just like Meyer, Usener felt
that the general historical disciplines that dealt with history, linguistics, and
religion  had  fully  absorbed  the  historical  elements  of  philology.  Echoing
Milhauser, Usener’s verdict was unambiguous: “philology is not a historical
science.”192

That he was nevertheless largely unsuccessful in convincing his peers of
the merits of thematic comparative study should not come as a surprise: the
ossification of established academic tradition was hard to overcome. Thus,
the  more  the  old  template  came  under  pressure,  the  more  it  became
enshrined  in  veritable  monuments.  The  most  striking  example  of  that
reaction
classischen
Alterthumswissenschaft” compiled by August Friedrich Pauly from 1837 to
1852,  which  starting  in  1893  was  replaced  by  the  multigenerational
“Realencyclopädie  der  classischen  Altertumswissenschaft”  in  eighty-three
volumes that took eighty-five years to complete—a fearsome relic of faith
in Greco-Roman unity and exclusivity.193

“Real-Encyclopädie

was

der

the

Wilamowitz  stood  ready  to  engage  dissenters.  In  a  speech  in  1892,  he
acknowledged the separation of history from philology for the study of the
more  recent  past  but  warned  against  following  this  example  for  Greco-
Roman  antiquity  itself:  even  if  genuine  breadth  was  impractical  in  actual
scholarship, “this holistic vision remains the ideal demand of scholarship.”

Thus,  he  insisted,  we  must  reject  “the  desire  for  emancipation”—note  the
term!—among certain historians and archaeologists who wished to imitate
their  peers  who  dealt  with  later  periods  in  abandoning  the  study  and
teaching  of  ancient  languages;  for  this  could  only  invalidate  their  own
work. “Ancient History” was to be kept on a tight leash.194

But when Wilamowitz in 1921 reiterated the foundational vision of the
field, he not only abandoned its claim to superiority as a thing of the past
and  gave  up  on  strict  demarcations  but  also  found  himself  compelled  to
recognize new disciplinary divisions, if only as a sign of human weakness.
“Philology—which is still called classical even though it no longer claims
the  primacy  that  this  epithet  implies—is  defined  by  its  subject  matter,
Greco-Roman culture in its essence and in every expression of its existence.
This civilization is a single whole, even if it may not be possible precisely
to define when it began and ended. It is the task of classical scholarship to
bring  that  past  existence  back  to  life  by  the  power  of  scholarship.…
Because the life we strive to fathom is a single whole, our science too is a
single whole. Its division into the disciplines of philology, ancient history,
epigraphy,  numismatics,  and  latterly  also  papyrology  can  be  justified  only
with the limitations of human capacity and even in the specialist must not
smother awareness of the whole.”195

By  then  unitary  holism  had fully  devolved  into a purely  notional  ideal,
no longer applied in academic practice. And even that ideal was effectively
called  into  question  by  Wilamowitz’s  reference  to  the  barriers  that  had
fallen—between Greece and Rome and Asia and Egypt, between “classical
and  Christian  philology  and  archaeology,”  between  Greek  and  Latin  and
Arabic and Syriac. Yet even as this newly permeable field could no longer
claim  to  be  either  classical  or  classicizing,  it  somehow  remained  an
identifiable  “living  whole.”  It  is  unclear  how  all  these  disparate  elements
were  to  be  reconciled:  the  center  simply  could  not  hold.  In  an  address  in
1900,  Wilamowitz  himself  had  already  admitted  that  “antiquity  as  a  unit
and as an ideal are gone; scholarship itself has destroyed this belief.”196

In  his  memorial  speech  on  Wilamowitz,  his  Berlin  successor  Werner
Jaeger addressed the tension between idealism and historicism: “Two souls
continuously struggled in his chest: the historian, who only wants to know

what  happened,  and  the  humanist  and  philologist,  who  must  worship  and
proclaim  what  is  great  and  eternal.”  Yet  what  was  true  of  Wilamowitz
remains true of the field itself: these tensions endure wherever a version of
Altertumswissenschaft  has  survived,  most  notably  in  the  Anglosphere’s
Classics.197

In a field founded on the fusion of the idealization of the subject matter
and the application of rigorous empirical scholarship, the latter irrevocably
gained the upper hand even as it never quite managed to exorcize attenuated
versions of the former. As historicism pulled Greece and Rome back into a
wider  stream  of  past  cultures  and
their  artifacts  and  histories,
Altertumswissenschaft as a lodestar of “Bildung” lost what had remained of
its  Romantic  sheen,  even  as  its  institutionalized  assets—chairs,  seminars,
curricula—were  assiduously  guarded  by  the  elect.  This  created  serious
messaging  problems  for  the  academic-pedagogical  complex:  how  could
continuing investment at the customary level be justified?198

The  “Western Civ”  route  offered  one way  out.  No longer  meant  to  enable
communion  with  the  ideal,  the  study  of  Greece  and  Rome  could  also  be
legitimated  as  a  way  of  getting  to  know  the  cradle  of  “our”  (“Western”)
civilization.  Once  again,  Wilamowitz  entered  the  fray.  At  a  major
convention for secondary school education in 1900, he laid out the strategy.
A  century  ago,  he  said,  antiquity  had  been  held  up  as  an  ideal  and  was
supposed to be studied for that reason. “Now we have convinced ourselves
that that was an error.” We had come to understand “instead” (!) that we had
close  ties  to  that  period,  which  could  still  be  approached  as  “a  historical
unit.” While outright idealization was passé, Greece and Rome were still of
great value as the distant progenitors of the present. This perspective called
for greater breadth: Paul became as important as Plato, and classical Greece
might even have to take a back seat to the Hellenistic period that shaped the
Roman  empire  and  early  Christianity  (and  thereby  had  the  biggest  impact
on the present).199

Wilamowitz went all in: European civilization was not merely based on
Greece; it was in a sense still Greek. “This is what we want to tell people:
all  of  you,  whichever  nation  you  belong  to,  as  long  as  you  are  civilized

peoples, share the same foundation”—that was “what ties us together, that
the world is one, because it rests on the same culture, just as it is governed
by  the  same  God.”  Greco-Roman  “world  culture”  was  “not  only  the
foundation but also as it were a version of our own, and that [ancient world
culture] is Greek, since all of Roman civilization was merely an integrating
province thereof.” Scholars must therefore convince their students that the
lineages of all aspects of our thinking “lead directly to Hellas.” The Greeks
were “the ancestors of all modern civilized peoples.”200

In

this

terms,

tactical

reconfiguration  made  perfect

sense:
accommodating widespread disillusionment with the idealization of Greece
and  Rome,  it  capitalized  on  peak  colonialism,  Western  global  hegemony,
and white supremacy. But it was above all a rearguard action, covering an
unstoppable retreat as the abolition of Greek- and Latin-based credentialing
and  entry  requirements  fatally  undermined  the  political  economy  of  the
academic-pedagogical  complex.  The  privileges  it  had  been  created  to
ensure were no longer sustainable, even if they eroded at different speeds.
At the top of the food chain, conservative inertia allowed classical scholars
at  universities  and  research  academies  to  hang  on  to  prestige  and  funding
even  as  secondary  education  and  the  bourgeoisie  turned  away  from  their
former pet project.201

Wilamowitz sought to do.202

architects

original

the

This in turn made it desirable to reposition Greece and Rome, away from
the  image  of  a  self-contained,  completed,  and  somewhat  alien  double
culture and toward that of an integral part of a longer lineage leading up to
the present—in short, a return to older models of cultural transmission that
dated  back  to  the  Middle  Ages.  Yet  appealing  as  this  might  have  been  to
schoolchildren  or  the  general  public,  it  did  not  fully  address  the  needs  of
specialized  academics.  Whereas  the  idealization  model  and  the  culture  of
rigorous scholarship it had spawned called for intense study and immersion

in dead languages, the heritage model did not. It was perfectly possible to
accept and cherish one’s presumed Greek and Roman cultural roots without
being  particularly  familiar  with  ancient  matters  and  certainly  without
knowing the relevant languages.

As  a  result,  the  heritage  approach  was  better  suited  to  introductory
pedagogy, such as reading courses in translation, than to deep engagement
or  original  scholarship.  This  helps  account  for  its  popularity  especially  in
the  United  States,  where  elites  welcomed  the  opportunity  to  construct  a
narrative of Eurocentric superiority and progress that linked their country to
Europe  and  its  Greco-Roman  foundations.  Relaxing  the  constraints  of
classicizing purity, ancient Egypt and West Asia were pressed into service
as  the  earliest  links  in  that  evolutionary  chain.  We  can  observe  this
expansion  in  the  works  of  two  leading  textbook  authors,  James  Harvey
Robinson,  whose  first  textbook  on  the  history  of  western  Europe  in  1902
commenced  with  the  fall  of  Rome  but  whose  coverage  in  later  books
expanded to include Egypt, West Asia, Greece and Rome; and Harry Elmer
Barnes,  for  whom  pre-Greek  antiquity  was  likewise  limited  to  Egypt  and
West Asia.203

That  approach  was  not  new:  even  if  the  specific  concept  of  “Western
Civilization”  had  only  been  popularized  in  the  context  of  the  First  World
War,  these  cultural  lineages  had  long  been  studied  under  different  labels.
What  was  new  was  the  distillation  of  more  eclectic  forms  of  engagement
and instruction into formalized “Western Civ” courses. A process driven by
some of the most prominent universities in the United States, it established
a tradition of surveys “from Plato to NATO.” The moniker “Western” was
meant to upgrade the United States vis-à-vis Europe: fealty to “European”
civilization was no longer good enough for the new superpower.204

Whether in the United States or elsewhere, however, this approach could
not hope to satisfy Wilamowitz’s demand for deep immersion. Greece and
Rome, and their “Ancient History” along with them, had been turned into a
distant  antechamber  of  the  present,  only  opportunistically  connected  to  its
immediate  neighbors  and  strictly  cut  off  from  other  parts  of  the  ancient
world.

At  the  same  time,  the  idealization  model  showed  considerable  staying
power  despite  its  growing  irrelevance  to  public  discourse.  In  Germany
itself,  Wilamowitz’s  push  did  not  lead  anywhere.  Just  a  few  years  earlier
Jacob  Burckhardt  had  still
the  “free  …,  original,  and
identified
spontaneous”  Greeks  “as  the  brilliant  people  of  the  world.”  More
importantly,  Wilamowitz  failed  to  convince  conservative  schoolteachers,
committed  to  the  ideal  of  the  “classical,”  of  the  need  to  embrace
postclassical sources and the New Testament. The latter had been rejected
as a philological school text as far back as Heyne. The classicizing idealist
template  had  been  hammered  into  too  many  heads  for  too  long,  and  had
been faithfully reproduced for too many generations.205

The  First  World  War  finally  undermined  the  privileges  and  funding
sources  of  the  academic-pedagogical  complex.  In  its  wake,  turbo-charged
nationalism and racism revived key ideas that had once been promoted by
the  founding  fathers  of  Altertumswissenschaft.  Thus,  Helmut  Berve,  a
noted  historian  of  ancient  Greece  and  steadfast  supporter  of  national
socialism, cast doubt on the viability of Oriental Studies, “which have been
cultivated based on a different, ‘objective’ attitude” but owing to the alien
(read:  non-Aryan)  character  of  their  subject  matter  must  confront  the
question  “Is  understanding,  in  a  deeper  sense,  possible  there?”  Such
understanding was required more than ever: for “who, today, would content
themselves with the objectives of positivism, who would want and be able
to  forgo  an  understanding  of  historical  subjects  in  their  peculiarity,  given
that  we  pursue  a  particular  field  of  study  in  order  to  understand  the
peculiarity of its subject matter?” It is hard to miss Berve’s alignment with
the  claims  of  cultural  incommensurability  that  were  so  dear  to  the
Romantics,  and  with  Wolf’s  musing  that  German  philhellenism  may  have
been kindled by descent from the same original tribe.206

At the same time, the “third humanism”—that is, neo-neohumanism—of
Werner  Jaeger,  Wilamowitz’s  successor  in  Berlin,  appealed  to  the  exact
same  principles.  In his Paideia  of  1933,  Jaeger  stressed  that  there  was  no
history of the whole planet, only histories of clusters. The European cluster
was rooted in the Greeks, who, unique in being endowed with “culture” as a

“conscious  ideal,”  ensured  the  special  character  of  the  West.  The  Greeks
owed  their  special  position  not  merely  to  the  “affinity  of  racial  kinship,
however  important  especially  that  factor  is  for  the  inner  understanding  of
another  people”—Berve  would  have  approved—but  also  to  the  “fateful
spiritual  connection”  established  by  enduring  cultural  legacies.  The
implication  is  clear:  since  no  people  can  be  valued  more  highly  than  the
Greeks, and thus also those associated with them, others must by definition
be inferior.207

By hook or by crook, the premise of Greco-Roman supremacy was to be
upheld, whether by a racist fascist who returned to German professorships
after  the  war  or  by  a  racist  neo-neohumanist  who  left  Nazi  Germany  in
1936 for plum jobs at the University of Chicago and Harvard. The ghosts of
the past stubbornly refused to give up their own.

What  emerged  from  the  wreckage  of  Nazi  Germany  was  a  safetyist
positivism that retained old demarcations in space and time as a matter of
course.  By  then,  most  Greco-Roman  “ancient  historians”  identified
primarily  as  historians,  and  were  routinely  attached  to  History  programs.
But although Altertumswissenschaft was both spiritually and institutionally
virtually  defunct,  Greco-Roman  “Ancient  History”  continued  to  be
separated  from  other  ancient  studies.  There  was  no  room  for  any  ancient
world  history.  In  East  Germany,  Marxist  ancient  historians  dutifully
expressed some interest in collaborative work on a global scale—homing in
on  dynamics  such  as  class  struggle—but  did  not  make  any  tangible
progress.208

Elsewhere  in  Western  Europe  and  in  North  America,  the  lack  of
comparable  upheavals  generated  even  less  impetus  for  change.  When  it
came  to  the  study  of  the  ancient  world,  the  postwar  expansion  of  higher
education  simply  generated  commensurate  funding  for  more  of  the  same.
The  traditions  and  demarcations  that  had  formed  in  the  course  of  the
the  academic
nineteenth  century  slowly  but  surely  fossilized
arrangements we observe today.

into

These  fossilized  patterns  are  now  firmly  inscribed  in  organizational
structures.  There  are  currently  fifty-one  universities  in  Germany  that
employ  historians  of  Greece  and  Rome.  In  forty-two  of  them,  these
academics  are  exclusively  housed  in  History  departments.  Whether  or  not
they  form  their  own  subunit  depends  on  their  institutions’  overall  size.  In
two other universities, separate units for History and Ancient History exist
side  by  side.  Only  seven  institutions  still  maintain  departments  for
Altertumswissenschaft  that  include  Ancient  History,  though  in  one  case
jointly  with  the  History  department;  in  another  case  Ancient  History  is
excluded  from  Altertumswissenschaft.  Regardless  of  where  it  is  based,
“Ancient History” generally means Greece and Rome: only on the rarest of
occasions does the presence of ancient West Asia in an “Ancient History”
unit serve as the exception that proves the rule.209

In  Austria,  Altertumswissenschaft  has  been  enjoying  a  longer  shelf  life
in  the  form  of  two  surviving  or  reconstituted  units  that  include  Ancient
History. In one case Ancient History claims a free-standing department and
in another it is part of History, while in a third Greco-Roman and Ancient
Near  Eastern  History  are  (but  did  not  used  to  be)  joined  together.  In
German-speaking Switzerland, two universities place Greece and Rome in
History  and  one  in  Altertumswissenschaft.  In  the  Netherlands,  Ancient
History is for the most part incorporated into History programs.210

In Italy, cost-cutting consolidations have on occasion reversed German-
style splintering. Thus, at the Sapienza University of Rome, a larger entity
was forged from previous units for classical philology, ancient history, and
archaeology  (including  that  of  Egypt  and  West  Asia).  Bologna  created  a
superdepartment  of  History  and  Culture  out  of  Ancient  History,  History,
Archaeology, Oriental Studies, and Anthropology departments, but left out
Greek  and  Latin  language  and  literature.  In  Milan,  Naples,  and  Turin,  by
contrast, historians of Greece and Rome reside in History programs. Israeli
institutions  have  witnessed  a  shift  of  Ancient  History  from  Classics  to
History.211

Only in the Anglosphere has the holistic template of Greco-Roman studies
survived  intact.  If  anything,  this  has  caused  even  greater  isolation  for

historians  of  Greece  and  Rome:  whereas  both  models  cut  them  off  from
non-European  ancient  history,  the  Classics  model  separates  them  from  all
subsequent history as well.

In  the  United  Kingdom,  Greco-Roman  “Ancient  History”  is  generally
domiciled in Classics departments (which are sometimes labeled “Classics
and  Ancient  History  and/or  Archaeology”),  not  just  at  Cambridge  and
Oxford but also at a series of high-ranked places in England, Scotland, and
Wales.  Only  University  College  London  has  kept  it  in  the  History
department, where Greece and Rome are currently quite uniquely joined by
ancient  China,  Anatolia,  and  Mesopotamia.  Inclusion  of  Egypt  likewise
remains a fringe phenomenon, limited to a few larger amalgamated units.212
In  Australia,  absorption  of  “Ancient  History”  into  Classics  is  the
dominant paradigm. The same is true of New Zealand. Canada is likewise
dotted with Classics programs.213

In  the  United  States,  although  there  is  no  hard  and  fast  rule,  private
institutions tend to subscribe to the Classics model. This is the case at Duke,
Johns  Hopkins,  Notre  Dame,  New  York  University,  Pennsylvania,
Princeton,  Stanford,  Washington  St.  Louis,  and  (at  least  technically)  Yale.
Some public universities fall in the same category, such as Rutgers (private
until 1945) and the Universities of Colorado, Oregon, and Texas.

At  the  same  time,  “Ancient  History”  is  frequently  practiced  in  History
programs,  even  in  cases  where  separate  Classics  programs  exist.  This  is
almost the norm at public universities, such as the Universities of Georgia,
Iowa,  North  Carolina,  Washington,  and  Wisconsin,  and  the  University  of
California  campuses  at  Berkeley,  Los  Angeles,  Riverside,  San  Diego,  and
Santa Barbara.

To complicate matters further, at several (mostly private) institutions, we
encounter  hybrid  arrangements  in  which  some  ancient  historians  hold
primary  appointments  in  Classics  and  others  in  History:  current  or  recent
examples  include  Brown,  Columbia,  Cornell,  Harvard,  Michigan,  Tulane,
the University of Chicago, and Vanderbilt.

Overall,  there  are  two  models  for  “Ancient  History”:  one  that  favors  the
detached display of Greece and Rome on its own little institutional pedestal,

and  another  that  opts  for  familiarizing  inclusion  into  more  or  less
Eurocentric  history.  These  options  overlap  in  practice  but  assign  different
weights  to  different  features.  Just  as  the  “Classics”  model  had  always
acknowledged  heritage  but  tends  to  consider  it  secondary,  the  “History”
model does the opposite. Yet even as their rationales differ, both share the
same narrow focus on Greece and Rome.

The  study  and  teaching  of  ancient  history  have  borne  the  cost.  As  a
fragment  of  a  formerly  unitary  Altertumswissenschaft,  “Ancient  History”
has inherited the boundaries the former had drawn. Formally set free from
the  overlordship  of  language  and  literature,  it  has  largely  retained  the
that  nationalist,  organicist
separation  from  other  ancient  histories
disciplinary ideals had so harshly imposed.

Recourse to the continuities of “Western Civilization” at least in theory
opened up space by allowing inclusion of ancient Egypt and West Asia, if
only in a narrowly circumscribed role as precursors of Greece, Rome, and
Europe.  Whether  this  may  count  as  (modest)  progress  is  debatable;  and  it
has in any case been confined to undergraduate teaching. In recent decades,
“Western  Civ”  courses  have  ceased  to  be  required  or  often  even  to  be
offered  at  many  US  colleges.  While  K–12  education  has  been  gravitating
toward a more global view of the remote past, academic “Ancient History”
has  remained  shackled  to  Greece  and  Rome:  entrenched  traditions  have
been  too  powerful  to  overcome.  In  most  research,  and  in  the  higher-level
instruction it informs, splendid isolation continues to be norm.

Our Shattered Past

“the

incredibly  meager

By  the  early  twentieth  century,  the  carving  up  of  the  past  had  become  so
idiosyncratically  elaborate,  or  elaborately  idiosyncratic,  that  it  took  an
eccentric  interloper  like  Oswald  Spengler  to  challenge  the  whole  system,
from
of
template”
that  classical
ancient/medieval/modern,  “the  venerable  prejudice  …
antiquity  is  intrinsically  close  to  us,”  and  the  arrogant  neglect  of  the
massive  histories  of  Asia  and  the  exclusion  of  American  cultures  because
“they  lack  connection  (with  what?)”  to  his  verdict  that  “the  word  Europe
should  be  excised  from  history”  and  his  demand  that  “India,  Babylon,

pointless

and

China,  Egypt,  the  Arabs  and  Mayan  culture”  be  granted  equal  standing
alongside the West.214

Heterodox visions of this kind ran counter to everything that had been so
painstakingly  constructed  over  generations.  Gone  were  the  days  when
scholars  could  roam  freely,  or  universal  historians  cast  their  nets  wide.
When  specialized  expertise  became  the  coin  of  the  academic  realm,
historians  failed  to  adapt  their  habits  in  ways  that  might  have  ensured
routinized  communication  with  specialists  in  cognate  areas.  Nor  did  they
team  up  to  produce  rich,  integrated  world  histories  across  subdisciplinary
divides.  Thus,  even  as  scholars  in  the  humanities  craved  the  rigor  and
cachet  of  the  exact  sciences,  they  shied  away  from  the  practices  that
propelled the latter: seminars did not evolve into labs. With few exceptions,
ancient  historians  remained  unimpressed  even  by
the  collaborative
arrangements of archaeological fieldwork and analysis.

Instead, as though the quote by Wolf at the beginning of this chapter had
served as their guiding principle, historians of all stripes doubled down on
maintaining  and  reproducing  boundaries.  The  fundamental  divide  was  set
between  white  and  nonwhite  (meshed  up  with  that  between  Christendom
and  others),  and  that  is  where  it  has  remained  ever  since.  After  endless
rhetoric about provincializing and decentering Europe and decolonizing the
study  of  the  past,  the  basic  format  of  History  as  an  academic  discipline
remains true to the vision of its nineteenth-century founders.

Separated from humanity’s deep past by the hard border of literacy—the
only  true  equalizer  that  cuts  across  racial  demarcations—the  historical
profession  in  the  first  instance  concerns  itself  with  Europe  and  its  settler
offshoots,  as  well  as  any  other  societies  that  came  into  sustained  contact
with Europeans during the last five hundred years or so.

Temporal  limits  are  somewhat  flexible:  while  the  (European)  Middle
Ages  are  invariably  part  of  History’s  core,  ancient  Greece  and  Rome  are
included  in  principle  but  may  be  claimed  by  (and  housed  in)  Classics
instead.  Barring  occasional  ad  hoc  or  ad  hominem  exceptions,  other
premodern  societies  are  routinely  excluded.  Thus,  later  imperial  China  or
Latin America are in whereas early China or the preconquest Americas are
usually out.

This  Eurocentric  consolidation  of  “History”  has  not  been  balanced  by
analogous consolidation of the study of premodern societies elsewhere, not
even of literate ones. What might at first blush seem like a plausible reason
—that the skill requirements for dealing with highly diverse early cultures
around  the  world  impelled  disciplinary  specialization—is  nothing  of  that
kind. Experts can readily coexist within larger disciplinary frameworks: the
field  of  “History,”  after  all,  accommodates  a  huge  variety  of  skill  sets
adapted to anything from medieval manuscripts to electronic media, as well
as  a  vast  array  of  different  scripts  and  languages.  Specialization  of  skills
and  knowledge  and  hyperfragmentary  disciplinization  are  not  the  same.
Nor, one might add, is it obvious that we should treat even specialization as
such as a virtue rather than a necessary evil that we might wish to transcend
as well as embrace.

When it comes to ancient history, peculiarities surrounding the study of
Greece  and  Rome  have  played  a  decisive  role  in  shaping  the  dynamics  of
fragmentation  and  differential  valorization.  One  factor  is  the  holistic
conception  of  “GreeceandRome”  that  by  its  very  nature  required  strict
spatiotemporal  delimitation.  Another one is  the  racial  divide that fractures
the  study  of  ancient  societies  between  its  most-studied  bits—Greece  and
Rome—and  all  the  others.  A  third  one  is  the  abiding  confusion  about  the
location  of  Greek  and  Roman  history  between  Altertumswissenschaft  and
now  Classics  on  the  one  hand  and  History  on  the  other,  which  weakens
professional communication and solidarity among students of antiquity. In
all these  respects,  the  erstwhile  elevation of Greece and Rome  has had an
outsized impact on our engagement with the ancient world as a whole.

All  this  has  created  a  bewildering  jumble.  The  history  of  Greece  and
Rome,  also  known  as  the  “real”  “Ancient  History,”  may  be  domiciled  in
Classics,  History,  or  both.  The  study  of  ancient  North  Africa  and  most  of
ancient  Asia  is  either  organized  in  miniclones  of  Altertumswissenschaft
such  as  Egyptology  (which  separates  the  dynastic  period  from  the
remainder  of  that  country’s  history)  or  subsumed  within  more  vertically
integrated  area  studies  fields  such  as  South  or  East  Asian  Studies.
Alongside  Aboriginal  Australia,  most  of  precolonial  Africa,  and  all  of  the
preconquest Americas—insofar as they receive any attention at all—tend to

be  relegated  to  Anthropology.  So  are  pre/nonliterate  societies  in  general,
unless they happen to end up in dedicated programs of prehistory or “Early
History,” as they sometimes do in Europe. That, at any rate, is the situation
in  Western  and  Western-influenced  academic  systems.  In  China,  by
contrast,  the  principal  divide  between  Chinese  history  and  world  history
tends to render the latter more inclusive.

With ancient history divvied up among Anthropology, Prehistory, Early
History,  Classics,  History,  Egyptology,  Assyriology,  Hittitology,  Iranian
Studies,  South,  Southeast,  East  and  Central  Asian  Studies,  and  however
many  other  academic  units  and  programs  there  might  be,  the  chances  of
interpreting  any  meaningful  broader  patterns  and
perceiving  and
connections, contrasts, and similarities are about as slim as they can be. It is
as if in the West, the study of ancient history had been organized to ensure
the highest possible degree of fragmentation and self-isolation, maximizing
its  distance  from  earlier  more  cosmopolitan  perspectives.  As  a  result,
understanding the shared foundations of our world is so much harder than it
needs to be.

While we cannot undo the academic maneuvering that has produced this
outcome,  it  is  nonetheless  imperative  to  be  aware  of  it.  The  history  of
scholarship tells us that things did not have to turn out that way but did so
only because of what once made sense to (some) people, many generations
ago.  Insofar  as  we  can  discern  a  logic  behind  the  divisions  those  actors
wrought, it is hardly one we would wish to subscribe to today, steeped as it
was in Romantic special pleading, Eurocentrism, and racism. Acquiescence
to  the  status  quo  may  be  the  path  of  least  resistance  but  simply  means
surrender to these multiple birth defects.

There  is  reason  for  hope.  It  is  not  a  law  of  nature  that  once  put  in  place,
particular  forms  of  specialization  must  always  remain  stable.  The  contrast
between the humanities and the life sciences is instructive. As James Turner
puts  it,  the  latter  have  been  “nimbler  than  humanists  in  adapting
disciplinary  boundaries  to  emerging  problems,”  even  as  globalization
challenges  the  former’s  traditional  narratives.  “Life  scientists  reorganize
themselves,  as  research  agenda  develop,  into  units  that  come  and  go:

microbiology,  molecular  biology,  biochemistry,  biophysics,  biomedical
informatics,  neurosciences,  behavioral  biology.…  Meanwhile,  English  and
history  departments  soldier  stolidly  on,  muskets  on  their  shoulders.  But
ultimately  stasis  will  not  serve.  Sooner  or  later,  the  humanities  disciplines
must shift their shapes, even drastically shrink in number.”215

This  inertia  has  been  particularly  detrimental  to  the  study  of  antiquity,
riven as it is by the proliferation of academic microcosms. Legacy traditions
profoundly  shape  modes  of  engagement.  These  traditions  are  deeply
entrenched  and  will  not  simply  buckle  under  the  weight  of  their  own
contradictions. But if a globalized ancient history is our goal, we must find
ways to transcend them in order to move forward.

3

Going Global

WHAT WE SHOULD DO

It is possible to get an idea of the whole from a portion, but impossible to acquire knowledge
and accurate understanding.

—POLYBIUS, HISTORIES1

Putting Ancient History Back Together Again

In the first chapter, I argued that those of our ancestors whom we to like call
“ancient” laid the foundations for the world we inhabit now. They did so for
better  or  worse—creating  novel  opportunities,  choking  off  older  lifeways,
and nudging their descendants away from alternative paths of development
in  a  heady  mix  of  variability  and  convergence,  of  contingencies  and
persistent  trends,  of  roads  not  taken  and  the  growing  dominance  of  a
particular bundle of norms, practices, expectations, and realities. In the end,
this process was global in nature: although it did not unfold everywhere or
at the same time, it slowly but surely swallowed up our entire species.

A process that was global in both scope and impact ought to be studied
and  taught  in  ways  that  are  true  to  that  scope  and  impact.  This  certainly
does  not  mean  that  history  always  ought  to  be  approached  on  a  planetary
scale: that is just one option among many, and often not the most useful one
or  even  unfeasible.  What  it  does  mean,  however,  is  that  we  must  seek  to
take  proper  account  of  broader  connections,  patterns,  and  dynamics  no
matter how small and local the cases we study happen to be. We can do this
in ways that suit a wide variety of needs and preferences.

As we saw in the previous chapter, historians have for many generations
moved further and further away from that premise. Shattering the one world
they were charged with understanding, they carved up its history into more
and  more  fragments,  conjuring  up  smaller  and  smaller  worlds,  truncating
and trapping the human experience in ever-tighter cages. Worse still, for a
long time Western academics focused on gilding one particular cage (which
housed the Greeks and Romans) at the expense of others. After a quarter of
a  millennium  of  these  efforts,  the  manner  we  view,  study,  and  teach  our
shared foundations has come to be seriously out of whack.

is  straightforward.

Changing track might seem like a revolution but is merely a restoration:
a move to put the past back together again. The intellectual imperative for
restoration
foundational  developments  were
If
(increasingly) global in scale, why hack them into little pieces and box them
up? How can we hope to understand them if we do that? And why would
we  choose  to  imagine  that  “Ancient  History”  somehow  “belonged”  to  the
ancient Greeks and Romans, perhaps with some of their neighbors thrown
in to soften the edges?

As  we  have  seen,  escalating  compartmentalization  and  the  enduring
privileging  of  Greek  and  Roman  culture  were  driven  by  the  interplay  of
academic  dynamics—such  as  the  gravitational  pull  of  specialization  and
discipline formation—and evolving intellectual currents from scholasticism,
Renaissance  humanism,  and  Romantic  antiquity-worship  to  Eurocentrism
and white supremacy. At the end of the day, the way we approach ancient
history is shaped not by necessity or some rational guiding principle but by
the deadweight of the more recent past.

This  deadweight  has  failed  to  shift  even  as  we  have  learned  so  much
more  about  so many  different early societies  around the  globe  and textual
records are no longer the only game in history town. Instead of expanding
our  gaze,  our  engagement  with  ancient  history  remains  imbalanced  and
obstructed by barriers erected when so much less was known and proudly
exclusionary tastes were so much more acceptable.

This  final  point  brings  us  to  another  and  equally  important  reason  for
embracing  restorative  change.  However  we  label  it,  ancient  history  is

studied  by  the  living  and  for  the  living.  However  much  we  might  respect
our distant ancestors for who they were—and safeguard their remains—our
engagement with their history is of our own world, as it must needs be.

Once  again,  conventional approaches  to  ancient  history  are  ill-suited  to
this task. While this problem is by no means limited to any one country, the
case  of  the  United  States  illustrates  it  most  dramatically.  According  to
census  data  (and  categories)  from  2021,  close  to  three-fifths  of  the  US
population identifies as white, a little less than one-fifth as Hispanic, a little
more  than  one-seventh  as  Black,  six  percent  as  Asian,  and  1.6  percent  as
Indigenous American or Pacific Islander, alongside 3 percent of mixed race
—a share minimized by reporting preferences and in truth several times as
large.2

What  is  that  society’s  “ancient  history”?  In  terms  of  Americans’
ancestry, it is obviously global in scope. There is nothing to suggest that it
should  primarily,  let  alone  wholly,  be  defined  as  the  history  of  ancient
Greeks and Romans, or indeed as any one slice of the world’s early history.
It  would  not  even  do  to  consider  Greece  and  Rome  the  most  appropriate
ancient  history  for  the  single  largest  (and  historically  dominant)  group,
those  descending  from  white  and  therefore  European  settlers.  After  all,
more than half of US whites claim ancestors originating from countries that
had  never  been  part  of  the  Roman  empire,  let  alone  Hellenic  in  any
meaningful  sense,  such  as  Ireland,  Scandinavia,  (most  of)  Germany,  and
Slavic Central and Eastern Europe.3

US citizens and residents from Latin America hail from areas associated
with  Mexica,  Maya,  Quechua,  Taíno,  and  a  host  of  other  precolonial
cultures.  The  range  of  what  might  be  classified  as  “ancient”  societies  is
wide,  reaching  from  the  Eurochronologically  “ancient”  civilizations  of
Olmec, Teotihuacan, Maya, Caral, Chavín de Huántar, Nazca, and Moche to
the  later  Toltecs,  Aztecs,  Wari,  Tiwanaku,  Chimu,  and  Inca,  alongside
innumerable smaller-scale societies.

Black  US  Americans  predominantly  originate  from  West  Africa.
Developmentally “ancient” societies range from the early iron-working Nok
culture  in  what  is  now  central  Nigeria  and  the  urban  communities  of  the

Niger basin to the first Ghana empire, Takrur, and their regional successors,
as well as the kingdom of Kongo.

Asian Americans face an embarrassment of riches, represented not only
by the deep histories of China and India but also by Yamato-era Japan and
the  Korean  polities  of  Silla,  Paekche,  and  Koguryo  alongside  emerging
societies in South-East Asia and elsewhere.

Meanwhile, the most immediate “ancient history” of the United States is
provided  by  Indigenous  traditions.  The  early  mound-building  cultures
known as Hopewell were Eurochronologically “ancient” and closely linked
to later formations such as the Mississippian culture that produced Cahokia
and  many  other  formidable  settlements.  Farther  west,  we  encounter
Ancestral Pueblo and various canyon cultures. Pacific coastal societies are
noteworthy  for  building  up  social  complexity  by  means  of  aquatic
adaptations. Foragers roamed the Great Plains. Rich diversity characterized
the terrain of the future United States.

All  told,  the  current  inhabitants  of  that  country  are  linked  to  an
extraordinary variety of different ancient histories—arguably more so than
those of any other modern society. This abundance ought to be a matter of
pride, not an opportunity for erasure. If the United States is not an obvious
place  to  acknowledge,  explore,  and  celebrate  the  global  nature  of  ancient
history, it is hard to see what other country ever would be.

The inevitable retort that regardless of people’s provenance, it is cultural
impact  that  matters  most  and  deserves  to  be  put  center  stage  is  as
condescending  as  it  is  ignorant.  Of  course  there  is  no  denying  that  settler
colonialism elevated Greco-Roman influences to a disproportionate degree.
But can the fact that other traditions—whether those ancestral to the region
or  imported  from  other  parts  of  the  world—were  thereby  pushed  aside
justify  elision?  Ancient  history  is  what  once  was,  not  what  the  winners
chose  to  prioritize.  Besides,  no  matter  how  powerful  certain  hegemonic
traditions  may  be,  it  would  be  bizarre  to  assume  that  they  somehow
rendered all others moot. In reality, the latter continue to shape this highly
diverse nation.

This is not a matter of essentializing descent or tribalizing the citizenry:
it is about recognizing the richness of historical lineages and diasporas and

giving  it  its  due.  What  are  the  alternatives?  In  this  context,  the  only
rationale  for  upholding  the  fiction  of  a  narrowly  demarcated  “ancient
history”  that  is  “classical”  in  nature  depends  on  adherence  to  premises  of
white supremacy, which, while undoubtedly central to the standard model,
are hardly worth maintaining. E pluribus unum surely does not call for the
elevation  of  one  sliver  of  ancient  history  to  canonical  status.  Instead,
pluralistic  unity  requires  the  restoration  of  antiquity  to  its  true  global  self.
The ancient histories of different groups must be allowed to contribute to a
foundation story for everyone.

This  does  not  merely  apply  to  parts  of  the  world  that  have  been  as
heavily  transformed  by  recent  immigration  flows  as  North  America:  the
need  for  inclusionary  ancient  history  must  not  be  misconstrued  as  a
quintessentially American concern, only to be dismissed elsewhere. Even if
other  societies  wished  to  lay  claim  to  (comparatively)  more  cohesive  or
restricted foundational pasts, they would nevertheless be unable to escape a
world  that  has  become  increasingly  interconnected  and  globalized.  In  that
regard, we are indeed “all Americans now,” as Le Monde proclaimed  after
9/11. Understanding our shared roots has become more important than ever.
The foundational history model actively promotes that aspiration, taking us
a step closer to what Sebastian Conrad calls “the utopian promise of global
history: to turn us into citizens of the world.”4

The standard model, by contrast, has little to contribute to this end. Take,
for  instance,  the  German  “ancient”  (i.e.,  Greco-Roman)  historian  Michael
Sommer’s recent plea for renewed engagement with—we need to retain the
German article for proper emphasis—“the antiquity” (“die Antike”), which
just  as  in  the  days  of  Wolf  is  exclusively  equated  with  “GreeceandRome”
and  the  study  of  Greek  and  Latin.  Sommer  identifies  three  main  benefits:
the promotion of a shared European heritage as an “identity anchor” for the
present; enlightening engagement with the alien-ness of early societies; and
the chance to learn lessons about the reversibility of cultural integration.5

Two  of  these,  however,  should  strongly  encourage  us  to  branch  out  far
beyond  Greece  and  Rome  instead  of  doubling  down  on  the  status  quo.
Other early cultures may well seem even less familiar to Western eyes than
those two; integration and retrenchment have always been widespread. All

that  remains  is  a  Eurocentric  discourse  that  mobilizes  the  past  to  address
current  anxieties.  This  makes  sense  only  if  we  take  proper  account  of
traditions  associated  with  immigration  from  other  spheres  as  well  as  all
traditions’  embeddedness  in  the  global.  What  this  calls  for  is  a  much
broader ancient history, not more of the same.

There is no denying a simple fact: global ancient history, conceived of as
foundational history, is “our” ancient history, whoever and wherever we are.
Just as we all share African roots, all of us are now the heirs, or captives, of
that global ancient history, of the world it created. There is no other kind.

An Embarrassment of Riches

How can we broaden our understanding of these foundations? There is no
need  to  reinvent  the  wheel.  A  rich  literature  exists  to  guide  us  along  the
many different ways of globalizing the study of history, ready to be applied
to  the  ancient  world—and,  it  must  be  stressed,  has  already  begun  to  be
applied.  In  principle,  it  would  take  a  whole  team  of  area  specialists  to
survey  these  promising  efforts  in  sufficient  detail  to  do  them  justice.  My
objective  is  more  modest  but  no  less  important:  to  sketch  out  the  main
approaches and consider their payoffs and drawbacks.

In  a  second  step,  we  need  to  confront  a  crucial  issue:  the  trade-offs
between technical expertise and greater breadth. We will see that far from
diluting  traditional  strengths  or  laying  waste  to  established  scholarship,  as
traditionalists  might  fear  or  choose  to  claim,  open-ended  broadening  is
perfectly  compatible  with  the  preservation  of  academic  standards  and  the
wealth of historical knowledge that has been so laboriously acquired. At the
same time, we need to come to terms with the fact that conventional norms
of  academic  industry  are  likely  to  obstruct  that  broadening,  and  explore
solutions to that problem.

But  let  us  start  with  hope  and  ambition.  Attempts  to  globalize  history
stand out for their sheer variety, the breadth and pluralism of perspectives,
approaches,  and  methods.  This  variety  is  of  critical  importance  in
determining the extent to which the globalization of history requires change
in  established  practices  or  adoption  of  particular  strategies.  This  varies
hugely  depending  on  what  exactly  it  is  we  hope  to  accomplish.

Consequently,  globalization  may  affect  existing  modes  of  teaching  and
research  very  moderately  or  to  a  dramatic  degree.  It  is  essential  to  realize
that  the  globalizing  agenda  is  much  more  flexible  and  eclectic  and  thus
more widely applicable than one might expect.6

Global history is not a theory or a method but a process. Nor is it limited
to specific subjects or questions, let alone beholden to a particular style or
ideology.  In  this  respect,  it  fits  well  into  what  Lynn  Hunt  has  termed
“mindful  eclecticism,”  historians’
from  debates  about
methodology  and  their  embrace  of  pluralism,  fostering  a  discipline  that
comfortably  accommodates  social-scientific
investigations  alongside
humanistic preoccupations with meaning.7

turn  away

In  Conrad’s  words,  global  history  is  “a  form  of  historical  analysis  in
which  phenomena,  events,  and  processes  are  placed  in  global  contexts”—
which can be done in any number of ways. Similarly, for James Belich and
his associates, global history represents “the pursuit of significant historical
problems over time, space, and specialism” and “seeks answers to the same
question in multiple sites.”8

It is for those reasons that it has a “polemical dimension” insofar as “it
constitutes  an  assault  on  many  forms  of  container-based  paradigms,  chief
among them national history.” In this regard, it aligns with a broader trend,
the  retreat  from—or  rather  the  denaturalizing  of—the  template  of  the
“nation”  created  in  eighteenth-  and  nineteenth-century  Europe.  This  is  a
critical  prerequisite,  given  that  “[t]he  compartmentalization  of  historical
reality—into  national  and  world  history,  into  history  and  area  studies—
means that parallels and entanglements cannot come into focus.”9

Global  history  unifies  and  decenters:  as  Richard  Drayton  and  David
Motadel put it, unlike earlier universal historians who employed the history
of  others  to  frame  their  own,  “the  enterprise  of  global  historians  in  the
twenty-first century is our attempt to map the human past from and for the
view of humanity as a whole.” This ambition joins intellectual and political
goals: to improve our understanding of history, and to generate “a sense of a
shared  global  humanity”  as  a  counterweight  to  parochialism  and  populist
national narratives.10

Franz  Fillafer  astutely  identifies  four  means  by  which  globalizing
approaches help “soothe the woes of the discipline” of doing history today:
acknowledge  a “multipolar  world order”; “cater to  an increasingly  diverse
student body”; “defend the relevance of academic historiography amid the
democratization  and  privatization  of  citizens’  relationship  with  the  past  in
pluricultural  societies”;  and  mitigate  or  overcome  hyperspecialist
fragmentation of fields.11

All  of  these  points  are  just  as  relevant  for  our  engagement  with  early
history. After all, quasi-national templates have been extended deep into the
past,  whether  in  replication  of  modernizing  historiography  of  more  recent
periods or to connect ancient histories to modern national histories, or both.
Spatiotemporal  specialization  has  solidified  such  retrojections
into
persistent norms and practices. Thus, ancient historians stand to gain just as
much from globalizing approaches that counter these trends as others.12

Such  approaches  come  in  different  flavors.  What  may  well  be  the  most
ambitious  scheme  to  date  derives  from  Diego  Olstein’s  taxonomy  of
comparing,  connecting,  conceptualizing, and contextualizing,  which yields
fully  a  dozen  varieties:  comparative  and  relational  history;  international,
transnational, and oceanic histories; civilizational or world-systems analysis
and  historical  sociology;  and  global  history,  the  history  of  globalization,
world history, and big history (from the Big Bang). All of these differ and
intersect  in  terms  of  units  of  analysis,  temporal  scope,  disciplinary
affiliation,  epistemic  approaches,  and  use  of  evidence.  Many,  if  not
necessarily all of them, are suitable for ancient historians.13

I mention this primarily to convey a sense of the richness on offer. There
is  no  need  to  ponder  this  or  rival  schemes  in  any  detail—Jürgen
Osterhammel,  for  example,  comes  up  with  his  own  six-part  formula.
Simplicity is key. For present purposes, I use a more basic typology of four
versions,  what  I  call  contextualizing,  connective,  comparative,  and  world
histories.14

Contextualization refers above all to awareness of larger contexts. Such
awareness  serves  as  a  framing  device  that  informs  the  historian’s  work,
which  does  not  need  to  focus  on  connections  or  involve  comparisons,  let

alone  aspire  to  a  global  scope.  Anything  that  transcends  the  local  and
particular  falls  in  that  category.  In  a  sense,  there  is  no  history  that  goes
beyond mere description that does not operate in this way: any concepts are
inevitably rooted in a variety of concrete cases that frame and contextualize
any  one  of  them.  The  globalizing  dimension  of  this  framing  derives  from
awareness,  from  making  contextualization  explicit  and  from  actively
seeking it out.

Connectivity is commonly defined in spatial terms, as observers focus on
connections  between  cases  and  interpret  history  at  least  in  part  as  the
outcome  of  such  interactions.  Yet  equal  weight  is  due  to  diachronic
transfers and influences. As a result, very different kinds of work fall under
this  rubric.  What  unites  them  is  that  they  are  all  translocal,  transregional,
and often transcultural, and therefore qualify as globalizing approaches.

Comparison considers the properties of two or more cases, and may but
need not overlap with connectivity studies depending on whether cases are
related.  Comparison  is  concerned  with  similarities,  differences,  and
causation.  While  it  plays  an  essential  role  in  identifying  overarching
patterns and dynamics, for most historians its greatest value lies in helping
them understand individual cases.

World  history—history  on  a  planetary  scale—is  the  most  ambitious
variant,  whether  it  aims  to  be  universal  or  more  narrowly  tracks  specific
phenomena  across  the  globe.  As  Jerry  Bentley  puts  it  in  his  paper  on  the
“tasks  of  world  history,”  it  overcomes  traditional  preoccupations  with  the
cultural  distinctions  and  experiences  of  particular  societies  by  opening  up
the study of larger processes that connect and transcend such societies.15

Broadly  speaking,  and  especially  when  it  comes  to  premodern  history,
to  ever-larger  scope.  Whereas
this  four-part  sequence  corresponds
contextualization  is  generally  unencumbered  by  specific  expectations  of
scale,  connections  tend  to  be  bounded  in  space  and/or  time,  comparison
may but need not roam across history, and planetary histories must take in
the whole world.

Even  more  broadly  speaking,  this  sequence  also  reflects  growing
difficulty.  Whereas  framing  can  be  quite  modest,  comparative  analysis
tends to involve more serious epistemological complications than much of

the  connectivist  work  that  is  being  done,  and  planetary  approaches  pose
challenges  of  coverage  and  expertise  that  strain  against  conventional
academic practices and expectations.

But there is no hierarchy: all versions are equally of value. Flexibility is
of the essence: globalized history may but need not focus on exchanges or
study processes of globalization; it may but need not involve comparisons;
it  may  but  need  not  tackle  big  questions.  It  accommodates  established
modes  of  scholarship  and  does  not  commit  historians  to  any  particular
scope or method.

Contextualization

This  lack  of  prescriptions  applies  most  of  all  to  basic  contextualization.
While the very term “global” may imply a large scale, global history does
not have to be megasized. Drayton and Motadel put it well: “Global history
has  never  been  a  demand  that  historians  only  pay  attention  to  ‘big’
transnational  phenomena.  Its  more  pertinent  meaning  is  a  change  in  the
explanans  of  history:  a  new  sensitivity  to  historical  agents,  forces,  and
factors at scales above and below those of the nation or region.” The goal is
to  draw  attention  to  “the  interdependence  of  the  scales  of  space—village,
province, nation, region, and world—and time—days, decades, centuries—
through which we explore and explain the past.”16

When we speak of global(izing) history, we may refer to the perspective
of the historian or the scale of processes. Those are not the same. In John
Darwin’s  words,  this  “is  not  a  historical  programme,  still  less  a  uniform
approach to the history of the world. Its appeal and its value lie precisely in
the  multiple  vistas  it  opens  up,  in  the  connections  it  suggests,  in  the
questions it asks.” In the end, as Hunt reminds us, “The scale of the study
depends on the question to be answered.”17

This  means  that  all  manner  of  work  that  does  not  expressly  explore
global  horizons  may  count  as  a  contribution  to  global  history.  Conrad
deems  this  apparent  paradox  “less  absurd  than  it  seems.”  Drayton  and
Motadel  agree  when  they  note  that  “the  global  frame  of  reference  is  not
always the  most relevant  one.… To be a global historian is  often  to  study
very  specific  places,  institutions,  and  people  and  not  to  pretend  to  any

general or generalizable claim.” Nor, as Geraldine Heng insists specifically
with  reference  to  premodern  global  studies,  do  globalizing  efforts  call  for
“the elision or the relegation to unimportance of the local” or of the study of
specific “languages, literatures, and histories, including microhistories.”18

What they do call for, however, is awareness of wider contexts, ideally at
every  step  of  the  way:  when  designing  projects,  executing  them,  and
communicating  the  results  to  peers,  students,  and  other  constituencies.
Mindful  contextualization  is  likely  to  affect  all  of  these  processes,  and  is
essential in conveying insights to wider audiences both within and beyond
academia. As already noted, flexibility is integral to this approach. In some
cases, depending on the nature of the project, this kind of framing may not
even  be  feasible  or  worthwhile.  Yet  even  then  the  very  act  of  asking
whether  it  might  be  is  not  in  vain:  self-reflection  helps  build  a  different
mindset.

In practical terms, contextualization arguably works best whenever some
form of broader connections is reflected in particular cases. This underlies
the  concept  of  the  “glocal,”  of  sustained  interactions  between  local  and
global  whereby  widely  shared  features  are  adapted  to  local  conditions.
Although this may be particularly relevant for the modern capitalist world,
Conrad maintains that it “can be applied to earlier periods, though in such
cases the degree of integration may be rather weak.” This caveat highlights
another benefit of this perspective: how would we know if or how or how
much a particular case is affected by wider influences unless we ask those
questions and explore them beyond conventional specialist boundaries?19

Asking these questions ought to become just as common as the policing
of  established  academic  standards—accuracy,  precision,  adherence  to
disciplinary conventions, and so on. It must not be an optional add-on, nor a
smoke screen to protect the status quo, deploying globalizing rhetoric while
carrying  on  with  business  as  usual.  It  is  only  through  the  routinization  of
posing such questions that incremental change becomes possible: regardless
of  how  often  they  lead  nowhere,  the  sum  total  of  experimenting  with
broader frames and perspectives will inevitably be broader perspectives and
integration overall.

Communication  is  a  vital  element  of  this  process,  and  this  is  where
teaching  comes  in.  In  the  classroom,  and  likewise  in  the  public-facing
dissemination  of  knowledge  from  social  media,  blogs,  and  podcasts  to
traditional  news  media  and  other  commercial  venues,  contextualizing
framing holds considerable promise: after all, nonexpert audiences want to
know  how  something  relates  to  larger  issues  and  dynamics.  Ideally,  this
encourages  a  feedback  loop,  a  virtuous  circle  from  contextualization  in
communication  to  contextualization  from  the  beginning,  in  terms  of
research design and execution.

While this may seem rather vague, there is merit in this indeterminacy.
In  practice,  most  ancient  historians  will  never  become  committed
comparativists or embark on projects for which connectivity offers the most
suitable  principal  framing  device.  Yet  all  of  them  ought  to  be,  and  in  fact
are, able to participate in the broadening of horizons, at low cost and with
likely  payoffs  in  intellectual  gain  and  outreach.  At  a  minimum,  routinized
contextualization  helps  overcome  hyperspecialist  siloing  and  related
obstacles  to  accessibility  and  understanding,  such  as  jargon  and  tediously
self-referential modes of engagement.

Connectivity

temporally)

Connective histories go beyond contextualization by centering relationships
between  cases.  This  focus  “elucidates  how  history  is  made  through  the
interactions  of  geographically
separate  historical
(or
communities.” Connections manifest in time and space. Temporal lineages
have  long  been  a  staple  of  the  historian’s  trade,  above  all  in  tracking
cultural  features  such  as  languages,  beliefs,  institutions,  technologies,
styles,  and  symbols.  Emergent  genetic  history  has  more  recently  been
adding yet another layer to this enterprise: it not only allows us to explore
human  experiences  that  are  not  captured  by  conventional  records  but
reveals  a  complexity  of  migrations  and  mixtures  that  is  at  odds  with
virtually every ancestry myth in existence and underscores the remarkable
homogeneity of our species.20

Even so, recent scholarship suggests that the pursuit of such connections
does  not  normally  earn  a  high  profile  among  globalizing  approaches.  The

nature of modern globalization is a likely reason for this, encouraging us as
it does to cast connectivity more as a spatial than a temporal phenomenon.
Besides,  studying  the  impact  of  earlier  periods  on  later  ones  might  more
readily  be  regarded  as  business  as  usual.  More  specifically,  the  latter  can
more  easily  be  aligned  with  national  history  templates  that  privilege
lineages over hybridity and are out of favor in globalizing circles. Thus, the
“Western Civilization” model that links “GreeceandRome” to later Europe
would not normally be considered an example of global history, even as it
diachronically connects cultural “worlds.”

is  concerned  with

Giving  short(er)  shrift  to  temporal  connectivity  represents  a  loss:  since
that  dimension,
much  of  what  historians  do
acknowledging  the  globalizing  qualities  of  such  work  would  boost  the
standing  of  global  history.  Just  as  with  contextualization,  this  approach
benefits  from  explicit  problematization.  For  any  given  case,  it  is  just  as
challenging to account for the impact of temporal transfers as for those of a
spatial  nature.  Any  periodization,  including  the  concept  of  “foundational
history,”  is  predicated  on  shifts  in  the  balance  of  continuities  and
discontinuities over time.

That said, the study of spatial connectivities aligns particularly well with
global  history’s  emphasis  on  processes  and  dynamics  that  are  supralocal,
supraregional,  and  supracultural  in  character.  In  recent  years,  this  type  of
work has conspicuously gained in popularity among students of the ancient
world. To be sure, they had long embraced the twin premises that no society
exists in isolation and that mobility and exchange are truly ancient features.
Now  the  question  is  to  what extent  they  are  ready  to  deploy  connectivity-
centered approaches as a “method of space enlargement” that transcends or
even erases boundaries and defines larger units of analysis at the expense of
political, linguistic, or other demarcations.21

Much  of  this  is  driven  by  interest  in  interconnectivity  on  increasing
scales,  in  globalization  processes  “by  which  the  world  becomes  more
interconnected  and  more
instance,
“globalism exists as a dynamic: as forces pushing towards the formation of
larger scales of relation.”22

interdependent.”  For  Heng,  for

By  now  it  is  almost  banal  to  observe  that  such  notions  are  heavily
indebted  to  modern  processes  of  planet-wide  integration.  Nation-centered
templates had a head start in leaving their mark on modern historiography,
preceding the first great wave of globalization that commenced in the 1870s
and boosted attention to connectivities. The big globalization push since the
1980s provided renewed impetus for work on that topic, especially as it also
helped fill the ideological vacuum left by the end of the Cold War and most
recently  has  meshed  well  with  growing  concerns  about  environmental
stresses on a planetary scale. The trend has been away from nationalism and
“Western Civilization” and toward cosmopolitanism and interdependence.23
Critics  might  point  out  that  globalizing  approaches  that  prioritize  the
spatial  expansion  of  interconnectivity  suffer  from  the  twin  problems  of
privileging  processes  that  reflect  contemporary  concerns  and  of  allowing
the experience of European colonizers to play an outsized role in narratives
of global integration. These problems, as Fillafer observes, date back to the
Enlightenment  history  of
the  eighteenth  century  “making  growing
geographical  and  material  interrelatedness  the  surreptitious  guiding  theme
and  trajectory  behind  world  history,”  whereby  “European  conquest,
commerce, and culture were taken to reunite the previously disjointed zones
of the planet by co-opting them to the core of world history.”24

Even  so,  historiography  that  foregrounds  connective  elements  is  not
inherently  bound  to  a  particular  period  or  centered  on  a  particular
experience.  It  can  shed  light  on  any  environment  in  which  communities
interacted  at  scale.  Associations  with  modernity  or  Eurocentrism  need  not
be  a  concern  for  premodern  historians  of  connectivity,  at  least  as  long  as
they  resist  facile  back-projections  from  more  recent  developments.  Unlike
modern  globalization  stories,  the  study  of  ancient  connectivities  is  not
centered  on  any  specific  part  of  the  world.  Other  than  that,  Tamar  Hodos
stresses  that  premodern  globalizations  differed  from  modern  processes  in
terms of “scale and, perhaps, intensity,” but not in kind.25

In  large  parts  of  the  world,  integration  had  already  occurred  on  a
considerable  scale  by  the  first  millennium  BCE.  While  “world-systems”
approaches  have  on  occasion  been  pushed  back  as  much  as  5,000  years,
Philippe Beaujard more conservatively argues that from the early Iron Age

By

transcending

onward, Afoeurasia contained three major world-systems. Centered on the
Mediterranean, South Asia, and East Asia, by the beginning of the Common
Era  they  had  merged  into  a  single  meaningfully  interdependent  system.
Other parts of the globe underwent analogous processes at their own pace.26
transnational
conceptions  of  history  create  friction  in  dealing  with  the  recent  past.
Conversely,  such  conceptions  are  far  less  challenging  when  applied  to
earlier periods; unless, that is, we continue to fall for projections of national
templates  onto  premodern  societies.  Thus,  far  from  being  a  corollary  of
modernity, connective histories are if anything a more natural and suitable
mode of engaging with the ancient world.27

traditional  national  boundaries,

In practical terms, the study of spatial connectivities in the more distant past
tends to focus to a considerable extent on the material record, providing a
welcome  means  of  rebalancing  text-centered  discourses  that  can  obstruct
globalizing  perspectives.  This  increased  reliance  on  archaeology  raises
challenges  of  interpretation:  whereas  the  physical  transfer  of  objects  can
often  be  tracked  with  considerable  precision,  the  context  and  meaning  of
such  processes  become  the  object  of  open-ended  exploration  that  among
other things calls for consideration of comparative cases (as discussed in the
following  section).  This  in  turn  adds  a  whole  new  layer  of  connectivity
beyond—and  potentially  far  beyond—that  embodied  by  the  transfers
themselves.28

Moreover,  connections  may  be  indirect  in  nature,  created  by  shared
influences  rather  than  transfers.  Relevant  examples  from  the  premodern
period include the impact of climate events, pandemics, and the movement
of  steppe  populations  on  diverse  and  otherwise  separate  societies.  This
perspective  has  recently  been  gaining  a  lot  of  traction  among  ancient
historians, driven by growing interest in science-based approaches and the
role played by nonsedentary actors.29

Yet  for  all  these  payoffs,  connectivity-focused  work  also  suffers  from
significant limitations. It tends to privilege simultaneity, aiming more often
than  not  to  “relate  things  at  the  same  time.”  For  premodern  history,  this
perspective  can  be  exclusionary,  separating  as  it  does  the  Americas  from

Afroeurasia.  While  this  might  seem  a  small  price  to  pay,  such  separations
are  in  fact  of  critical  importance  in  determining  who  is  “in”  and  who  is
“out” of “ancient history.”30

Moreover,  recent  historiography  in  a  globalizing  vein  has  not  only
shown  a  marked  preference  for  synchronicities  but  also  for  ideas  of
exogenous  causation.  The  latter  arises  from  opposition  to  models  of
endogenous causation within particular cases that appear to isolate or even
essentialize  them:  the  more  interconnected  cases  can  be  shown  to  be,  the
easier it becomes to ascribe developments to interactions among cases.31

That  need  not  be  a  problem,  even  though  it  serves  to  contribute  to  the
relative  marginalization  of  comparative  perspectives  that  rely  on  treating
cases  primarily  as  discrete  units  rather  than  as  the  manifestation  of
interaction  between  cases.  The  main  challenge  lies  in  ascertaining  the
relative  weight  of  such  interactions  and  exchanges  in  producing  observed
outcomes.  The  point is  not  merely  that (especially)  in  premodern settings,
globalizing effects “manifested diversely, unevenly, and differentially from
place to place.” The intensity of connections is the key issue. What matters
most is not the mere presence of connections but the degree and impact of
linkages, an apparent truism that can nevertheless get shunted aside in the
hunt for connections for the sake of establishing connections.32

Bentley  sounds  a  much-needed  warning:  “We  can  all  agree  that  it  is
‘interesting’ to examine contacts between societies and civilizations; but a
strong case for global perspectives depends on a convincing demonstration
of  the  function  of  those  contacts.  Declaring  a  ‘system’  of  relationships  is
easier than demonstrating one, far less one with explanatory power, outside
the now familiar language of trade or economic penetration.”33

In  antiquity,  only  certain  types  of  connections  were  strong  and  durable
enough  to  create  recognizable  systems.  Belich  and  associates  survey  the
different degrees of connectivity—contact, interaction (regular contact, e.g.,
luxury  trade),  circulation  (bulk  trade),  integration  (establishing  mutual
interdependence)—which  were  in  turn  linked  to  different  vectors—
diffusion,  outreach,  dispersal,  expansion  (from  a  center),  attraction  (vice
versa),  setting  up  a  taxonomy  reminiscent  of  the  four  types  of  world-
systems  (embodied  by  bulk  goods,  prestige  goods,  political/military  and

information networks) envisioned by theorists Christopher Chase-Dunn and
Thomas Hall.34

The lesson is that we must be careful to distinguish between dramatically
different  types  of  connections  and  concentrate  on  their  relative  impact.
Failing that, connectivity studies run the risk of remaining superficial and of
confusing more than they illuminate. Conrad warns us “to move beyond the
fetishization of mobility,” a preoccupation that “tends to render the past into
a  simple  prehistory  of  globalization”  and  thus  “tells  us  more  about  the
desires of the present than it does about the past.” Establishing connections
can only be the starting point, not the conclusion. Assessing their meaning
and  effects  is  more  important  but  also  likely  to  be  considerably  more
challenging. That, in the end, is a good thing, as it opens up new arenas of
debate.35

A final point. Connective approaches study contacts that were real, in the
sense  that  they  once  existed  in  a  physical  or  cognitive  sense.  But  it  is
equally important for us to explore connections that did not exist in this way
—connections that we can make based on shared features. If we fail to do
the  latter,  we  risk  replicating  nineteenth-century  precepts—enunciated  by
luminaries  such  as  Leopold  Ranke—to  study  ties  only  among  peoples  as
they became interconnected but to steer clear of other linkages in the form
of  perceived  similarities  between  unconnected  peoples,  which  ought  to  be
dealt with on their own terms.36

Even  as  they  are  no  longer  couched  in  prescriptive  terms,  such
tendencies have by no means disappeared. They continue to encourage us to
privilege  absolute  chronology  (for  spatial  connections),  geographical
proximity (for temporal connections), and more generally connections that
existed  at  the  time.  Yet  in  order  to  make  the  most  of  connective
perspectives, we need to transcend such conventional modes and overcome
the  constraints  of  synchronicity  and  physical  connectivity  in  a  bid  to
establish our own connections across time and space. Instead of relying on
history  to  make  connections  for  us,  we  can  do  so  ourselves.  That  is  the
domain of comparative study.

Comparison

In  the  context  of  ancient  global  studies,  comparative  perspectives  deserve
attention  because  they  offer  greater  flexibility  in  terms  of  space  and  time
than connectivity-centered work—which is a considerable benefit when we
are  dealing  with  a  period  of  often  limited  or  absent  integration—but  also
because they have been far less frequently employed. To make this happen,
we  need  to  understand  what  comparison  helps  us  do,  what  it  is  like,  and
how  its  rewards  outweigh  the  drawbacks,  and  ask  why  despite  its  varied
uses  and  great  potential  it  has  not  quite  caught  on  among  historians,
“ancient” or otherwise.37

What  is  comparative  history  good  for?  It  lifts  us  out  of  the  niches  of
(over)specialization  that  cut  up  the  study  of  history.  It  “defamiliarises  the
deceptively familiar,” removing us from the comfort zones and the specious
certainties (and cognate obliviousnesses) of narrow specialisms, by showing
us  what  was  possible  elsewhere.  Geoffrey  Lloyd  and  Nathan  Sivin  even
argue that the “chief prize is a way out of parochialism” because those who
