only  work  in  a  single  area  “easily  suppose  that  its  ways  are  natural  and
inevitable.”  Raymond  Grew  concurs:  “At  its  least,  comparison  is  an
effective  device  for  removing  the  inexcusable  blinders  of  parochialism.”
Complacency and lack of imagination impair our ability to understand and
explain  what  we  observe.  We  rely  in  no  small  measure  on  comparison  to
develop “awareness of alternatives.”38

Professionalized parochialism simulates the scenario of looking for lost
keys  under  a  streetlight  at  night  simply  because  that  is  where  the  light  is.
Historians  can  be  misled  by  the  idiosyncrasies  of  their  sources  or  of
academic microtraditions sustained by the intense fragmentation discussed
in  the  previous  chapter.  Actively  looking  at  other  areas  where  different
types  of  data  and  approaches  may  be  found  can  free  us  from  such  traps:
whenever a particular configuration of the evidence or a specific academic
microclimate  highlights  A,  it  is  all  too  easy  to  slight  B  even  if  its
importance  is  recognized  in  other  settings.  In  Dietrich  Rueschemeyer’s
words, “going beyond the boundaries of a single case can put into question
seemingly well-established causal accounts and generate new problems and
insights.”39

Comparison  forces  historians  to  confront  a  fundamental  question:  why
are  they  asking  the  questions  they  are  asking?  Self-reflection  is  easier  to
dodge  or  fudge  if  they  remain  safely  ensconced  in  the  comfort  zone  of
single-area  specialism  in  which  their  peers  are  used  to  asking  certain
questions and going about answering them in certain ways, with no need to
account  for  or  query  established  conventions.  Comparative  perspectives
make business as usual less likely, and innovation more so. As Conrad puts
it,  comparison  opens  conversations  between  different  trajectories  and
experiences;  compels  us  to  frame  questions  clearly  and  “pursue  problem-
oriented  research  strategies”;  and,  ideally,  injects  analytical  rigor  into
narratives.40

While comparative history is generally understood to be concerned with
differences  (or  similarities)  between  A  and  B,  it  is  in  the  end  at  least  as
helpful for studying individual cases. For how can we hope to proceed from
description to explanation except through comparison? We cannot tell what
really mattered in any one case, or even ask the right questions to find out,
unless we have a sense of other cases. Marc Bloch rightly insists that “there
is  no  true  understanding  without  a  certain  range  of  comparison.”  It  is  not
the differences and similarities between cases that are crucial; it is how they
help us understand the cases themselves.41

Comparative  awareness  helps  historians  every  step  of  the  way.  Grew
lists  four  consecutive  stages  at  which  it  offers  support—  “(1)  in  asking
questions,  (2)  in  identifying  historical  problems,  (3)  in  designing  the
appropriate  research,  and  (4)
testing  significant
conclusions.” The first two elements are vital: comparison alone enables us
to  establish  the  commonness  or  rarity  of  particular  features,  which  in  turn
generates new questions.

in  reaching  and

Comparative  history  allows  us  to  critique  “local”  explanations  derived
from  and  for  a  single  case  by  contrasting  inputs  and  outcomes  in  other
cases.  This  matters  especially  whenever  “local”  explanations  are  tied  up
with  notions  of  specialist  expertise  and  mastery.  Grew  captures  this
poignantly:  “Whereas  concern  for  detailed  accuracy  can  bury  significance
under  trivia,  comparison  can  make  the  apparently  trivial  significant—an
asset few of us can afford to ignore.”42

Historians should embrace all of these payoffs regardless of their subject
matter.  But  comparison  plays  an  even  greater  role  in  the  foundational
history  approach  to  global  ancient  world  studies  that  I  outlined  in  the
opening chapter: its typological-developmental perspective all but requires
systematic comparison across the globe.

What does comparative history look like? Historians and sociologists have
proposed  intersecting  taxonomies  of  different  kinds  of  comparative
analysis.  Some  types  primarily  serve  purposes  I  just  mentioned,  such  as
reorientation  (by  helping  to  formulate  questions)  and  illustration  (of  the
range of answers or outcomes). Meanwhile, more ambitious variants focus
on causality and generalization.43

In a much-cited paper, sociologists Theda Skocpol and Margaret Somers
distinguish between three major types: parallel demonstration of theory by
means  of  comparing  multiple  cases;  macrocausal  analysis,  in  which  cases
are  compared  to  identify  and  explain  large-scale  processes  and  structures;
and  contrast  of  contexts  to  establish  similarities  or  differences  between
individual cases.44

Very  broadly  speaking,  the  first  two  are  more  closely  related  to  each
other  than  to  the  third,  and  also  show  greater  affinity  to  concerns  and
methods of the social sciences—to generate and test hypotheses, and thus to
for
function,
experimentation.” The third more closely reflects concerns that are common
among historians.45

in  Judit  Bodnár’s  words,  as  a  “crude  substitute

How  so?  The  first  two  generally  involve  transcultural  comparisons
among  a  large  number  of  cases,  are  comfortable  with  the  high  degree  of
abstraction  this  approach  inevitably  requires,  and  are  driven  in  the  first
instance by the desire to test explanatory hypotheses, sometimes even with
universalizing  ambitions.  The  third,  by  contrast,  seeks  to  clarify  the
specifics of individual cases in ways that would not be feasible by looking
is  highly  suitable  for  small  samples  and
at  each  case  alone.  It
reflect
accommodates
preferences associated with different academic disciplines, such as different

contextualization.  These  differences

richer

degrees of tolerance for abstraction and the different weight they accord to
time and place as opposed to themes and concepts.46

For  present  purposes,  there  is  no  need  to  worry  about  whether  only
comparative history that is committed to hypothesis testing for the sake of
generating explanations merits that label. Nor are we compelled to restrict
the  meaning  of  “comparative  method”  to  “a  set  of  rules  which  can  be
methodically and systematically applied in gathering and using evidence to
test explanatory hypotheses.”47

It is true that “explanation and the identification of causal configurations
that  produce  major  outcomes  of  interest”  are  cornerstones  of  comparative
history.  I  am  sympathetic  to  the  general  thrust  of  Dan  Hoyer  and  Joe
Manning’s  argument  in  what  may  well  be  the  most  detailed  overview  of
comparative  work  and  methods  in  the  study  of  ancient  history  to  date.
Bemoaning the lack of clear methodology, they call on their peers to adopt
social science methods that break up spatiotemporal synchronicities in order
to  come  up  with  improved  explanations.  I  also  share  their  reservations
about  multiauthored  thematic  comparative  history  collections  that  gather
side by side studies of individual cases that are not actually analytical, “so
that the comparative perspective enters only in the sum of the chapters.”48

By the same token, I share their hope that “ancient history can regain a
prominent  place  in  academic  research,  with  the  insights  and  material
offered by ancient societies playing a key role in broader studies on human
evolution,  economic  development,  social  and  cultural  systems,  political
theory,  climate  change,  and  any  number  of  topics  pressing  to  the  modern
world.…  [H]aving  scholars  in  the  Humanities  and  the  natural  and  social
sciences  learn  to  speak  the  same  methodological  language,  it  may  again
become  apparent  that  and  in  which  ways  the  study  of  the  ancient  history
really matters.”49

Yet even as I personally concur with such views, and agree with Conrad
that comparative history is an essential tool for addressing big questions on
a global scale, it is imperative not to be exclusionary. Comparative history
must be a broad church in order to succeed. Accommodation of humanistic
preferences, embodied in the ancient Greek historian Kostas Vlassopoulos’s
recommendation  in  his  own  survey  of  “comparative  histories  for  ancient

historians”  to  “compare  systems  as  a  whole,  rather  than  particular
phenomena  or  processes,”  promises  to  go  a  long  way  in  alleviating
anxieties  and  overcoming  unhelpful  divisions  rooted  in  disciplinary
traditions and biases.50

Even a proponent of treating the comparative method as a “set of rules”
such  as  William  Sewell  promotes  a  more  flexible  stance,  a  comparative
perspective. While this may seem like splitting hairs, it is not. It reflects the
same openness as my advocacy for contextualization and framing alongside
work that is more strictly defined by globalizing approaches. A comparative
perspective need not be subject to particular expectations about purpose or
method  or  questions  of  scale  and  scope.  It  simply  “reduces  our  biases  by
presenting  us  with  alternative  systems  of  values  and  world  views,  and  by
imparting to us a sense of the richness and variety of human experience; it
provides  us  not  with  rules,  but  with  insights.  A  comparative  perspective
thus is valuable even to historians who can make no use of the comparative
method.”51

Grew,  who  as  a  historian  goes  so  far  as  to  reject  the  very  notion  of  a
comparative  method,  makes  a  similar  point:  “To  call  for  comparison  is  to
call  for  a  kind  of  attitude—open,  questioning,  searching—and  to  suggest
some  practices  that  may  nourish  it,  to  ask  historians  to  think  in  terms  of
problems  and  dare  to  define  those  problems  independently,  and  to  assert
that even the narrowest research should be conceived in terms of the larger
quests of many scholars in many fields.”52

Indeed,  he  sees  “no  paradox  in  insisting  that  the  study  of  a  single  case
can be comparative. The use of common terms and recognizable categories
assumes comparison.” A comparative perspective simply situates cases in a
broader  context:  we  may  “put  the  social  interactions,  expectations  and
demands  of people  who lived in  these  times into global focus  through  the
juxtaposition  of  specific  cases”  without  looking  for  parallels  or  imposing
taxonomic concepts.53

In the end, comparison is a mindset, not a set method. All that is required
is  that  it  is  applied  as  a  means  to  an  end,  which  we  can  define  in  any
number of ways.

This leaves us with an obvious question: if comparative approaches yield so
many  important  benefits  and  are  so  flexible,  why  are  they  not  more
commonly discussed and employed by historians?

There are some practical concerns. Historians might use comparisons to
home in on and thus merely confirm well-known similarities or differences.
Then again, there is no approach that is guaranteed to produce only original
work.  Some  gravitate  toward  study  of  the  ostensibly  similar:  note  for
instance  the  relative  popularity  of  comparisons  between  ancient  imperial
states at the expense of other kinds of polities.54

Reservations  of  a  philosophical  nature  are  more  powerful  and  firmly
rooted  in  the  history  of  the  field.  Heinz-Gerhard  Haupt  and  Jürgen Kocka
identify three major issues. First of all, comparative approaches may clash
with  the  premise  that  historians  ought  to  be  close  to  primary  sources  in
order  to  ensure  authenticity  and  professionalism.  The  more  ambitious  the
comparisons,  the  more  they  may  require  some  relaxation  of  expertise-
centered disciplinary standards. Second, whereas historians tend to be very
interested  in  change  over  time,  overtly  analytical  comparative  approaches
may  turn  history  into  an  assemblage  of  disjointed  cases  unmoored  from
chronological  scaffolding.  And  third,  historians  tend  to  regard  historical
processes as deeply embedded and difficult if not impossible to understand
outside  their  specific  contexts—a  premise  that  inevitably  complicates
comparative  analysis.  In  sum,  comparative  history  conflicts  with
ideographic ideals that prioritize the peculiarities of individual cases simply
because it ranges across multiple cases.55

In theory at least, the first point might readily be addressed by promoting
collaboration  among  area  experts.  While  comparative  history  is  held  back
by the countervailing disciplinary norm of individualized scholarship, it is
not  inherently  incompatible  with  high  levels  of  expertise.  The  other  two
reflect  resistance  to  abstraction.  Abstraction  is  an  inevitable  corollary  of
comparison:  as  I  noted  in  2018,  “[c]ases  have  to  be  defined  and  hence  to
some  extent  isolated  in  order  to  be  subjected  to  comparison,  and  to  be
related  to  each  other  as  individual  cases.  This  process  breaks  continuities:
the focus shifts from change over time to similarities and differences. Most
importantly,  comparison  is  predicated  on  selectivity:  cases  tend  to  be

decontextualized and stripped down, especially in multi-case comparisons.”
While any analysis, even the most comprehensive engagement with a single
case, involves a certain degree of abstraction, there can be no doubt that this
feature becomes more prominent the more cases are involved.56

This is not a trivial problem. On the one hand, multicase comparisons—
unless  undertaken  by  a  whole  team—detach  observers  from  primary
sources  and  contexts.  On  the  other,  they  can  be  expected  to  yield  more
robust findings. They do so by avoiding the so-called “small N” problem: it
is hard to tell how reliable conclusions drawn from just two or a few cases
really are. As Rueschemeyer puts it from a sociologist’s perspective, due to
“the  combination  of  many  factors  assumed  to  be  causally  relevant  with
evidence  from  only  a  small  number  of  comparable  cases,”  small  N
comparisons  may  end  up  with  “too  few  cases  chasing  too  many  causal
factors.”  That,  by  itself,  is  a  perfectly  good  reason  for  going  global  and
being  less  anxious  about  abstraction.  Yet  doing  so  is  bound  to  reinforce
anxieties about the loss of context, not to mention nuance.57

There is no fully satisfying solution to this: trade-offs are inevitable. At
the  end  of  the  day,  everything  depends  on  what  we  value  more:  robust
results or embeddedness. But this is no either-or scenario. Grew stresses the
absence of sharp demarcations: “These multiple uses of comparison form a
continuum,  as  do  the  differences  in  scale  from  one  instance  seen
comparatively  to  many  and  from  comparisons  within  one  society  to  those
that move across countries, cultures, or time.”58

Humanistic  concerns  about  decontextualization  can  be  mitigated  by
that  permits  more  faithful
focusing  on  fewer  cases,  an  approach
preservation of context and limits simplification and abstraction. Moreover,
historians may choose to focus on processes rather than specific variables in
order  to  preserve  continuities  and  accommodate  traditional  interest  in
change  over  time.  Comparative  history  can  be  built  “from  the  inside  out”
instead  of  from  some  abstract  assumptions,  sparing  historians  separation
from primary sources. As always, framing is decisive, including the choice
of units of comparison. There is no one-size-fits-all approach.59

Even so, resistance to abstraction is not merely a function of humanistic
historians’  defining  themselves  in  contradistinction  to  social  scientists.

to  “easy
Humanistic  emphasis  on  contingency  generates  aversion
determinism,” let alone reductionism, which translates to skepticism about
comparative work. Such attitudes are informed by a tradition committed to
the  putative  uniqueness  of  individual  cases  and  their  incommensurability
with others.60

Romanticism and historicism share responsibility. The former reacted to
an Enlightenment rationalism that stressed shared properties common to all
humans  beyond  their  cultural  differences;  instead,  it  chose  to  celebrate
diversity  and  specificity.  The  latter  echoed  some  of  this  in  emphasizing
particularistic developments, a perspective that made it harder to juxtapose
them  as  comparable—compare-able—cases.  As  George  Steinmetz  notes,
nineteenth-century  historicism  consequently  insisted  on  the  “irreducible
singularity  of  national  history,”  which  effectively  became  the  “most
powerful  anti-comparative  approach.”  This,  alongside  anthropology’s  turn
away from evolutionism, contributed to the fragmentations discussed in the
previous chapter.61

We  are  still  struggling  to  recover  from  such  perspectives.  Following  a
phase of positivistic comparativism in the postwar period that focused more
on  similarities  and  regularities,  they  have  been  revived  in  postmodern
notions  of  cultural  traditions  as  historically  constructed  sense-making
systems.  Playing  up  idealist  determination,  they  call  for  cultures  to  be
understood  on  their  own  terms,  a  premise  that  would  once  again  render
sustained  comparison  unattractive  or  even
impossible.  Much  of
contemporary  cultural  anthropology  is  a  striking  manifestation  of  that
trend.62

In principle, the uniqueness of cases does not rule out comparison unless
it is elevated to mutual incommensurability. Thus, only extremist positions
might prevent meaningful comparative work. We must not forget that even
claims to uniqueness are themselves comparative in nature. More generally,
all historical judgment depends on comparison, for example when we track
change over time: whenever we do, do we not already delimit cases in time
or  space  and  therefore  engage  in  comparison?  Thus,  in  Olstein’s  words,
comparison  is  a  “basic  mode  of  thought”:  “All  historical  knowledge  is

comparative  knowledge.”  What  comparative  history  does  is  make  this
explicit.63

Finally,  we  must  acknowledge  the  overlap  between  connective  and
comparative approaches. A century ago, Bloch, in his influential discussion
of  comparative  history  for  European  societies,  expressed  a  preference  for
comparing adjacent and contemporaneous societies that shared origins and
engaged in  sustained  interaction. Allowing  that this might  improve  ceteris
paribus  reasoning,  Sewell  sees  no  other  reason  to  follow  this  precept:  for
him, the utility of cases for hypothesis testing matters most.64

These  positions  once  again  reflect  differences  between  humanistic
preoccupation  with  context  and  commensurability  and  social-science-
inflected  emphasis  on  formal  analysis.  Comparisons  between  more
detached  cases  make  it  easier  to  identify  similarities  and  differences  that
derive from local factors.65

However, except in particular cases—say, comparisons between the Old
and  New  Worlds  before  1492—assumptions  about  independence  may  not
be  realistic.  True  autonomy  of  cases  is  often  impossible  to  ensure;  more
often  than  not,  connective  and  comparative  approaches  intersect.  It  might
be  tempting  to  contrast  them  in  ideal-typical  terms:  comparisons  tend  to
privilege  endogenous  causation  while  connective  perspectives  highlight
exogenous  factors;  comparisons  do  not  need  to  be  synchronic  whereas
spatial  connectivity  to  some  extent  relies  on  this.  Yet  in  practice,  such
divisions are blurry at best.66

It  may  well  be  that  for  some  scholars,  the  increased  embrace  of
connections  and  flows  serves  as  a  strategy  of  evading  explicitly
comparative  approaches.  But  it  does  not  have  to  be  that  way:  connective
and comparative perspectives are interoperable. Just as comparative history
needs  to  take  account  of  endogenous  and  exogenous  causation  in
accordance  with  the  strength  of  linkages  between  cases,  histories  of
transfers and entanglements call for comparison of cases that were subject
to such processes. In the end, we must combine connective and comparative
approaches  in  order  to  globalize  our  understanding  of  the  ancient  world:
neither one of them is enough.67

World History

At  its  most  basic,  world  history  may  simply  offer  parallel  exposition  of
regional developments in different parts of the planet. In practice, it tends to
be  structured  by  the  search  for  unifying  elements,  for  common  or
contrasting  trajectories  and  dynamics.  Much  as  in  the  more  ambitious
versions of comparative history, its greatest promise lies in approaches that
focus  on  features,  concepts,  and  processes  and  track  them  across  cases
regardless  of  whether  those  cases  were  connected  at  the  time.  This  makes
world  history  particularly  suitable  for  covering  phenomena  that  emerged
multiply and independently around the globe, such as those that define the
foundational  phase  of  sociocultural  evolution  described  in  the  opening
chapter.  This  preoccupation  with  typological  and  developmental  patterns
and
the  widest  possible
spatiotemporal  scope:  the  more  cases  we  capture,  the  more  robust
assessments can be.68

their  comparative  assessment  calls

for

For  recent  periods,  when  networks  finally  extended  across  the  entire
planet, “world history” has increasingly been absorbed by “global history,”
which is commonly defined by its emphasis on connectivity. That, however,
is not an issue for students of the premodern world. In fact, while global in
scope,  foundational  history  is  concerned  precisely  with  the  phase  of
maximum disconnection, after the Holocene had severed the Americas from
Afroeurasia but prior to the modern Atlantic exchange.69

In  terms  of  its  primary  objectives,  its  emphasis  on  features  and
processes,  world history  might  justifiably  be regarded  as a special  version
of  comparative  history.  However,  whereas  comparative  study  may
accommodate  great  nuance  and  require  specialized  expertise—especially
when it targets just two or a few cases—world history calls for a different
approach, most notably a departure from the disciplinary fixation on “close
scrutiny  of  primary  sources.”  It  relies  heavily,  sometimes  exclusively,  on
secondary  scholarship,  on  “a  huge  historiography  highly  fragmented  in
need of connection and synthesis.”70

Rather  than  considering  this  a  liability—as  conventional  academic
standards,  discussed  in  the  following  sections,  might  imply—this  is
perfectly  fine:  after  all,  what  is  all  that  detailed,  expert  scholarship  for,  if

not to support overarching syntheses? As Grew observes, “The benefits of
using  primary  materials  are  often  misstated.  In  practice,  greater  historical
understanding  is  threatened  more  by  mindlessness  than  by  error,  for
inaccuracies  are  easier  to  identify  and  correct  than  are  misleading
assumptions.”  That  said,  planetary  approaches  work  particularly  well  in
teaching,  which  is  subject  to  more  relaxed  professional  expectations  of
expertise.71

At the beginning of her global history book, Pamela Crossley somewhat
optimistically maintains that “historians as professionals … would probably
all  agree  that  it  is  impossible  to  understand  the  great  changes  in  human
history  without  considering  the  whole  earth.”  Even  if  they  did  in  fact  all
agree, implementation has been a very different story. This is true above all
of  what  Conrad  calls  the  “all-in  version  of  global  history”—the  history
either  of  everything  (the  whole  planet)  or  of  a  particular  idea  or
phenomenon through space and time (such as empire, kingship, tea, coffee,
sugar, cotton, glass, gold, and so forth). The latter has been a growth area
among public-facing books, whereas more comprehensive overviews often
take  the  form  of  textbooks.  (“Big  History,”  which  endeavors  to  cover
everything  from  the  Big  Bang  to  the  present,  tracks  and  establishes
connections  between  cosmological,  geological,  biological,  and  social
evolution.)72

Genuine  world  histories  specifically  of  the  ancient  period  are  rare:  a
forthcoming  book  by  Ian  Morris  is  poised  to  fill  that  gap.  Existing  work
tends  to  suffer  from  familiar  shortcomings.  To  name  just  two  recent
examples: despite its title, Stanley Burstein’s The World from 1000 BCE to
300 CE is limited to Eurasia and North Africa. Africa south of the Sahara
receives  a  total  of  three  pages  of  text  and  the  Americas  are  altogether
absent.73 The same is true of Eivind Seland’s Global History of the Ancient
World,  whose  subtitle—Asia,  Europe  and  Africa  before  Islam—gives  the
game  away.  He  invokes  a  focus  on  “unifying  and  common  developments
and the importance of connections across political and cultural boundaries”
to justify omission of the Americas, even as those “common developments”
ought  to  have  favored  inclusion:  “Whenever  we  talk  about  global  history
and about the world with regard to Antiquity, it is nevertheless the case that

we primarily think of the ‘Old World’. Societies in Europe, Asia and Africa
were in constant contact with each other, while Australia and the Americas
were  only  drawn  into  the  same  networks  at  a  much  later  date.”  The  same
equation of world history with spatial connectivity defines the scope of the
otherwise pioneering Institute for the Study of the Ancient World (ISAW) at
New  York  University,  resulting  in  the  same  confinement  to  Eurasia  and
parts of Africa.74

This matters a lot for the foundational history project. Both comparative
and  (genuine)  world  history  are  essential  means  of  overcoming  the
limitations of absolute chronology and connectivity, both of which tend to
exclude  the  Americas  and  large  parts  of  Africa  from  global  ancient  world
studies. Yet just as “world histories” of antiquity commonly give short shrift
to those parts of the globe, comparative studies that juxtapose Afroeurasia
and the preconquest Americas have also remained rare, even as we finally
observe  an  uptick  in  interest.  This  is  one  of  the  biggest  potential  growth
areas for a globalized ancient history.75

While it would be unwise to rank different globalizing approaches, it seems
fair  to  note  gradations  in  terms  of  ambition  and  effort.  Contextualization
can  be  accomplished  with  limited  effort,  whereas  all-in  world  history
involves  huge  challenges,  as  do  the  more  sophisticated  varieties  of
comparative  analysis.  Connectivity  studies  differ  depending  on  what  they
seek  to  accomplish  and  how:  demonstrating  connections  is  not  that  hard,
making sense of them much more so.

The  intrinsic  properties  of  different  approaches  are  not  the  only  factor
that  determines  their  appeal  or  chances  of  success.  Academic  traditions,
incentive  structures,  and  support  systems  matter  just  as  much.  As  we  will
see  in  the  remainder  of  this  chapter,  relaxation  of  conventional  ideals  of
mastery  and  the  promotion  of  collaborative  efforts  are  of  paramount
importance  for  bolstering  work  that  moves  beyond  basic  framing  or
exploration  of  connections  toward  more  systematically  comparative  or
planetary perspectives.

In  the  final  analysis,  the  latter  two  are  not  merely  more  challenging—
whether  in  terms  of  method  (comparison)  or  data  volume  and  variety
(planetary  scale).  They  are  also  the  ones  most  likely  to  advance  the
globalization  of  ancient  studies,  and  thus  to  make  the  concept  of
foundational  history  a  reality.  If  we  want  to  put  our  understanding  of  the
ancient  world  on  a  more  solid  footing,  it  is  those  approaches  that  will
require the greatest attention and investment.

Who Is Afraid of Foundational History?

There is no shortage of different ways to globalize ancient history. So, what
are the obstacles? Reduced to essentials, the urge to compartmentalize the
study of the past into ever-smaller slivers springs from a quest for mastery
—of evidence, of languages, of scholarship. Long art and short lives favor
niche specialization. But we ought to be wary of conflating two issues: the
desire for mastery, and the need. They are not the same.

Academia  considers  mastery  a  virtue,  valued  as  a  safeguard  of
professional conduct. On a personal level, it instills pride while satisfying a
yearning  for  comfort  and  protection  from  the  unmastered.  Quasi-objective
criteria seamlessly converge with matters of ego. This potent mix makes the
desire for mastery all the more powerful, though not inevitable. It is after all
perfectly possible to be a “real” scholar while venturing from safe cocoons,
the  pursuit  of  something  new.  However  much
risking  error
institutionalized  incentive  structures  may  obstruct  such  efforts  and  create
reputational risk for the bold, there is nothing in the exercise of scholarship
as such that requires strict self-siloing or perfectionism.

in

It would be grotesque to deny the need for expertise, especially in a book
that  calls  for  stronger  emphasis  on  transformative  dynamics  of  change:
everything we have learned about that change has come from the relentless
accumulation  of  specialized  knowledge.  Yet  there  is  ample  room  for
improvement when it comes to organizing and applying such knowledge.

In

the  study  of

the  ancient  world,  conventional  disciplinary
specialization  and  niche  formation  are  predicated  on  a  peculiarly  narrow
notion  of  expertise:  one  that  emphasizes  the  factual  over  the  analytical,
memory  over  intelligence,  skill  over  vision,  depth  over  range,  familiarity

over risk-taking. They who know the most about a particular subject, who
have  studied  it  the  deepest  and  longest,  and  who  can  penetrate  it  with  the
richest toolkit of method and theory conform most closely to the normative
expectations of an intensely fragmented field.

Such ideals tend to obscure a critical challenge: to strike an appropriate
balance  between  narrow  specialization  and  broader  contextualization.  As
they are currently—and have indeed long been—configured, ancient studies
fields  overemphasize  the  former  at  the  expense  of  the  latter.  Yet  both  are
needed  in  equal  measure.  We  cannot  hope  to  unify  the  study  of  ancient
history unless we revisit professional expectations.

is

just  as

The ability to make connections and make sense of them, or to undertake
nonnaïve  comparisons,
important  as  are  conventional
demonstrations  of  mastery  of  sources  or  technical  skills.  The  former  must
not be treated as a bonus feature but as integral to our engagement with the
ancient world. But that is not the case, and indeed cannot be the case in a
professional  environment  that  is  designed  to  prize  conventional  forms  of
individualized expertise above all else.

The Language Trap

requirements  are

All  of  this  applies  to  any  branch  of  historical  scholarship.  Even  so,  the
specializing  impulse  is  particularly  strong  among  ancient  historians.
reason.
Language
Notwithstanding their ostensibly spatial and/or temporal framing, subfields
that  deal  with  literate  cultures  are  conventionally  constructed  around
languages  and  writing  systems.  Greek  and  Roman  “Ancient  History”  or
“Classics”  set  the  tone,  followed  by  Jewish  studies,  Sanskrit  studies,
Egyptology, and Assyriology. The same is true of Sinology.

single  most

important

the

This  did  not  happen  for  want  of  alternatives.  Language  is  not  always
constitutive  of  such  fields.  Byzantine  studies  are  not  normally  part  of
Greco-Roman Ancient History or Classics even though the Byzantines were
self-styled  Romans  who  spoke  and  wrote  Greek.  Use  of  Latin  is  not  the
paramount criterion for demarcating medieval European history; nor is the
use of classical Chinese in East Asia. In some cases, languages and scripts
cut  up  organic  entities,  most  notably  in  the  case  of  Egypt,  where  the

succession  of  ancient  Egyptian,  Greek,  Coptic,  and  Arabic  truncates  the
study of the same uniquely distinctive ecosystem.

It  is  obvious  that  language  is  essential  for  the  understanding  of  early
literate cultures. It is equally clear that those languages are often difficult to
access  for  modern  observers,  and  that  learning,  teaching,  and  employing
them consequently requires considerable effort. Sunk cost effects are large:
such language skills are not portable. However, none of this should distract
us  from  a  fundamental  question:  just  how  much  language  expertise  do
ancient historians actually need?

The answer is not nearly as clear-cut as it is routinely made out to be—
that is, the more mastery, the better. If you aspire to join an orchestra, you
need to excel at playing an instrument. If you want to compete as an athlete,
you need to train your body. If you want to be a physicist, you had better
know your math. And if you want to be a philologist, you ought to immerse
yourself in the finer points of the language you are studying.

History  is  different. Ancient  historians  need exactly  as many  skills—as
much  (and  thus  as  little)  knowledge  of  ancient  and  modern  languages,
archaeological methods, statistics and social science approaches, or coding
—as  they  need  to  do  their  work,  whether  in  research  or  teaching.  These
requirements are contingent on the task, not fixed; they certainly do not call
or even allow for universalizing aspirations to—or pretensions of—mastery.
For  a  lot  of  undergraduate  teaching,  which  accounts  for  most  of
instruction,  ancient  historians  may  need  little  specialized  language  skills,
sometimes  none.  In  their  research,  requirements  depend  entirely  on  what
they  want  to  do.  If  this  sounds  painfully  banal,  it  is  because  it  is:  yet  one
could not tell by observing disciplinary norms and expectations. More often
than  not,  these  center  mastery  of  language  in  ways  that  ignore  the  huge
amount of variation in what it is historians actually do.

Once again, this is a hallmark of the study of early cultures. Historians
of, say, Russia had better know some Russian. Yet they would not normally
be  expected  to  teach  it.  Ancient  area  studies  fields  are  different,  most
pointlessly  so  Classics,  which  has
long  burdened  historians  and
archaeologists with demands that they teach Greek and/or Latin, ideally at
all levels: more on this in the next chapter.

In this context, the alternative—accepting that historians simply need to
be  good  enough  at  what  they  do—is  bound  to  seem  alien  and  suspicious.
This  is  a  mistake.  If  you  wish  to  edit  Egyptian  papyri,  your  Egyptian  or
Greek or Arabic had better be excellent. If you work in areas in which high-
quality  translations  are  commonly  available  and  the  answers  to  your
questions do not hinge on the meaning of particular words or phrases—yes,
such  projects  do  exist!—you  need  less  of  that.  If  you  focus  on  material
culture, you may end up relying even less on dead languages or perhaps not
at all.

None  of  this  might  be  ideal  in  an  abstract  sense:  in  a  perfect  world,
everyone  would  excel  at  everything.  But  it  is  certainly  good  enough.  In
practice,  moreover,  it  is  often  tacitly  if  grudgingly  accepted.  At  the  same
time, normative models are resilient, biting when aspirants are at their most
vulnerable:  defending  dissertations,  applying  for  academic  positions,
coming up for tenure. If only academics got a penny every time someone in
a  faculty  meeting  darkly  observes  that  a  student’s/candidate’s/colleague’s
“languages” might not be quite up to snuff!76

All  this  posturing  is  more  than  just  a  nuisance.  It  has  long  shaped  and
indeed  warped  our  engagement  with  the  ancient  past.  Feedback  loops  are
inevitable.  As  long  as  mastery  of  languages(s)  rather  than  pragmatic
flexibility is the ideal, the kind of ancient history that gets done is the kind
that requires and rewards that mastery, centering texts. This in turn attracts
aspirants  who  like/love  languages  and/or  are  good  at  learning/teaching
languages,  which  once  again  shapes  what  is  done  and  how.  Conversely,
those who may excel in other respects are penalized and sidelined.

As  a  consequence,  niches  that  boast  the  most  texts  are  likely  to  attract
disproportionate support. Nonliterate or text-poor cultures may be relegated
to  a  second  tier.  Translation  will  be  undervalued,  not  just  in  absolute  but
also in relative terms—relative to how highly this vital enterprise would be
esteemed if mastery of the original languages were not as highly prized as it
is. All of these features mutually reinforce each other, creating a matrix that
is not only highly robust but invites ossification.

The  payoff  is  clear:  highly  specialized  work  that  grows  out  of  detailed
engagement with the written record. So is the cost: the warping of skill sets

and  modes  of  training,  and  the  creation  of  a  systematic  barrier  to  cross-
cultural  approaches  that  are  incompatible  with  principles  of  individual
philological  expertise.  Circular  reasoning  discourages  alternatives.  Once
fields  have  been  thoroughly  shaped  by  specific  expectations  of  mastery,
more  wide-ranging  work  seems  more  difficult  and  unattractive  than  it
otherwise would, and is  therefore  less  likely  to  occur,  which in  turn  helps
perpetuate those expectations.

To be sure, the study of early cultures has long offered a way out, a parallel
track, in the form of archaeological fieldwork. The benefits are well-known:
all cultures, and not just those that left textual records, can be studied in a
fairly standardized manner. This makes it possible for archaeologists to do
something  that  would  be  unthinkable  for  all  but  the  most  prodigiously
gifted  historians  who  are  committed  to  firsthand  use  of  texts:  work  in
different areas. They enjoy portable skills. In practice, of course, powerful
constraints  assert  themselves:  notions  of  expertise  and  mastery  extend
beyond language and writing, pulling archaeologists into individual niches.
Moreover,  the  material  record  in  different  places  can  be  so  different  that
learning  a  new  language  may  not  be  a  bad  analogy  for  what  footloose
archaeologists are required to do. In addition, what Colin Renfrew calls the
“Great Divide” between Old and New World archaeology has long curtailed
professional exchanges. But at least in principle, and sometimes in practice,
the option of mobility is real.77

While  this  mobility  may  help  scholars,  it  does  not  address  imbalances
between subfields that in turn slant the playing field for cross-cultural work.
Catherine Holmes and Naomi Standen are right on target in their discussion
of  the  “Global  Middle  Ages”  when  they  note  that  there  are  many  reasons
why regions outside specific parts of Eurasia are marginalized: “Aside from
often  unacknowledged  assumptions  of  cultural  superiority,  the  most
important reason … is the marked disparities in surviving evidence types. It
is  not  necessarily  that  less  evidence  on  aggregate  survives  in  one  world
region than in another; rather that we may think it easier to discuss certain
types of human behaviour if we have access to particular kinds of records.”
Material  evidence  helps  overcome  this  attitude.  In  any  case,  they  insist,

“marginalization  of  entire  world  regions  from  long  periods  of  human
history is not the answer to the evidence conundrum.”78

We  might  even  want  to  go  a  step  further  and  embrace  that  conundrum
instead of using it as an excuse to stick to business as usual with the usual
suspects.  We  should  direct  more  attention  to  underserved,  marginalized
cases,  not  double  down  on  what  is  readily  available  and  intelligible.
Otherwise, all we end up doing is adhere to the looking-for-lost-keys-under-
the-streetlight  principle:  we  foreground  the  most  visible  and  fashion  our
narratives accordingly. We cannot  hope to  globalize ancient  studies  unless
we do better than that.

Escape Routes and Roadblocks

But  what  about  the  many  historians  who  want  or  need  to  work  with  texts
but  would  nevertheless  like  to  branch  out  beyond  their  own  niches?  One
day,  software  may  solve  their  problems,  by  providing  serviceable
translations  of  ancient  languages  on  demand,  by  putting  all  relevant
information at their fingertips, by forging connections beyond specialisms.
All this still seems to lie very much in the future. Even so, given the pace of
recent advances,  it would be unwise to  bet  against  technological  solutions
over the long run. If we are lucky, these questions might even seem quaint
to future generations.79

But what about the here and now? The least practicable model on offer is
that of the polymath genius who traverses different bodies of evidence and
scholarship by picking up arcane languages at the drop of a hat and drawing
on  eidetic  memory.  In  real  life,  immersion  in  two  (or  more)  different
cultures represents a huge investment even for the talented and locks them
into a particular configuration of specialisms. What if someone were to add
classical  Chinese  to,  say,  Greek  and  Latin  (and  thus,  for  modern
scholarship,  Mandarin  and  Japanese  to  the  canonical  quartet  of  English,
German, French, and Italian) in order to undertake comparative work, only
to discover that they would rather work on Sanskrit texts or Mayan culture?
Individual  efforts  along  these  lines  incur  daunting  sunk  costs  as  well  as
opportunity costs.80

The  most  plausible  strategy  for  overcoming

the  constraints  of
specialization is both glaringly obvious and remarkably rare: collaboration.
Individual experts will always be with us. All the global turn requires them
to do is to pool their skills and resources to address broader questions across
space and time.

This is not an idle fantasy. Not only is this approach standard practice in
science  and  technology  fields,  it  can  also  be  found  in  most  of  the  social
sciences.  On  occasion,  it  receives  active  support  even  in  the  humanities,
including  Ancient  History:  the  German  academic  system  with  its  various
excellence clusters and similar group initiatives is but one example.81

Most  importantly,  collaboration  is  already  common  in  a  field  that  is  of
central importance to the study of ancient cultures, namely archaeology. It
is striking how little influence this has had. For many generations, teacher-
scholars of ancient societies who primarily or exclusively work with textual
evidence have been rubbing shoulders with those who focus on the material
record. They often study the same things, inhabit the same departments, and
in  some  cases  even  attend  the  same  professional  meetings.  Even  so,
intimate  coexistence  in  Classics,  Egyptology,  Assyriology,  and  cognate
programs has signally failed to encourage text-centered ancient historians to
explore and adopt collaborative routines.

By  contrast,  the  heroic  humanistic  model  of  academic  individualism
continues  to  have  a  powerful  and  regressive  impact  on  the  working
conditions of archaeologists who find themselves forced into the straitjacket
this  model  mandates:  normative  expectations  for  training  that  prioritizes
single-authored seminar papers and dissertations, for faculty hiring, and for
tenure  and  promotion  dances  around  the  golden  calf  of  the  personal
monograph.

Instead  of  embracing  the  greater  openness  of  archaeological  practice,
academic institutions tend to chip away at it as best they can. Thus, not only
has  the  more  conservative  and  solipsistic  mode  of  individualized  teaching
and  research  stood  fast,  but  it  keeps  encroaching  on  alternative  forms  of
engagement. It is hard to decide which is the more astounding pathology—
that this has been going on for so long, or that it generally seems to be taken
as a given.

In  practice,  these  persistent  differences  widen  gaps  between  text-  and
material-based studies of ancient cultures. That in turn hinders globalizing
moves  because  they  crucially  depend  on  material  remains  that  are  much
more  readily  available  than  written  sources.  As  a  result,  indifference  to
collaboration ends up holding back the entire field.

Options for archaeologists, meanwhile, stretch from rocks to hard places.
One is embrace of the heroic model of language mastery and individualistic
practice. In the United States and other Anglo systems, archaeologists who
find  themselves  in  Classics  programs  are  routinely  expected  to  submit  to
that model without further ado (more on this in the next chapter).

Detachment is another. In many settings, employment in departments of
Anthropology  and  History—which  may  or  may  not  belong  to  different
divisions  (humanities  and  social  sciences)—separates  scholars  who  study
the  same  kinds  of  ancient  societies.  That  impedes  both  understanding  and
exchange,  not  to  mention  cross-cultural  work.  In  parts  of  continental
Europe, free-standing departments of “Classical Archaeology” embody the
worst of the dissective impulse, creating multiple fractures across text- and
material-centered  scholarship  and  teaching.  In  the  United  Kingdom,  the
Institute of Archaeology in London liberates members from language-based
hegemonies, yet without being able to sustain close ties with historians of
early cultures.

Few  scholars  are  likely  to  collaborate  unless  organizational  templates  and
institutional incentive structures recognize and reward them for it. Instead,
those  who  might  be  inclined  to  follow  this  path  might  give  up,  or  be
discouraged  from  joining  in  the  first  place.  Along  the  way,  normative
arrangements become a self-fulfilling prophecy. As it is currently set up, the
system  almost  seems  deliberately  designed  to  stymie  collaborative  efforts.
Unless  that  system  changes,  the  academic  humanities,  and  ancient  studies
within  them,  will  be  unable  to  escape  the  grip  of  a  model  of  heroic
individualism  that  is  fundamentally  incompatible  with  cross-cultural  work
and the flexible modes of collaboration this work calls for.

Every  step  of  the  way—from  undergraduate  theses,  admission  to
graduate  programs,  graduate  instruction,  and  credentialing  based  on

“masterpieces” (aka dissertations) to hiring, renewal, tenure, and promotion
—the  academic  humanities  doggedly  adhere  to  a  model  that  undermines
cross-cultural  and  multidisciplinary  engagement.  True  “foundational
history” is all but impossible under these circumstances: feasible perhaps at
some levels of teaching but hardly in advanced training or research.82

reveals

It  is  from  that  vantage  point  that  the  seemingly  innocuous  notion  of  a
genuinely  globalized  ancient  history
its  disruptive,  even
revolutionary potential. For how could it be accommodated within existing
epistemic  and  institutional  structures?  While  this  may  seem  a  drawback,
even a fatal flaw, it need not be so. This simply means that our engagement
with ancient history cannot be redesigned in isolation. Reform needs to be
part of broader changes that challenge, alter, and enrich practices that have
grown and hardened over time like barnacles on a shipwreck.

I chose words such as “challenge,” “alter,” and “enrich” carefully and for
a reason. The issue at hand is not dismantling or burning it all down. Some,
many, perhaps even most scholars may prefer to work on their own or have
no particular interest in a globalizing agenda. There is no reason for that to
change:  nobody  should  wish  for  five-year  plans  for  academia.  The  real
problem is that the current system is set up so as not to tolerate alternative
modes of practice: it is stridently, unselfconsciously majoritarian instead of
pluralistic.  My  point  is  not  that  all  ancient  historians  should  do  things
differently. It is that right now they are all expected to do things a certain
way or face de facto penalties in career prospects. There are no routinized
protections for minority practices that deviate from the hegemonic mode.

This  is  not  just  a  formidable  obstacle  to  globalizing  work  and
collaboration.  It  is  also,  as  unthinkingly  majoritarian  and  antipluralistic
systems  tend  to  be,  latently  exclusionary  and  morally  dubious.  Current
arrangements  persist  above  all  thanks  to  inertia  rather  than  any  intrinsic
advantage  or  compelling  ethical  rationale.  Yet  inertia  is  not  necessary  to
uphold  standards:  pluralism  is  not  to  be  confused  with  a  free-for-all.  It
merely requires that different practices that produce measurable payoffs in
terms of knowledge and understanding be admitted to the table. Then again,
anxiety about standards is more often than not about “standards,” code for

maintaining  whatever  conservative  and  exclusionary  strictures  have  long
been in place.

Collaboration  ought  to  alleviate  such  anxieties.  It  is  a  way  of  avoiding
what  Nathan  Ballantyne  calls  “epistemic  trespassing,”  defined  as  passing
judgment  in  areas  without  the  expertise  experts  in  those  areas  command.
Instead,  collaboration  safeguards  standards.  At  the  same  time,  it  offers  a
much-needed  check  on  delusions  of  mastery.  Mira  Seo  comments  on  the
experience  of  team-teaching  a  cross-cultural  version  of  “Classics”  as  “a
humbling experience. No single instructor ever has full mastery over all the
materials.…  In  a  truly  global  syllabus,  meaning  emerges  from  thematic
juxtapositions,  and  the  teaching  team  can  engineer  the  course  narrative  to
feature  different  aspects  of  faculty  expertise  that  may  evolve  over  time.
That  is,  we  not  only  teach  together,  but  we  learn  from  each  other.”
Humbling and progress go hand in hand.83

In  the  world  of  business  as  usual,  meanwhile,  the  fetishization  of
expertise,  specialization,  and  individualism  elides  a  critical  but  widely
ignored question: what is specialized scholarship grounded in expertise for?
Is  it  just  an  end  in  itself,  undertaken  for  the  amusement  of  fellow  experts
and personal advancement? Surely not: it is (also) meant to support broader
studies and insights. But what if the same system that sustains specialized
expertise  within  small  silos  by  its  very  nature  imposes  hard  limits  on  the
scope,  ambition,  and  feasibility  of  such  a  wider  reach?  What  if  by
privileging one it curtails the other?

In  their  History  Manifesto,  Jo  Guldi  and  David  Armitage  observe  that
“[p]rofessionalisation  has  led  to  marginalization.”  The  conventional  de
facto  divide  between  “real,”  niche-facing  scholarship  and  outward-facing
work—textbooks,  popular  media,  commercial  publishing—leaves  a  huge
gap  in  between  that  cannot  easily  by  filled  by  scholars  and  instructors
operating within the current system. Grounded in but reaching beyond the
former  type  of  work,  the  cross-cultural,  globalizing  approaches  we  have
encountered in this chapter aim to do just that.84

Present  conditions,  however,  do  not  favor  such  endeavors.  Without  the
necessary  support  and  protection,  this  kind  of  work  may  never  realize  its
potential. Nor will it be easy to develop and agree on appropriate standards

of  training  and  evaluation;  yet  without  them,  a  sense  of  qualitative
difference—that  is,  inferiority—to  established  modes  of  research  and
instruction is bound to linger.

The  foundational  history  agenda  contends  with  a  self-enforcing  system
of  deterrence.  The  lack  of  systemic  opportunity  and  incentive,  of
instructional  models,  and  of  consensual  standards  all  feed  off  each  other,
preventing alternatives from gaining traction. Formidable as it is, however,
that  negative  feedback  loop  is  only  part  of  a  bigger  story.  As  though  the
lack  of  incentives  for  change  were  not  enough,  meaningful  reform  faces
further obstacles.

4

Closing Classics

WHY WE NEED TO MOVE ON

There is no Alterthumswissenschaft.

—KARL HEINRICH MILHAUSER, UEBER PHILOLOGIE1

IN  THE  ANGLOSPHERE,  the  continued  existence  of  Classics  as  an  academic
field endowed with its own institutional units and degree programs is one of
the biggest impediments to the development of a foundational history that
does  justice  to  the  full  sweep  of  the  human  journey.  Its  presence  deepens
the fractures created by the manner in which past generations of European
scholars responded  to  the  accelerating  expansion  of new  knowledge about
ancient  cultures  beyond  Greece  and  Rome:  instead  of  nurturing  and
accommodating
flexibly
comprehensive domain of studying and understanding the ancient world, an
ever-larger number of ever-narrower niche fields were spun off to populate
the  academic  landscape  while  hard  lines  were  drawn  both  between  white
Europe and everywhere else and between literate and nonliterate societies.

skills  within  a

specialist

required

the

As we saw in the second chapter, these processes unfolded in the context
of much broader intellectual trends and power dynamics. At the same time,
the  study  of  GreeceandRome
the  precocious
embodied  and  modeled  all  these  divisive  principles  with  prophetic  clarity
and persuasive authority. Where this format still lives on, primarily in North
America,  the  British  Isles,  and  Australasia,  it  prevents  any  future

institutionalization  of

consolidation  of  our  engagement  with  the  ancient  world.  There  are  three
reasons for this.

First,  its  label  and  residual  privilege  among  area  studies  fields  in  the
Anglo-American  university  scene  marks  out  Classics  as  somehow  special
and  therefore  worthy  of  special  attention  and  commitments.  That  all  by
itself arrests the development of globalized scholarship and teaching on the
ancient  world.  More  specifically,  this  relative  privileging  of  Classics
prioritizes Greek and Roman history in ways that curtail engagement with
other and generally (even) less funded ancient histories.

Second,  in  a  considerable  number  of  academic  institutions,  Classics
partly or entirely absorbs the study and teaching of the history of the Greco-
Roman  world,  an  area  that  thanks  to  its  rich  documentation  and
exceptionally deep scholarly penetration will always be an essential element
of any global foundational history. By laying claim to this essential element,
Classics isolates and detaches it from the study of other ancient histories as
well as from intellectual currents in the History profession at large. Both of
these barriers interfere with globalizing perspectives.

Third,  embedding  professional  historians  of  Greece  and  Rome  in  the
Classics template not only separates them from other ancient historians and
from historians more generally but in practice tends to ensnare them in the
“language trap” that I discussed in the previous chapter.

None  of  these  dynamics  are  conducive  to  the  globalization  of  ancient
history  that  I  sketched  out  in  chapters  1  and  3.  In  all  these  respects,  for
ancient historians the Classics model and its corollaries ought to serve as a
model of how not to go about our business.

To be sure,  globalizing  ancient  history  is  not  the  measure of all things.
Perhaps  Classics  as an organizing  principle is  imbued with so many  other
virtues that they outweigh these concerns. But that is not the case. The field
of Classics in and of itself is deeply flawed and obsolete.

Defenders of the Faith

Classics  has  come  a  long  way  since  the  heydays  of  the  late  nineteenth
century.  Since  then,  the  mighty  have  fallen  farther  than  even  the  greatest

pessimists  (or  optimists,  depending  on  their  preferences)  could  have
imagined at the time.

Gone are the days when Classics, and above all a thorough grounding in
Greek and Latin, were embraced (or at least endured) as a marker of status
by the rising bourgeoisie. Gone are the days when in the German homeland
of Altertumswissenschaft, committing four of every ten hours of secondary
schooling to Greek and Latin could be presented as a harmonious balance;
when  doctoral  dissertations  in  any  field  had  to  be  submitted  in  Latin;  and
when  at  full-service  universities,  one  in  twelve  of  all  chaired  professors
across  all  fields  dealt  with  some  aspect  of  Greece  and  Rome.  (For
comparison, my own institution, Stanford University, would need to employ
more than a hundred full professors in Classics to match that ratio.)2

In the US, most colleges required Greek and Latin up to the Civil War,
yet by 1912 two-thirds of them mandated neither. At Oxbridge, Greek entry
requirements died with the First World War, and those for Latin at the end
of the 1950s.3

Over time, Classics has become just one humanities field of many, and a
rather marginal one at that. Shrinkage has gone hand in hand with anxieties
about  survival,  an  experience  it  shares  with  English,  History,  and  similar
disciplines. That should not come as a surprise: alarums about crisis are as
old  as  the  academic  humanities  themselves.  In  their  recent  book  on  this
perennial discourse, Paul Reitter and Chad Wellmon argue that this is not by
accident:  rather,  they  hold,  the  very  notion  of  crisis  forms  “a  core  part  of
the project of the humanities,” as long as the humanities are considered “a
privileged resource for resolving perceived crises of meaning and value.” In
a sense, ever since the eighteenth century the humanities have needed to be
in crisis to perform their mission—to respond to the unstoppable advance of
technology, science, and capitalism.4

This  mission  fosters  an  adversarial,  defensive  posture  vis-à-vis  such
modernizing  changes.  Yet  the  academic  humanities  arose  from  the  same
dynamics:  they  derive  their  force  from  disciplinization,  from  their
institutionalization within the modern university. The resultant emphasis on
professionalism  and  specialization  as  sources  of  authority  and  legitimacy
inevitably  engenders  tensions—“therapeutic  value  versus  epistemic  value;

liberal  value  versus  utility  and  practical  value;  moral  formation  versus
method;  knowledge  versus  information.”  Classics,  born  of  neohumanistic
and  Romantic  yearnings  yet  trapped  in  the  steel  cage  of  contemporary
professional standards, embodies this dissonance with unusual intensity.5

Steffen  Martus  and  Carlos  Spoerhase  have  drawn  up  a  splendid
inventory  of  symptoms  of  crisis  in  the  humanities—too  many  students  or
too  few;  too  much  specialization  or  too  little;  too  much  jargon  or  not
enough professionalism; too much or too little method; too much focus on
microscopic work or a turn away from specialized scholarship; books that
are  too  fat  or  are  fading  from  view;  retreat  into  the  ivory  tower  or
fashionable  engagement  with  contemporary  issues  and  novel  media;  too
much or too little politicization; too much interest in outside funding or too
little interest in competition; persistence or loss of the canon; and much else
besides. The only thing everyone seems to agree on is that there is a crisis, a
consensus  which  in  turn  sustains  an  entire  genre  of  academic  crisis
literature.6

Looked at more closely, permacrisis is made up of many specific crises
that  unlike  generic  sentiments  of  unease  can  be  tracked  through  hard
numbers.  And  those  numbers  suggest  there  are  objective  reasons  for
concern.  In  the  United  States,  bachelor’s  degrees  in  the  humanities  as  a
share of all such degrees rose from 9.7 percent in  1949  to  17.2 percent in
1967, dropped to 6.4 percent in 1985 and then declined from 7.6 percent in
2000  and  7.2  percent  in  2010  to  an  all-time  low  of  4.4  percent  in  2018.
Undergraduate History majors have tracked this downturn, falling from 5.7
percent in 1967 to 1.8 percent in 2014 and 1.2 percent in 2019. The share of
graduate  degrees  in  the  humanities  has  likewise  been  steadily  declining
since  around  2000.  Academic  job  openings  have  decreased  across  the
humanities as well, with a strong dip in the wake of the financial crisis of
2007/8.  Large  foundations  are  scaling  back  support  for  the  academic
humanities. Meanwhile, the academic precariat keeps growing.7

Classics  has  not  been  immune  to  any  of  these  trends.  Academic
positions, especially permanent ones, have become rarer, and some smaller
departments  have  been  closing  altogether.  The  volume  of  pertinent
publications  is  shrinking.  Adding  to  the  pressure  are  concerns  about

themselves

Eurocentrism and colonialist legacies, and about structural impediments to
equity and inclusion. While such anxieties affect many other fields, Classics
finds itself in a particularly tight corner. History or Art History, which have
privileged  certain  times  and  places  by  custom  but  not  by  necessity,  may
reorient
line  with  contemporary  preferences  while
Anthropology,  capitalizing  on  its  protean  nature,  can  effectively  reinvent
itself.  No  ready  exit  options  are  available  to  classicists.  Eurocentrism  and
colonialism irremovably weigh down a field originally designed to glorify
the  putative  cultural  ancestors  and  role  models  of  modern  Europeans,  and
wedded to language requirements that come with a long history of serving
as a potent means of exclusion.8

in

The claim to being “classical” raises the stakes in responding to changes
in  cultural  expectations;  the  credentialing  and  employment  crisis  in  the
humanities  puts  disproportionate pressure  on smaller  programs;  and social
justice  agenda  challenge  a  field  that  makes  it  harder  to  succeed  for  those
who  lack  access  to  exclusive  forms  of  preparation  such  as  instruction  in
dead  languages  in  secondary  school.  As  Sasha-Mae  Eccleston  and  Dan-el
Padilla  Peralta  put  it,  “the  pleasures  and  perversities  of  language  training
have always existed  within a political economy  of race”  (as  well  as class,
one might add).9

In this unpromising context, it is hardly surprising that conversations about
the  future  of  Classics  often  betray  a  sense  of  impending  doom.  The
pervasive  uncertainty  that  arises  from  such  anxieties  has  spawned  what  I
shall call a preservationist discourse: arguments that acknowledge problems
ranging  from  manageable  challenges  to  existential  threats  and  respond  by
proposing  strategies  of  survival.  More  often  than  not,  this  discourse  falls
short  of  calls  for  truly  transformative  change.  Rather,  the  ultimate  goal  is
the  preservation  of  the  overall  business  model—not  just  of  the  study  of
Greece  and  Rome  as  such,  but  also  of  the  underlying  organizational
template.10

As we will see, the distinction between content and template is vital, if
often elided, especially but not only in conservative interventions. It should
be  perfectly  obvious  that  no  meaningful  engagement  with  foundational

history  is  possible  without  Greece  and  Rome.  The  same  is  true  of  any
globalized  version  of  art  history,  comparative  literature,  or  philosophy.  At
the  same  time,  it  is  by  no  means  obvious  that  the  concept  and  the
established practice of Classics as an academic field are somehow required,
or  at  least  desirable  or  helpful,  to  ensure  a  strong  presence  of  the  Greco-
Roman world in any such endeavors.

The basic fact that unitary Classics as a model of engagement with that
world has long fallen by the wayside outside the Anglosphere demonstrates
without a shred of a doubt that Greece and Rome can be taught and studied
in  a  variety  of  institutional  configurations  and  contexts.  Even  so,  the
preservationist discourse either openly avers or tacitly implies that content
and template are somehow inextricably linked.

This discourse comes in five principal flavors: exceptionalist, organicist,
pragmatist, progressivist, and expansionist. While these do not necessarily
always occur in a pure form and are frequently combined, they represent the
main ingredients of preservationist reasoning and rhetoric.

Exceptionalism

Exceptionalism  is  predicated  on  the  notion  that  the  subject  matter  of
Classics  is  so  valuable—thanks  to  its  inherent  quality,  richness,  and
uniqueness  and/or  its  universal  relevance  and/or  its  contribution  to  the
“Western” heritage—that it deserves not only special attention but also, or
rather  therefore,  constitution  as  a  separate  institutionalized  field.  As  we
have seen in chapter 2, this premise lies at the very heart of the genesis of
Altertumswissenschaft or Classics.

Nevertheless,  this  postulate  suffers  from  a  number  of  non  sequiturs.
Identifying  them allows  us to  bypass a less  promising alternative strategy,
that of challenging the aforementioned value judgments. To be sure, all of
them  invite  debate:  yet  such  a  debate,  fruitful  and  indeed  necessary  as  it
may be, is not actually required for justifying or rejecting current academic
practice. For present purposes, the question is not whether these judgments
—which may strike some as correct, others as controversial, and others still
as  toxic—are  reasonable  or  true;  it  is  whether  they  can  be  used  to  sustain
the current model of Classics as a discipline.11

That is not the case. First of all, any claim to exceptionalism can only be
assessed  in  a  comparativist  fashion:  it  will  not  do  simply  to  assert  it  or
justify  it  with  reference  to  established  tradition.  In  a  perfectly  circular
motion, a priori notions of exceptionalism are used to legitimate a field that
is supposed to establish that very exceptionalism. It is worth remembering
that  more  than  200  years  ago,  Friedrich  August  Wolf  somehow  knew that
only  Greece  and  Rome  had  real  culture.  Present-day  exceptionalists  who
fail  to  back  up  their  claims  with  rigorous,  systematic  comparison  are  not
really  all  that  different.  By  its  very  nature,  however,  Classics  is  not
equipped to undertake such assessments: its spatiotemporal circumscription
militates against them. As Lorraine Daston and Glen Most note with respect
to  Classics  as  a  field  is  that  “what  is  lacking  is  a  genuinely  comparative
framework that would examine the history of diverse intellectual traditions
on an equal footing.” Then again, a Classics that adopted such a framework
would no longer be Classics but global ancient studies.12

A second non sequitur arises from the elision of the difference between
substantive  content  and  academic  organization.  This  is  a  common  fallacy:
thus,  efforts  to  alter  existing  institutional  arrangements  or  credentialing
requirements  are  liberally  misrepresented  and  slandered  as  attempts  to
abolish  or  otherwise  destroy  the  study  of  Greek  and  Roman  culture.  This
rhetorical gambit is particularly popular among political conservatives. And
not  by  chance:  these  days,  claims  to  exceptionalism  serve  as  a  rearguard
action  against  approaches  that  seek  to  decenter  European  hegemony  and
decolonize  the  curriculum.  Such  claims  have  consequently  come  to  be
associated with political conservatism (or reactionary leanings) and deeply
enmeshed with the latest round of culture wars over “wokeness” and related
concepts. They also reflect and instrumentalize the profession’s traditional
valorization of mastering Greek and Latin: manufactured outrage about the
Princeton  Classics  program’s
language
requirements provides a good example.13

recent  easing  of  ancient

That  said,  progressives  may  at  times  succumb  to  the  same  fallacy.  For
instance,  Mira  Seo  mixes  apples  and  oranges  in  her  warning  that  “It  is
difficult and certainly unpleasant for us to imagine a world without Classics

programs, a world in which students no longer learn Greek or Latin or write
dissertations on Greek and Roman topics.”14

As  has  repeatedly  been  pointed  out,  this  equation  of  form(at)  and
substance is incorrect and deeply misleading. Yet such reminders are bound
to be ignored as long as the exceptionalist camp requires any changes to the
established  format  to  be  cast  as  existential  threats  rather  than  as  the
cosmetic alterations they usually are. Anything less would make it hard to
invoke  the  imminent  demise  of  the  West  and  everything  that  is  good  and
holy.  In  a  crowded  field,  pride  of  place  belongs  to  the  New  York  Post
headline  “The  Moronic  Social-Justice  War  on  Classics  Threatens  our
Civilization.”15

In  her  detailed  account  of  recent  controversies  about  the  future  of
Classics,  Alice  Borgna  identifies  three  types  of  responses  to  efforts  to
reform  Classics:  the  “sword  drawn”  defense  of  Western  civilization  (“¡No
pasarán!”);  shrugging  it  off  (“Americans!,”  which  works  best  in  Europe);
and fear of contagion (with American craziness; ditto). That just about sums
it up.16

A third non sequitur lies in the fact that even if one wished to privilege
Eurocentric  perspectives  or  revive  a  Western  Civilization  narrative,  this
would  not  actually  require  Classics  to  exist  as  an  academic  field.  What  is
more,  there  are  better  ways  to  promote  such  preferences.  It  is  easy  to  see
why. The more Eurocentric a society’s—or education system’s—outlook is,
the  more  strongly  it  will  shape  the  teaching  of  history,  literature,  art,  and
philosophy,  and  the  more  Greece  and  Rome  will  be  viewed  as  vital
ingredients of this tradition.

This in turn obviates the need for separate Classics departments. In fact,
such  departments  make  sense  only  if  Greece  and  Rome  are  regarded  as
meaningfully  distinctive  and  detached  from  the  mainstream  of  European
development.  This,  of  course,  is  precisely  what  men  like  Humboldt  and
Wolf  believed:  that  ancient  Greece  in  particular  was  to  be  studied  on  its
own  not  only  because  it  was  so  excellent  but  because  it  was  sufficiently
remote  and  closed.  To  the  extent  that  this  idealistic  paradigm  has  faded,
engagement with Greece and Rome has been waiting to be folded into other
disciplines that concern themselves with history and culture.

From  a  stridently  Eurocentric  perspective,  Classics  departments  are
merely relics, testimony to the failure of the Western Civilization approach
to become fully normalized and hegemonic. For any true champion of the
“West,”  their  existence  ought  to  be  a  disappointment,  evidence  that  the
glories  of  Greece  and  Rome  have  been  insufficiently  embraced  by  other,
larger humanities disciplines. If this tradition is as amazing as it is said to
be,  it  should  be  spread  across  as  many  of  these  disciplines  as  possible  to
enlighten  its  scholars  and  students  and  help  fortify  them  against  the
perceived poisons of relativism and progressive change.17

In  short,  conservatives  and  reactionaries  ought  to  be  the  most  vocal
opponents of stand-alone Classics. That they are not suggests that they have
come to see Classics programs as the last remaining bastions in culture wars
that have otherwise been lost. Defending them is tantamount to admission
of overall defeat.

In more progressive circles, meanwhile, exceptionalism has spawned a new
fashion  of  faux,  make-belief  de-exceptionalization.  The  majority  of
professional classicists—just like humanities faculty more generally—tend
to be politically left of center, and some of them have emerged as the most
vocal critics of exceptionalist premises.18

For  them,  the  exclusionary  tendencies  of  their  field  are  a  source  of
anxiety,  as  is  its  name.  There  is  no  getting  around  it:  the  very  label
“Classics”  bespeaks  exceptionalism  and  elitism.  Only  in  English  does  the
field  bear  this  name.  Even  though  in  other  national  traditions  the  moniker
“classical”  (as  in  classical  philology  or  classical  archaeology)  carries  the
same connotations, free-standing “Classics” makes an ever-bolder claim to
self-evident prominence and value. (Harvard’s Department of “the Classics”
is another step up, putting potential rivals in their place.)

Originally applied to members of the highest census class of the Roman
republic,  the  second-century  CE  author  Aulus  Gellius  first  used  the  label
“classical”  to  describe  a  fellow  author  as  “high  net-worth  (classicus)  and
landowning,  not  a  proletarian.”  According  to  Salvatore  Settis,  when  the
term  reemerged  in  early  modern  Europe  it  was  once  again  attached  to
writers,  although  it  was  not  until  the  eighteenth  century  that  it  was

specifically  linked  to  Greece  and  Rome  in  order  to  denote  ideal  and
canonical  qualities.  In
the  Greco-Roman  “classical”
epitomized  the  aspirations  of  the  Romantic  program,  fusing  different
meanings—being of high value, old, established, and aesthetically superior
—into a single educational pinnacle.19

its  vagueness,

As immersion in the study of Greece and Rome was thought to improve
the  mind  in  ways  no  other  subjects  could,  Classics  became  a  marker  of
prestige not just for the subject matter but also for those who were familiar
with  it.  The  enduring  application  of  the  term  “classical”  to  matters  Greek
and  Roman  serves  as  a  reminder  that  the  shift  from  emulation  and
idealization  to  professionalized  study  has  remained  incomplete.  Yet  even
the  venerable  Ulrich  von  Wilamowitz-Moellendorff  detested  it:  “I  have
never  been  able  to  make  anything  of  the  word  ‘classical,’  which  is  an
abomination to me.”20

Since then, reservations about the term have grown more serious. Settis
worries  that  a  trivialized  understanding  of  the  classical  “is  not  only
determinedly  Eurocentric;  it  also  exactly  overlaps  the  concept  of  Western
civilization  as  superior  to  every  other  one,  and  therefore  legitimizes
colonialism’s expansionist or hegemonic policies and economic and cultural
subjugation.” For the academic field itself, its name, once a source of pride
but  now  just  a  relic  of  a  bygone  age,  is  rapidly  becoming  a  liability;  that
much  is  clear.  Shelley  Haley  already  made  that  point  back  in  1989:
“Classics is a misnomer.… It is the height of ethnocentrism to say classics
and mean only the antiquity of Greece and Rome.”21

The pressure has now risen to the point where remedial action is finally
beginning  to  be  taken,  even  if  it  has  so  far  been  limited  to  the  supremely
superficial  move  of  simply  renaming  existing  Classics  programs.  “Greek
and Roman Studies” and “Ancient Mediterranean Studies” have emerged as
popular  options. (“Ancient Studies,”  the  most flagrantly  misleading  stand-
in, remains mercifully rare.) Yet while this may deflect criticisms of elitist
labeling, it does nothing to address the underlying premise of Greco-Roman
exceptionalism.

Since  cosmetic  changes  do  not  affect  the  field’s  substance  or  practices,
they  preserve  the  Classics  template  with  little  or  no  change.  This  is  not  a

novel insight. As Dimitri Nakassis has pointed out in a scathing critique of
rebrandings,  “Renaming  our  departments  Ancient  Mediterranean  Studies
while  retaining  a  standard  Classics  curriculum  is  window  dressing,  and  a
not entirely unproblematic one at that,” given long-standing concerns about
the  cultural construction  of the  “Mediterranean.”  Twenty years  earlier, Ian
Morris already noted that “stopping being classicists and becoming instead
ancient Mediterraneanists” offers an “escape route” to those anxious about
obsolete value claims associated with Classics as a field, a ploy he deems
potentially “cynical” yet defensible.22

Krishnan  Ram-Prasad  agrees,  arguing  that  “renaming  constitutes  a
hollow  gesture  if  it  is  not  backed  up  by  action.  Classics  faculties  and
departments  rechristened  could  continue  doing  exactly  what  they  do  now,
albeit  with  an  overt  admission  that  their  focus  is  localized.  It  would  be  a
cleansing  act,  acknowledging  that  the  rest  of  the  world  exists,  but  the
supremacist  praxis  that  Classics  has  embodied  since  its  conception  would
remain  unchallenged.”  Worse  still,  according  to  Nakassis,  name  changes
“might  also  convince  (some  of)  us  that  we  have,  in  fact,  solved  the
problems of Classics without actually doing any of the work required to do
so.”23

The  proof  is  in  the  pudding.  We  must  ask  in  which  ways,  if  any,  a
department of Greek and Roman Studies or Ancient Mediterranean Studies
differs  from  a  Classics  program,  and  why  Greek  and  Roman  Studies  or
Ancient  Mediterranean  Studies  deserve  their  own  departments  and  degree
programs. If the answers turn out to be not much or not at all for the first
question,  and  to  be  the  same  employed  to  justify  Classics  for  the  second,
why  even  bother?  Nakassis  again:  such  efforts  “seem  to  be  an  attempt  to
shed the term ‘Classics’ while retaining Classics” in substance.24

At  first  blush,  Greek  and  Roman  Studies  might  seem  a  more  honest
alternative  to  Classics,  equally  narrow  yet  shorn  of  elitist  undertones.
Ancient  Mediterranean  Studies  on  the  other  hand  sounds  more  inclusive,
even  as  they  effectively  remain  centered  on  Greeks  and  Romans  while
coopting suitable neighbors as junior associates (more on this below, in my
discussion  of  expansionism).  But  what  is  the  raison  d’être  of  Ancient
Mediterranean  or  Greek  and  Roman  Studies,  as  opposed  to,  say,

Mediterranean Studies or Ancient World Studies? If the former are deemed
