worthy of a separate institutional identity because the Greeks and Romans
are  just  really  important,  influential,  special,  and  generally  awesome  in
ways  that  do  not  allow  their  traditions  to  be  properly  recontextualized
within  more  inclusive  frameworks,  we  are  back  to  square  one:  the
ostensible remedy for exceptionalism is itself resolutely exceptionalist.25

Organicism

As  we  saw  in  the  second  chapter,  notions  of  organic  unity  have  a  long
pedigree  that  goes  back  to  Romantic  Hellenomania.  Just  as  ancient  Greek
culture  was  thought  to  form  a  natural,  coherent,  indissoluble  whole,  the
same  was  to  be  true  of  contemporary  engagement  with  that  culture.  All
things Greek and Roman—language and literature, art and culture, history
and archaeology—had to be studied and taught side by side, within a single
academic program.26

What Wolf hath joined together, let not man put asunder. The organicist
imperative was presented as an intellectual stance. Yet from the beginning,
it was joined at the hip to exceptionalist claims and convictions: Greek or
Roman  culture  ought  to  be  studied  in  all  its  various  manifestations  and
remains  not  merely  because  that  was  the  best  way  to  understand  these
cultures but also and indeed above all because of their inherent greatness—
otherwise,  what  is  the  point  of  studying  them  in  all  their  various
manifestations and remains?

One might argue that over time, as resources and evidence expanded and
academic compartmentalization advanced, other early cultures received the
same treatment. But that is only partly true. Whereas Greco-Roman studies
were  the  first  to  occupy  their  very  own  and  highly  elevated  perch,  other
early  and  not  so  early  cultures  elsewhere  were  for  a  long  time  lumped
together  under  the  much  more  expansive  label  of  Oriental  Studies.  Even
now,  Middle  Eastern,  South  Asian,  or  East  Asian  Studies  programs
accommodate  far  more  heterogeneous  components  and  a  longer  timespan
than  Classics,  which  in  its  sharp  demarcations  continues  to  stand  apart.
There is simply no compelling reason why the study of Greece and Rome is
best served by, let alone requires, a self-contained organicist template.

But the real problems with organicism lie deeper. One is that by its very
nature,  the  organicist  framing  of  Classics  marginalizes  connectivity  and
hybridity  and  implies,  perhaps  even  celebrates,  cultural  autonomy  and
closedness.  Anthony  Appiah,  in  his  critique  of  the  concept  of  “Western
Civilization”—as  a  “precious  golden  nugget,  dug  out  of  the  earth  by  the
Greeks, and transferred … to Rome, where it got a good polish,” passed on
through later European societies until “finally reunited in the academies of
Europe and the  United  States”—rejects  the  “organicism” that informs  that
vision, which suffers from a number of shortcomings.

One is that there is no single catch-all concept of culture. Appiah invites
us  to  distinguish  between  two  versions,  one  that  captures  the  totality  of
institutions, capabilities, and customs of a given society, and another that is
limited  to  what  we  might  call  high  culture.  Even  as  he  allows  for  the
presence of lineages reaching back to the Greco-Roman world in domains
such as elite literature and fine arts, he cautions against overestimating their
impact: “Intellectuals like me have a tendency to suppose that the things we
care about are the most important things. I don’t say they don’t matter. But
they matter less than the story of the golden nugget suggests.”27

That  tendency  is  on  display  in  Cambridge  classicists  Mary  Beard  and
John  Henderson’s  claim  that  “So  much  of  Western  culture  turns  on
centuries  of  exploration  of  the  legacy  of  the  classical  world  that  it  lies
somewhere at the roots of pretty well all we can say, see, or think.” It almost
comes as a relief to be informed that even though “There is no other foreign
culture that is so much part of our history. This does not necessarily mean
that  whatever  belongs  within  the  traditions  of  Greece  and  Rome  is
intrinsically  superior  to  any  other  civilization.”  Not  necessarily  indeed.
Leaving aside the origins of golden nuggets, the inevitable cosmopolitanism
of  overall,  everyday  culture  defies  neat  categorization.  For  that  reason
alone,  attempts  to  fuse  different  types  of  cultures  into  constructs  of
civilizational continuity or descent are bound to fail.28

The historian Peter Stearns, in a bracing critique of backdating “Western
Civilization,”  likewise  emphasizes  the  difference  between  “high  culture”
and  “popular  culture,  religion,  and  characteristic  social  forms”  alongside
political  ideals  and  practices,  urging  us  to  avoid  “glib  assumptions”  about

civilizational continuity reaching back to antiquity because of the presence
of some legacies or “emotional reasons.” It is precisely the combination of
such presences and “emotional reasons” that accounts for the very existence
of Classics as a concept and field of study.29

that

Neither the Greco-Roman nor the “Western” traditions were or are in any
way  “organic”  in  nature.  Josephine  Quinn  has  most  recently  laid  out  that
case  in  compelling  detail.  Yet  insisting  on  a  format  of  engagement  with
Greece  and  Rome
is  simultaneously  clearly  demarcated  and
comprehensively holistic implies a degree of independence and detachment
that  echoes  Winckelmann  and  Herder’s  vision  of  Greek  purity  and
eliminative  absorption  of  outside  influences.  Although  that  vision  already
went  out  of  fashion  back  in  the  nineteenth  century  and  classicists  have  of
course long been identifying and assessing a wide array of such influences,
none of this has somehow been deemed sufficient to destabilize the unitary
model of Classics as such.30

To be fair, it is now decidedly less fashionable to wax lyrical about organic
unities than about the virtues of interdisciplinarity. At least in terms of lip
service  being  paid,  interdisciplinarity  is  commonly  considered  desirable,
held  up  as  an  antidote  to  excessive  specialization  and  its  corollaries.  So,
what  if  organicism  could  be  redeemed  after  all,  as  a  means  of  fostering
interdisciplinary  connections?  If  it  were  true  that  the  Classics  model
inspires more broadly based scholarship and teaching, that would indeed be
a strong argument in favor of the traditional template. And while it is true
that  interdisciplinarity  within  a  given  area  studies  field  may  steer  it  away
from  perspectives  that  cut  across  space  and  time,  it  does  promise  a  richer
context  for  understanding  Greek  and  Roman  matters.  When  trade-offs  are
inevitable, why not simply stick with the intradisciplinary interdisciplinarity
of Classics?31

But  this  ideal  is  only  sporadically  realized.  The  smallest  Classics
programs are perforce the most interdisciplinary, not for intellectual reasons
but out of sheer necessity: if there are only three positions, everyone has to
play the generalist. In larger programs, by contrast, those that graduate the

most  students  and  manage  the  training  of  the  next  generation  of  scholars,
interdisciplinarity often exists more in name than in practice.

There, it is possible to graduate from undergraduate programs that offer
multiple  tracks  by  studying  mostly  Greek  and  Latin,  or  none  of  it,  or
focusing on history or material culture. In those settings, interdisciplinarity
is a shell, to be polished whenever the model is in need of justification but
not by a long shot a reflection of actual practice.

Among  the  scholars  who  staff  these  programs,  the  organicist  template
serves to maintain ancestral asymmetries in status and power. The ideal of
organic  unity  might  be  an  acceptable  intellectual  foundation  if  all  parties
were equally invested in every branch of Classics. In practice that has not
been  the  case  for  many  generations,  and  not  just  because  of  intensifying
specialization.  Philologists  are  permitted  to  know  little  about  history  and
nothing  about  archaeology  whereas  “ancient”  historians  and  “classical”
archaeologists  are  commonly  expected  to  master  Greek  and  Latin  to  the
extent that they can not only understand but also teach one or both of these
languages,  preferably  at  all  levels.  This  expectation  has  long  been  a  core
principle of advanced training and academic hiring.

More often than not, the concept of holistic unity, under its shiny veneer
of inclusiveness  and interdisciplinarity, serves  to perpetuate  the  hegemony
of  language  skills  and  philological  prowess.  It  establishes  a  pecking  order
and  keeps  archaeologists  and  historians  from  escaping  the  language  trap
that we encountered in the previous chapter. In the final analysis, it serves
as  an  instrument  of  exercising  power,  weaponizing  language  requirements
either deliberately (to maintain inherited status hierarchies) or unwittingly,
by reducing access to the field.32

The  Classics  template  is  simultaneously  weak  and  strong.  It  is  weak
because  it  cannot  hope  to  overcome  specialization  that  aligns  with  larger
fields of study from history, literature, and linguistics to art and philosophy.
At the same time, it is also a powerful agent of exceptionalist conservatism
in  its  adherence  to  an  institutionalized  status  quo,  its  fetishization  of
language skills, and its lingering association with cultural purity.

Pragmatism

Pragmatic rationalizations of the Classics model focus on the mechanics of
institutional survival. The basic idea is that whatever its intellectual merits,
this  organizational  template  ought  to  be  preserved  for  practical  reasons
alone:  dissolving  Classics  programs  might,  could,  or  would  kill  off  the
study  and  teaching  of  the  subject  matter.  Without  them,  engagement  with
Greek, Latin, and Greek and Roman art, history, and thought would face a
grave threat, if not eventual extinction. Better, therefore, the devil we know.
This  line  of  reasoning  is  not  driven  by  any  recognizable  intellectual
agenda; it is political in a tactical, academic politics kind of way. That, to be
sure, does not automatically disqualify it as such: it might be a lifesaver, or
at least the least bad path on offer.

But  that  is  not  the  case.  It  takes  the  trope  of  the  permacrisis  to  new
heights  (or  lows):  not  only  are  we  in  crisis,  we  are  told,  any  substantive
reform  will  make  matters  worse.  The  non  sequitur  is  striking:  in  a  low-
pressure  environment,  preserving  the  status  quo  might  well  be  the  best
option,  whereas  crisis  calls  for  change.  The  more  serious  the  crisis,  the
more change is needed.

Equally  importantly,  the  underlying  premise  is  fatally  flawed.  Are  we
really to believe that without Classics departments, nobody would study or
teach  Greco-Roman  literature  or  history  or  art  or  philosophy?  Surely  not:
that  is  what  programs  in  (Comparative  or  World)  Literature,  History,  Art
History,  and  Philosophy  are  for.  If  anything,  the  existence  of  separate
Classics departments discourages them from including Greece and Rome in
their offerings.

The  don’t-rock-the-boat  approach  is  so  parochial  as  to  be  downright
silly.  The  study  of  Greece  and  Rome  manifestly  does  not  depend  on
Classics programs: just take a look at the universities of continental Europe,
with  their  programs  of  Greco-Roman  classical  philology,  Greco-Roman
ancient history, and Greco-Roman classical archaeology. A generation ago,
the  annual  Classics
about
bibliography  L’Année  Philologique  were  written  in  French,  German,  and
Italian.  In  much  of  the  world,  the  study  of  Greece  and  Rome  is  alive  and
well with nary a Classics department in sight.33

two-thirds  of  all  publications

listed

in

None  of  this  is  a  secret.  So,  what  drives  the  pragmatist  argument?
Looked at more closely, we must wonder to what extent it is rooted, once
again,  in  an  old  acquaintance:  the  privileging  of  language  skills  within
Classics.  In  2022,  the  eminent  Simon  Goldhill,  not  exactly  known  as  a
particularly conservative classicist, declared that “Becoming a classicist is a
privilege,  for  anyone.…  Learning  to  write  Latin  and  Greek  verse  was  a
privilege, a considerable privilege, given to me.… The teaching is in me, in
my body in how I react to the rhythm of ancient poetry.” This is as good an
example as any of the persistent congruence of the imageries of privileged
content (“GreeceandRome”), privileged skill (mastery of ancient Greek and
Latin), and privileged status (credentialed classicist).34

Cui  bono?  Classics  is  anomalous  among  humanities  fields,  which  are
usually  organized  around  subject  matter  and  scholarly  approaches  rather
than  languages as such. This  is  also  true  of other  area  studies  fields: even
though  Chinese  and  Arabic  are  central  to  East  Asia  and  Middle  Eastern
Studies,  they  are  not  as  hegemonic  as  Greek  and  Latin  are  in  Classics.  In
university settings, languages can be, and routinely are, taught by adjuncts
or other non-tenure-track faculty. That this practice long predates the recent
expansion of the academic precariat suggests that it is not the by-product of
neoliberal  late  capitalism  and  some  such  boogeyman  but  a  sustainable
feature of humanities teaching and learning. There is no compelling reason
why language  acquisition  and instruction  should  occupy a central  position
in tenure-line faculty appointments, as it has traditionally done in Classics.
After  all,  centuries  of  privilege  have  ensured  that  the  practice  of  teaching
Greek and Latin is extremely well established. Unlike for, say, Sumerian or
Classic Mayan, the requisite skills are not limited to a tiny set of specialists.
Yet Classics has long, consciously, and even proudly been an outlier by
centering  language  skills.  This  tradition  is  so  powerful  that  unflattering
distinctions  may  still  be  drawn  between  (“real”)  Classics  and  (watered-
down) Classical Studies, the former heavy on Greek and Latin and little or
none for the latter. Even so, the downsides of language weaponization and
fetishization are rarely addressed head-on in Classics circles.35

This is a feature, not a bug. Classics depends on it. Without it, classical
language teaching could (ideally) be reserved for faculty with the necessary

background in the pedagogy of language instruction, or otherwise (if need
be)  outsourced  to  lecturers  and  adjuncts,  along  the  lines  of  instruction  in
modern  languages.  Without  it,  historians  and  archaeologists  of  the  Greco-
Roman  world  would  be  liberated  from  unnecessary  and  discriminatory
expectations.  Classical  philologists  are  the  only  ones  who  stand  to  lose:
were  Classics  programs  to  be  dismantled,  they  would  need  to  prove  their
worth to literature programs instead of enjoying the luxury of falling back
on repetitive language instruction.

At  the  end  of  the  day,  the  demise  of  traditional  Classics  departments
would simply be the end of unearned, inherited privilege for the few. Rather
than  a  clever  ploy  to  save  the  study  of  Greece  and  Rome,  the  pragmatist
argument is a classic(al) case of the tail wagging the dog: the desire to keep
established intradisciplinary hierarchies and inequities in place trumps calls
for transformative change no matter how serious the situation.

One might object that the don’t-rock-the-boat approach might work in a
narrow  sense,  especially
in  an  environment  of  cost-cutting  and
consolidation where a retreat from the Classics model might indeed lead to
retrenchment  and  net  losses.  But  whether  or  not  that  is  correct,  this
observation is shortsighted: insistence on a status quo under siege can only
delay the eventual reckoning.

Classics  as  an  organizational  template  is  defenseless  in  the  face  of
consolidation.  Broader  skills—humanities  perspectives,  interpretive  skills,
not  to  mention  the  old  canard  of  critical  thinking—are  generic  and  their
transmission  does  not  rely  on  a  particular  configuration  of  educational
institutions.  Students  who  are  interested  in  Greek  and  Roman  matters  can
derive information and skills from platforms such as History, Anthropology,
Art History, and other programs. Some of these will either survive or will
themselves be folded into larger units. How Greece and Rome will fare in
this  context  will  depend  on  how  they  present  themselves  to  students  and
administrators, but not on maintaining purist separation.36

In  the  end,  Classics  as  we  know  it  may  prove  most  resilient  at  the
wealthiest  institutions  of  the  Anglosphere.  In  the  most  extreme  cases,
dedicated  endowed  funds  may  keep  it  alive  indefinitely.  In  the  meantime,
pragmatist  defenses  will  be  breached  elsewhere.  That  in  turn  will  further

increase the gap between how things are done at those places that produce
the  most  competitive  PhDs  and  how  things  are  done  everywhere  else,
including  their  future  places  of  employment.  It  seems  fanciful  to  imagine
that  the  Classics  model  could  absorb  and  withstand  the  resultant  frictions
and tensions.

The pragmatic rationale for Classics is bereft of intellectual ambition and
fearful, even cynical in its preoccupation with preserving the status quo: it
is  an  approach  in  which  the  ends  justify  the  means.  It  is  also  likely  to  be
self-defeating: instead of actively trying to secure the future of the study of
Greece and Rome by embracing change, it doubles down on an antiquated
format. All this is likely to accomplish over the long run is that changes will
be made for rather than by classicists.37

Progressivism

Progressive  voices  in  Classics  question  or  reject  traditional  notions  of
Greco-Roman  exceptionalism.  They  also  note  the  potential  for  redefining
the field, and sometimes claim that it is already in the process of reforming
or even reinventing itself in ways that confront its historical complicity in
Eurocentrism and white supremacy, imperialism and colonialism, classism
and sexism. This cautiously optimistic perspective is pithily encapsulated in
the  statement  of  the  Faculty  of  Classics  page  for  the  “Oxford  &
Colonialism”  project,  which,  after  listing  forays  into  connectivity  and
reception  studies  and  postcolonial  readings,  declares  that  “We  still  have  a
long  way  to  go  in  making  a  subject  with  a  deeply  colonial  past  into  a
discipline fit for a new era, but we are on our way.”38

Just as proponents of the previous three approaches, progressives are not
immune  to  the  familiar  conflation  of  the  study  and  teaching  of  Greek  and
Roman matters with the existence of the field of Classics. Consider Donna
Zuckerberg’s  manifesto  “Burn  It  All  Down?”  of  2019:  “Is  there  any  hope
for  Classics?  Can  we  save  the  soul  and  future  of  a  discipline  that  has
historically been implicated in fascism and colonialism—one that continues
to be enmeshed in white supremacy and misogyny—in the face of declining
enrollments,  the  student  debt  crisis,  and  the  increasing  precarity  of  the
academic  labor  force?  … There’s ample  reason  to  despair. Yet,  in  spite  of

all of that, people do keep studying Classics. They find ways to find value
and meaning and fun and joy in that study. And there are exciting signs of
growth  in  the  field,  with  projects  like  the  brand-new  Asian  and  Asian
American  Classical  Caucus.  The  people  involved  in  these  kinds  of
initiatives  are  keen  to  articulate  a  vision  for  studying  Classics  that  is  vital
and necessary and radical.”39

The  implication,  rarely  spelled  out,  is  Classics  can  be  saved  and/or
deserves  to  be  saved  if,  or  only  if,  it  changes  enough  to  take  account  of
contemporary cultural and political sensibilities. Interventions  in  print  and
online  tend  to  put  considerable  weight  on  practical  features  such  as
curricular  reform,  diversification  of  training  and  hiring,  adjustment  of
professional  expectations  regarding  teaching,  research  and  promotion,  and
organizing among interest groups.

None  of  this  offers  an  easy  way  out.  For  example,  one  would  need  to
make  sure  that  efforts  to  bring  about  inclusionary  diversification  among
students  and  academics  are  undertaken  for  the  sake  of  reforming  the  field
rather than merely to improve the odds of preserving it—the latter reflected
in  Mira  Seo’s  observation  that  “demographic  change  is  necessary  to  the
field’s survival.” Eccleston and Padilla Peralta call out the “deflating” fact
that  outreach  into  minorities  may  be  instrumentalized  for  the  end  of
disciplinary survival, more out of self-interest rather than a desire for actual
change:  “Terrified  by  the  prospect  of  its  demise,  the  field  has  recently
turned to hoarding Black, Brown, and Yellow bodies to save itself.”40

Pragmatic progressives stress the importance of opening up the field by
reducing  ancient  language  requirements.  As  Max  Goldman  and  Rebecca
Futo  Kennedy  point  out,  in  resource-restricted  institutions  Classics  is
already executing a shift from excessive emphasis on such language skills
—designed  to  prepare  students  to  become  professional  classicists  by
gaining  access  to  graduate  programs—to  greater  eclecticism  in  terms  of
outlook  and  content,  such  as  inclusion  of  materials  beyond  Greece  and
Rome, reception studies, critical engagement with the legacies of the field,
and contributions to interdepartmental programs.41

Such  revisions  go  far  beyond  faux-progressive  relabeling,  especially
traditions,  exclusionary

they  actively  confront  exceptionalist

when

practices,  and  their  reactionary  corollaries.  Yet  in  the  end,  such  initiatives
face  two  big  questions:  Can  these  efforts  redeem  Classics  as  an
institutionalized template for engaging with ancient Greece and Rome; and
if  reforms  are  taken  far  enough  to  satisfy  current  concerns  about  access,
equity, and historical baggage, would they leave any room or need for that
template?

The  standard  response  to  charges  that  Classics  may  have  been
irreparably  damaged  by  its  association  with  assorted  historical  ills  is  to
point out that it has always been made to serve widely divergent goals and
aspirations. Goldhill offers a representative rundown—Classics as a vehicle
of imperialism, colonialism, racism, sexism, and classism on the one hand,
and as inspiration for revolutionary change in a variety of domains on the
other—concluding that “So while it is demonstrably false that the study of
classical  literature  and  culture  has  been  or  is  inherently  or  necessarily
associated  solely  with  political,  social  or  cultural  conservatism,  the
institutions and practices of the discipline, as with society as a whole, must
do  a  lot  more  work  to  change  if  they  are  not  now  to  be  open  to  such
accusations.  Classics  opens  the  door  to  an  other  place,  a  place  often
idealised, which creates a space for the imagination of a better world: this
can  be  a  conservative  rejection  of  the  modern  world,  it  can  be  a
revolutionary charge to change the modern world. The tension between the
two trajectories together drives the rich history of the discipline.”42

A complementary, more evasive strategy appears in the inaugural text of
the so-called Postclassicisms Collective, a group of nine scholars of Greek
and Roman literature at elite universities. Their claim to “post”-ness seems
doubtful:  while  they  claim  to  be  critical  of  the  established  tradition  of
Classics,  they  do  not  challenge  the  concept  of  the  “classical”  as  such.
Moreover,  their  approach  is  strikingly  exclusionary:  not  only  do  they
question  the  application  of that label  to  classical  traditions  outside  Greece
and  Rome,  they  distinguish  classicists  from  “others  who  study  the  Greco-
Roman  world  (philosophers,  archaeologists,  political  theorists,  etc.)”—
suggesting that while historians are in (unless they are among the “etc’s”),
material culture is out.43

They  seek  to  defend  this  at  once  truncated  and  exclusive  version  of
Classics  as  a  unit  of  academic  engagement  and  organization  by  arguing,
following Edward Said, that what matters is not the “what” but the “how”:
“This is at one level a reassuring message for classicists: the issue is not the
subject itself but the way we approach it.”44

But that misses the point. Classics is defined both by its subject matter
and by the way it selects and approaches it, that is, by centering Greece and
Rome and by bundling different branches of study in a specific manner. The
very  concept  of  Classics  fuses  content  and  approach.  There  is  no  obvious
way  of  escaping  that  amalgamation,  of  separating  the  “what”  from  the
“how.”

Balance sheets of reactionary and progressive legacies or false distinctions
between  form  and  substance  beg  the  real  question—whether  given  its
genesis, Classics can truly hope to shed its baggage or whether we might be
better  off  moving  on  and  adopting  different  formats  of  engaging  with
Greece and Rome.

Even overtly activist interventions may shy away from this conundrum.
Take  Mathura  Umachandran’s  statement  that  “Decolonization,  for  me,
doesn’t mean turning my back on antiquity altogether. Rather I stay within
Classics because of the radical potential there is in picking up the master’s
tools for dismantling injustice not only in the academy, but beyond it too.”
Yet  turning  our  back  on  Classics  is  not  the  same  as  turning  our  back  on
antiquity.45

In the end, progressives need to come to terms with the possibility that
Classics as a field may have been constituted in a way that prevents it from
being  reformed  in  what  they  would  consider  politically  desirable  ways—
that its exclusionary  uses in  past  and present may  be a feature,  not  a bug;
that,  in  Katherine  Blouin’s  words,  the  field’s  “structural  issues  are  a
testimony to how this field’s Euro-American hegemonic vortex is woven in
a myopic, self-sabotaging fabric that serves the status quo.”46

With characteristic clarity, Krishnan Ram-Prasad puts his finger on this:

In  Classics,  however,  we  still  have  a  pathological,  inherent  focus  on
region that other subjects do not. Classics just is European, in a way that
History,  Philosophy,  Art,  Music,  Literature,  and  Politics  aren’t.  These
criticisms may seem obvious, but the solutions certainly aren’t. We can
keep tweaking  at  the  edges, as we  are  doing  now. These approaches  to
decolonization often go hand in hand with curriculum-independent anti-
racism: bring more people from underrepresented communities into the
field;  promote  the  scholarship  of  those  from  outside  rich  Western
institutions;  teach  students  to  think  critically  about  social  issues  when
reading classical texts. It goes without saying that these are all causes we
should be fighting for wholeheartedly.

But diversifying our staff and reforming our courses to address issues
of race and gender, while keeping the locus of our cultural reference the
same, is not enough. Those from historically marginalized communities
will continue to be marginalized.

White  Western  scholars  will  still  be  the  “inheritors”  of  the  great
tradition,  while  people  of  color  will  remain  outsiders,  graciously
afforded  a  look-in.  Our  ancestors  barely  exist  in  this  ancient  “world,”
and  when  they  do  appear  in  the  text,  it  is  always  in  some  notoriously
warped way.

There is an extent to which this marginalization is just the nature of
the texts, and that’s a hard truth. But another hard truth is that it is not an
accident  that  these  are  valorized  as  The  Classics,  while  texts  with
legitimate representation for people of color don’t make the cut. Today,
this  selective  recognition  constitutes  a  systematic  and  deliberate
exclusion that is not present elsewhere in the humanities.47

As  a  result,  progressive  classicists  seem  torn  between  optimistic,
gradualist  and  disaffected,  disruptionist  visions  of  change.  Johanna
Hanink’s  2021  piece  is  emblematic  of  these  tensions:  (re)titled  “A  New
Path  for  Classics,”  its  URL  reads:  “if-classics-doesn’t-change-let-it-burn.”
This  ambiguity  faithfully  reflects  that  of  Hanink’s  approach:  while
encouraged  by  the  fact  that  “a  new  generation  of  classicists  is  becoming
better in tune with the world’s shifting realities,” she “would rather see the

current  incarnation  of  classics  burn  than  fossilize,  …  eager  for  a  fire  that
will make way for healthy new growth.”48

What is left unclear is whether Classics as a field would still exist after
the  fire,  or  whether  it  would  have  been  replaced  by  some  alternative
configuration.  The  same  open-endedness  is  visible  in  Padilla  Peralta’s
rejection of “demolition for demolition’s sake” while asking why we should
not  keep  “all  options  for  reparative  intellectual  justice—including  the
demolition  of  the  discipline  itself—on  the  table.”  Or,  in  Sarah  Scullin’s
words, “I tend to consider ‘burning it all down,’ at least when I invoke it, as
a  hyper-rational  response.…  ‘Burn  it  all  down’  means,  to  me,  ‘let’s  start
from  the  ground  up  and  stop  trying  to  polish  a  turd.’  ”  In  all  these
interventions, the feasibility or desirability of Classics surviving as a field is
very much up in the air.49

And  that  is  as  it  should  be.  We  cannot  prejudge  the  issue  of  whether
drastic  changes  would  preserve  or  dismantle  the  established  format.  Take
Umachandran’s  comprehensively  radical-utopian  vision  of  an  antiracist
Classics that requires activist commitments, centers race and social justice
concerns,  incorporates  the  requisite  tools  and  resources  into  the  field,
restructures  credentialing  requirements,  and  seeks  impact  on  the  outside
world. Would there be any compelling reason to define and configure such
transformed  engagement  with  Greece  and  Rome  and  their  legacies  and
appropriations as “Classics?”50

The  more  profoundly  Classics  changes,  the  fewer  reasons  there  are  to
conceive  of  it  as  Classics.  The  more  ancient  language  training  is  being
decentered in the name of access, equity, and inclusion, the less need there
is to corral all students of Greece and Rome into the Classics fold. This is
not  a  trivial  issue:  even  moderate  critiques  question  the  centrality  of
language or that centrality’s continuing viability.51

At  which  point  will  scholars  who  engage  with  various  aspects  of  the
Greco-Roman  world  and  its  varied  afterlives  no  longer  have  enough  in
common  to  share  an  academic  home  and  be  better  off  elsewhere?  By
challenging foundational tenets of Classics such as Western exceptionalism
and exclusionary requirements that curtail access, a genuinely decolonizing

agenda can only  bring  us closer to  that point. It  is  more  likely  to  dissolve
the field than to save it.

Expansionism

Much the same is true of expansionist strategies of preservationist reform.
In that mode, Classics branches out beyond its ancestral territory of ancient
Greece  and  Rome  in  order  to  broaden  its  foundations  and  appeal  and
thereby  increase  its  chances  of  survival.  Expansion  can  be  temporal  or
spatial in nature, or indeed both.52

Temporal expansion may look back in time, seeking to connect Greece
and  Rome  to  historical  antecedents:  in  Shelley  Haley’s  pithy  injunction,
“Classicists  must  acknowledge
roots  of  ancient
Mediterranean  civilization.”  It  is  also  forward-looking,  if  unevenly  so.
Witness  the  gradual  expansion  into  an  increasingly  extended  “Late
Antiquity,”  which  has  been  challenging  traditional  academic  boundaries
drawn  between  the  study  of  Greece  and  Rome  and  the  study  of  early
Christianity, and between “ancient” and “medieval” (or “Islamic”).53

the  Afroasiatic

At  the  same  time,  Anthony  Kaldellis’s  more  ambitious  plea  for  the
inclusion of Byzantium within Greco-Roman studies seems a bridge too far,
notwithstanding  the  incontrovertible  facts  that  cut-off  points  between
Classics and Byzantine Studies “are not helpful and are a recent invention”
and  that  the  Byzantines,  who  thought  of  themselves  as  Romans,  were
simply part of a “long Roman history,” “Byzantium marks no rupture in the
history of Greek literature,” and “classicists live in a Byzantine house made
from  a  careful  Byzantine  selection  of  ancient  materials.”  There  is  no
rational answer to his question that if Greece and Rome can be studied with
a  highly  specific  set  of  skills,  why  is  Byzantium  excluded  if  it  can  be
studied  in  exactly  the  same  way  and  with  the  same  skills  and  linguistic
training?  For  once,  “expertise,”  however  narrowly  defined,  fails  as  a
legitimation device for disciplinary exclusion.54

Classicists,  however,  have  set  their  eyes  on  targets  closer  to  home.
Instead  of  bringing  an  additional  thousand  years’  worth  of  Romans  into
their  fold,  they  prefer  to  leapfrog  straight  into  the  modern  age.  Classical
reception  studies,  which  explore  the  myriad  ways  in  which  Greek  and

Romans  materials  have  been  used  in  recent  centuries,  have  long  been  a
major growth area.

The underlying appeal is easy to grasp: modern engagement with Greek
and  Roman  matters,  whether  by  intellectuals  or  in  popular  culture,  is  an
attractive object of study for academics trained and employed to teach and
research  Greek  and  Roman  matters.  Better  still,  there  is  a  lot  of  original
work  to  be  done,  work  that  remains  unburdened  by  the  constraints  of
disciplinary traditions.

But  does  any  of  this  legitimate  Classics  as  an  academic  field?  The
answer is no. For one, it is not necessary to undergo conventional Classics
training in order to enter classical reception studies: in that arena, intimate
familiarity with the finer points of the athematic aorist subjunctive will not
get  you  very  far.  While  reception  studies  undoubtedly  require  familiarity
with  ancient  materials,  they  rarely  do  so  to  the  extent  that  professional
credentialing  conventions  dictate,  and  not  at  all  in  terms  of  linguistic
proficiency:  nobody  needs  to  be  able  to  teach  ancient  Greek  or  Latin  in
order  to  investigate  the  reception  of  Greek  and  Roman  materials  in  more
recent  contexts.  In  this  respect,  classical  reception  studies  transcend  and
supersede Classics instead of preserving or strengthening it.

For another, on top of making certain traditional Classics skills less vital,
reception  studies  render  others  more  important.  Reception  studies  fall
squarely  into  the  domain  of  a  number  of  other  fields,  such  as  intellectual
history,  art  history,  cultural  studies,  media  studies,  comparative  literature,
and national literature fields. The more seriously we take reception studies,
the more essential a solid grounding in one or more of these fields becomes
—a grounding that Classics programs are in no position to provide.55

At  the  end  of  the  day,  expertise  in  the  time  and  place  where  the
engagement  with  Greek  and  Roman  materials  took  place  is  at  least  as
important  as,  and  arguably  even  more  important  than,  expertise  in  the
materials  themselves,  let  alone  in  their  original  overall  context.  Classical
reception  studies  are  at  least  as  much  of  an  invitation  to  historians  and
others  to  look  back  to  antiquity  as  they  are  to  classicists  to  direct  their
attentions to modern societies.

The  question  is  not  whether  traditionally  trained  classicists  have
successfully engaged with reception studies: there is no denying that some
have. What matters here is that classical reception studies do not depend on
the  existence  of  Classics  as  a  field.  Nor  do  they  offer  support  for  the
Classics  template:  they  are  just  as  compatible,  and  in  some  cases  perhaps
even more so, with the distribution of expertise regarding Greece and Rome
across  different  disciplines  and  departments.  The  ideal  classical  reception
scholar  might  well  be  someone  with  a  sufficient  background  in  Greco-
Roman  studies  who  is  based  in  a  disciplinary  humanities  program  that
focuses on art, history, or literature more generally.

While reception studies may boost interest in Greek and Roman culture,
they will not “save” Classics as a field. And that may well be for the best:
there  is  a  sinister  aspect  to  the  preservationist  instrumentalization  of
reception  studies.  If  rooted  in  the  Classics  tradition,  classical  reception
studies are inherently conservative in their focus on Greece and Rome. In a
way, they even help perpetuate or revive a familiar hegemonic centering of
these  two  cultures.  Greeks  and  Romans  already  “colonized”  later  periods
first through the transmission of a stabilizing canon and then thanks to the
indefatigable efforts of humanists and Romantics; there is no need for them
to  do  so  again,  this  time  through  classicists  in  pursuit  of  instances  of
reception.

A  particularly  striking  example  of  that  pursuit  is  the  ever-expanding
interest  in  the  uses  both  European  colonizers  and  Indigenous  Americans
made of Greek and Roman traditions. That this work unfolds in the context
of an academic environment in which the rich histories of the preconquest
Americas have long been systematically cut out of most disciplines leaves a
bitter  aftertaste:  for  classicists,  the  Americas  only  begin  to  exist  once
Columbus  has  set  foot  there,  opening  up  new  venues  for  the  recycling  of
Greek and Roman ideas. The contrast to a global engagement with ancient
cultures along the lines this book envisions could not be more striking.

Spatial  expansion  of  Classics  tends  to  target  the  immediate  neighbors  of
Greeks  and  Romans,  most  notably  those  in  North  Africa  and  Southwest
Asia,  from  Carthaginians  and  Egyptians  to  Hebrews,  Phoenicians,  and

Iranians.  Connectivity  and  hybridity  that  contextualize  Greek  and  Roman
developments are the guiding lights: as Bernal put it a generation ago, “This
type of work and its emphasis on the stimulus that ethnic mixture gives to
cultural  creativity  would  put  classics,  or  a  new  combined  discipline  of
‘Ancient  Studies,’  right  at  the  center  of  the  education  and  politics  of  this
country as it begins to face up to its multiethnic nature.”56

Geographical  broadening  may  occur  on  different  scales.  Coopting
directly  adjacent  cultures  upgrades  the  field  to  what  we  might  call
Classics+. The metrocentric, even annexationist character of this strategy is
captured  with  revealing  clarity  by  the  communiqué  “What  is  Classics?”
issued  by  the  Board  of  Directors  of  the  Society  for  Classical  Studies  in
September  2023:  while  Greece  and  Rome  “remain  a  major  focus,”  “the
boundaries  of  our  discipline  continue  to  expand.  For  example,  some
classicists  now  take  global  or  comparative  views  of  Mediterranean
antiquity, looking at Egypt, Persia, Ethiopia, Carthage, and the many other
premodern cultures with whom the Greeks and Romans engaged.”57

Reaching  out  into  Central  Asia  and  South  Asia  yields  Classics++,
especially  when  an  emphasis  on  connectivity  (usually  via  long-distance
trade) and diasporas (such as much-discussed Greek and Roman presences
in Afghanistan and coastal India) once again casts the newcomers above all
as  participants  in  Greco-Roman  affairs  rather  than  as  objects  of  study  in
their own right. This approach comes with built-in limits: thus, giving due
weight to East Asia would explode the Classics template. Even the popular
business  of  “Silk  Road  Studies”  could  not  force  early  China  into  an
in  what  remains  at  heart  a  Mediterranean-centered
auxiliary  role
enterprise.58

On  the  whole,  expansionist  moves  prioritize  connectivity.  While
Classics+  may  focus  equally  strongly  on  both  temporal  and  spatial
connections,  the  more  ambitious  outreach  of  Classics++  tends  to  favor
synchronous spatial connectivity that manifests in the movement of people,
goods,  and  ideas.  Thus,  the  bolder  the  scheme,  the  more  it  ends  up
reinforcing the tyranny of absolute chronology at the expense of diachronic
comparative perspectives.59

But  that  is  a  problem  only  if  we  are  interested  in  working  toward
creating  a  level  playing  field  for  open-ended  engagement  with  ancient
cultures.  By  contrast,  if  the  overarching  goal  is  the  preservation  of  some
form  of  Classics,  such  limitations  not  only  make  perfect  sense  but  are  in
fact indispensable. Without them, there would be no obvious way to contain
spatiotemporal  expansion  within  bounds  that  safeguard  the  centrality  of
Greece  and  Rome.  Otherwise,  we  might  end  up  with  a  perspective  that
reduces
in  multimillennial  and
to  mere  episodes
multicontinental  ancient  histories.  That,  needless  to  say,  would  be  the  end
of  Classics  as  we  know  it:  the  field  would  have  been  transcended  and
supplanted rather than reformed and reinvigorated.

those  cases

Jacques  Bromberg’s  concept  of  “Global  Classics”  exemplifies  this
preservationist  agenda.  More  accurately
labeled  (as  he  also  does)
“globalized  Classics,”  it  promotes  “methods  of  classical  research  and
scholarship  that  are  dedicated  to  the  study  of  globalizing  processes,
systems,  and  structures  in  and  since  antiquity  and  to  the  study  of  the
globalization of classics itself as a discipline.” This envisions a field that in
its  focus  on  Greece  and  Rome  can  never  become  truly  “global,”  only
to  cross-cultural  contextualization  and
“globalized”  with
transnational academic practice. This distinction is not trivial pedantry: we
must not forget that Classics, by its very nature, can never be meaningfully
“global.” As Fábio Morales observes, “Bromberg’s Global Classics is still,
above all, Classics.”60

respect

Any  “Global  Classics”  that  deserves  that  name  needs  to  abandon  its
ancient  Mediterranean  anchor  in  favor  of genuine  multilaterality.  This  can
be accomplished by identifying “classical” traditions (in textual and visual
formats) in different parts of the world and approaching them side by side.
Doing so allows us to acknowledge the privileged position certain bodies of
thought have occupied in various regional traditions without centering any
one of them. Placed alongside classical Chinese or Sanskrit canons, to name
just two examples, Greek and Roman “classics” would then be interpreted
and contextualized as species of a larger genus.61

Executed  without  reservations,  this  approach  effectively  dissolves
Greco-Roman  “Classics”  by  transferring  and  incorporating  its  traditional

core  elements  into  broader  structures  of  teaching  and  research.  Seo
describes  a  concrete  example  of  this  cross-cultural,  comparative  approach
as  it  was  practiced  at  the  Yale–National  University  Singapore  College  (an
experimental  entity  that  was  unfortunately  disestablished  in  2021).  In  that
program, team-teaching “classical” texts from different parts of Afroeurasia
not only disabused the scholars involved of notions of mastery; “[i]t has led
us  to  see  the  field  of  Classics  as  comprising  not  only  the  study  of
Mediterranean  culture,  but  also  of  the  ancient  cultures  of  Asia,  India,  and
inclusivity,
In  highlighting  multiple  benefits—resilience,
Africa.”
accommodation  of  diverse  student  interests  in  a  cosmopolitan  setting,  and
historians,
the
archaeologists,  and  scholars  of  religion”—and  calling  for  collaborative
teaching and shared faculty lines, Seo offers a blueprint for a viable “Global
Classics.”62

philosophers,

collaboration

“literary

critics,

Yet  even

to  escape

the
this  forward-looking  concept  struggles
gravitational  pull  of  the  preservationist  rationale.  When  Seo,  addressing
fellow  classicists,  opines  that  “Globalizing  Classics  may  ensure  our
profession’s  evolution  and  survival,”  the  distinction  between  the  field  of
Classics  and  the  study  of  Greece  and  Rome  remains  unclear.  The  same  is
true of her conclusion that “Antiquity can be part of the future, but only if
we accept that our place in academia isn’t a birthright. In order to survive,
we must make Classics as capacious and multiethnic as the ancient worlds
we study.”63

Are  we  still  talking  about  Classics  as  a  field  centered  on  Greece  and
Rome,  or  have  we  already  left  it  behind  in  favor  of  cross-cultural
engagement with foundational traditions? The survivalist impulse muddies
the waters, obscuring the fact that tough choices about the reorganization of
teaching and research would have to be made.

This impulse also shines through in the proposals of the Postclassicisms
Collective.  The  traditional  centrality  of  philology  is  reflected  in  their
insistence on “deep, immersive reading” as well as “horizontal, comparative
reading”  across  Demotic,  Coptic,  Hebrew,  Ethiopic,  Syriac,  Sanskrit,
Persian,  and  Arabic—all  of  which  are  simply  languages  or  in  some  cases
can  be  taken  to  signify  different  classical  canons.  Such  reading  “enriches

and  broadens  our  sense  of  the  classical”  by  helping  decenter  Greece  and
Rome.  At the  same  time,  Greco-Roman  Classics  itself is  meant  to  persist:
“Does  ‘world  literature’  represent  a  road  not  (yet)  taken  for  classical
studies? It is hard to speculate counterfactually about an alternative scenario
in which Greek and Latin might have been sheltered under the umbrella of
world literature rather than singled out for exceptionalism.” Yet there is no
apparent  interest  in  thinking  about  whether  this  could  or  should  happen
now.64

The  collective  also  appears  to  be  confused  about  the  politics  of  the
expansion they are calling for. On the one hand, they position Greece and
Rome as the core when they invoke Greek influences in Northern India and
sub-Saharan  Africa.  On  the  other,  they  claim  to  reject  the  “hub-and-
periphery model of classicism.” Their suggestion that a place like Palmyra
was not more or less “classical” than Carthage or Antioch but that “it was
differently  so”  further  adds  to  that  confusion.  What  does  this  label  even
mean, or contribute?65

In the end, all we are left with is a spruced-up version of the status quo:
“While much exciting scholarship has been generated by scholars working
in  multiple  cultural  and  linguistic  traditions,  it  simply  is  not  true  that  a
pupating classical studies is about to grow wings and fly from the study of
Greek and Roman antiquity. Many of us may, for sure, envisage a future in
which the ancient world is taught in a more holistic and mobile way. But for
now, classics cannot deny, and must face up to, its historically determined
ties to the Mediterranean peninsulae.”66

That is of course absolutely true. Moderate, incremental expansion that
is firmly rooted in the existing organizational and institutionalized template
of Greco-Roman Classics will never diminish the strength of those ties. In
real life, such changes are bound to be ones that are easiest to implement,
and may therefore seem superficially appealing. But they are also the most
hypocritical.  The  more  limited  the  field’s  expansion  beyond  its  ancestral
cores,  the  more  Greece  and  Rome  will  preserve  their  hegemonic  position.
Just  how  much  does  an  “Ancient  Mediterranean  World”  department  with
ten  scholars  of  Greece  and  Rome  and  one  or  two  others  differ  from  a
standard  Classics  program?  In  Dimitri  Nakassis’s  frank  assessment,  “An

‘ancient  Mediterranean  studies’  department  that  remains  mostly  Greco-
Roman is, to put it bluntly, a joke.”67

And  the  joke  is  not  even  funny  if  classicists  end  up  colonizing  their  twin
champions’ less prominent neighbors in order to shore up their own field.
Even  as  that  might  not  be  the  intended  effect,  it  is  nevertheless  a  likely
consequence.  Quinn  puts  her  finger  on  this  problem:  “The  Institute  for
Classical Studies, for instance, now promotes research into ‘the cultures of
the ancient Mediterranean and neighbouring lands.’ ” The eminent scholar
of ancient Iran, Pierre Briant, is more forceful: “We have understood: ‘the
Orient’  does  not  exist  in  itself.  The  space  that  defines  it  is  variable  and
adaptable  according  to  the  territorial  ambitions  of  the  Greeks,  or,  if  you
will, according to the depth of the anabasis that their leaders imagine they
can carry out. As it is presented through such a program, ‘the Orient of the
Greeks’ is a pure construction, of a type denounced in his time by Edward
Saïd. In the established couple of Greece/Orient, there is a full-fledged actor
and an extra.”68

This  is  notably  not  just  a  matter  of  labeling  West  Asia  or  Egypt  the
“Orient”;  it  is  about  fundamental  orientations  and  perspectives.  The  net
result  is  to  uphold  what  Briant,  quoting  Danielle  Nottara-Minne,  rightly
dismisses  as  “the  very  old-fashioned  appellation  ‘ancient  history’  ”—the
one  that  “includes  other  continents  only  insofar  as  they  participate  in  the
dynamics of European history, and moves towards the Mediterranean when
it is configured either as the cradle or as the threatened frontier of Western
civilization.”69

How do scholars of neighboring cultures view this situation? That they
are  experiencing  similar  anxieties  about  survival  does  not  mean  that  they
consider  Classics  a  potential  savior.  Thus,  the  Egyptologist  Juan  Carlos
Moreno García notes that the waning of Eurocentrism puts pressure on an
Egyptology that has in part been valued for its perceived link to the Greco-
Roman  world  and  thence  to  later  Europe.  What  is  to  be  done?  Moreno
García  proposes  increased  investment  in  studying  connections  between
Egypt on the one hand and Sudan and South Asia on the other, as well as in
comparative  work  that  includes  East  Asia  or  the  Americas.  In  his  view,

Egyptology’s  survival  depends  on  the  rejection  of  “any  isolationist
template”  and  on  prioritizing  dialogue  with  other  traditions.  Even  so,
creating  closer  contacts  with  scholars  of  Greece  and  Rome  does  not  even
rate a mention.70

The times when others might have been flattered to be admitted onto the
exclusive  premises  of  the  Classics  community  are  long  gone.  As  an
opportunistic  strategy  to  incorporate  the  study  of  Greco-Roman-adjacent
cultures  in  order  to  embed  and  thereby  stabilize  engagement  with  the
traditional  twin  core,  Classics+  ought  to  be  of  limited  appeal.  As  Morley
observes, from the vantage point of Classics, cultural exchanges are “often
interpreted in terms of contacts between ‘the Mediterranean world’ (as our
primary  focus)  and  exterior  forces;  but  it’s  equally  possible,  and
increasingly productive, to switch perspectives, and see the Mediterranean
as  the  western  extremity  of  Eurasia.”  Classics+  is  not  set  up  to
accommodate the latter.71

Conversely,  any  expansion  of  scope  and  content  that  results  in  truly
balanced  coverage  is  likely  to  erode  the  very  concept  of  Classics,  and
correspondingly  unlikely  to  be  implemented.  In  Nakassis’s  sketch,  “a  real
‘ancient Mediterranean studies’ department of the same size might look like
this:  3  Romanists,  3  Hellenists,  3  Egyptologists,  3  Assyriologists,  and  2
additional  scholars  to  cover  any  major  gaps  that  remained.…  That
configuration  would  mean  shrinking  the  Greco-Roman  core  of  the
department  from  13  to  6  (or  8).  We  could  suppose  that  our  new  ‘ancient
Mediterranean studies’ departments would be much larger in size than our
current  Classics  departments,  but  that  seems  naïve.  If  we  are  really
committed  to  studying  the  ancient  Mediterranean,  we  need  to  actually
reform  our  disciplinary  structures  and  curricula  to  do  so.  But  that  seems,
frankly,  unlikely.  Much  more  likely  is  that  such  units  will  retain  their
Greco-Roman  core,  add  an  Egyptologist  or  Assyriologist,  and  call  it  a
day.”72

Moreover, there is already little if any contact between those who seek to
globalize ancient  history  and archaeology and those  who focus  on literary
and reception studies. The more expansionist and decentered Classics were
to  become  in  terms  of  space  and  time,  the  less  would  be  left  of  its

interdisciplinary synergies: whatever case one might currently still be able
to  make  for  Classics  as  a  source  of  interdisciplinary  stimulation  would
simply collapse.73

Expansionism  offers  little  comfort  to  reformers  who  remain  committed
to  the  Classics  template.  If  classicists  dabble  in  reception  studies  or
selectively  coopt  the  occasional  colleague  who  specializes  in  neighboring
cultures, the field will not really change. Well-established priorities such as
the  primacy  of  Greece  and  Rome  might  even  be  reinforced  rather  than
overcome. If on the other hand reception studies and global ancient world
studies take over, Greco-Roman Classics effectively ceases to exist. Either
way, expansionism is not a viable strategy for ensuring the survival of the
field.

Crisis without Alternative

None of these five approaches makes a compelling case for the preservation
of  Classics  as  a  separate  academic  field.  In  fact,  only  one  of  them—
organicism—is intrinsically predicated on the Classics model, whereas the
preferences  and  objectives  of  the  other  four  can  be  served  just  as  well  or
even better by alternative configurations.

Organicism is a faded relic of academic history: stubbornly parochial in
its  denial  of  the  success  of  alternative  conceptions,  it  is  in  practice  either
blithely  coercive  (by  fetishizing  language  skills)  or  toothlessly  nominal.
Exceptionalism not only carries a heavy ideological baggage; its defense of
Classics as an organizational model fails even on its own terms. Pragmatism
is  as  cynical  as  it  is  shortsighted,  and  a  dead  end  in  the  long  run.
Progressivism  is  caught  up  in  internal  contradictions  and,  if  taken  to  its
logical conclusion, would end Classics anyway. The same is even more true
of expansionism, the least timid versions of which would likewise cause the
field to disintegrate.

What  we  end  up  with  is  an  array  of  options,  from  Classics  (politically
fraught, organizationally redundant), its mirror image Scissalc (a relabeled
but substantively unchanged version of Classics), Classics+ (composed of a
hegemonic  Greco-Roman  core  with  some  opportunistic  appendages),
Classics++  (most  likely  a  full-fledged  fusion  of  Classics,  Egyptology,  and

Ancient Near Eastern Studies), and “Classics” (such as Global Classics or
Ancient  World  Studies,  which  may  or  may  not  retain  the  label  but  either
way are no longer Classics).

In  short,  Classics  may  try  to  carry  on  as  Classics,  or  it  may  change  so
much  that  it  turns  into  something  else.  Yet  Classics  as  Classics  is
impervious to reform: if it finds itself in crisis, there is no exit option that
promises to preserve the template itself.

Crisis is not necessarily a calamity, and can even become an opportunity.
Crisis  may  force  us  to  confront  the  possibility  that  for  all  it  has
accomplished, Classics may simply have run its course. It helps to visualize
this  as  a  trilemma  in  which  only  two  out  of  three  options  are  available  at
once.  The  three  elements  are  (1)  intellectual  ambition  and  potential,  (2)
established  disciplinary  requirements  and  expectations,  and  (3)  concerns
about inclusion, in terms of both subject matter and contemporary access.

Conservative  or  reactionary  critiques  tend  to  conflate  1  and  2  in  an
attempt to cast the problem as a dilemma between 1/2 and 3. Yet the actual
trade-off occurs between 1 and 3 on the one hand and 2 on the other. Both 1
and  3  are  inherently  forward-looking,  progressive  if  you  will,  in  an
intellectual or ideological sense, whereas 2 is by nature fixated on the past
in its effort to maintain the status quo (which is itself but a faint echo of a
quasi-mythical Golden Age of classical scholarship).

We  can  debate  until  the  end  of  days  whether  Classics  is  intrinsically
conservative, but that debate is not in the end very interesting or helpful. At
the  same  time,  there  can  be  no  doubt  at  all  that  2  reinforces  whatever
conservative  properties  happen  to  define  Classics  as  a  field,  as  a  way  of
approaching  the  ancient  past.  By  contrast,  both  1  and  3  open  up  new
approaches,  and  in  so  doing  raise  hopes  that  this  engagement  may  in  fact
have a future.

A  different  future  does  not  condemn  past  practices,  or  abandon  their
harvests. There is plenty to be preserved from what Classics has produced,
and  to  be  employed  in  forging  new  ways  of  making  sense  of  what  it  has
studied and taught with such determination and perseverance. Conservation
and  preservation  come  in  many  forms:  they  should  not  privilege

organizational structures and traditions but content and access. Willingness
to let go of the former may in the end prove to be a better way of preserving
and strengthening the latter.

Let  us  return  one  more  time  to  Ram-Prasad,  who  arrives  at  the  same
conclusion: “As strange as it feels to put this in writing—as a Classics PhD
student—I arrive at the following conclusion: if the subject itself has been
built up over centuries in such a way that it is impossible to separate it from
an  oppressive,  colonial  mindset,  maybe  it  needs  to  die.  Not  the  subject
matter: of course we should study the literature, history, philosophy, art, and
archaeology of ancient Rome and Greece. I didn’t choose to stay in Classics
to be perverse; I share in the same love of the material that motivates us all.
But,  perhaps,  the  subject  has  had  its  time.  We  absolutely  cannot  give
Classics  a  free  pass  when  it  comes  to  decolonization.  As  the  rest  of  the
humanities  progress,  Classics  is  going  to  look  increasingly  ugly: a subject
that resolutely refuses to challenge its white supremacist foundations, while
its proponents earnestly suggest that ‘if it’s not Rome or Greece, it’s just not
our job.’ ”74

This is not just a provocation by a newcomer. In the same year but from
the  opposite  end  of  the  academic  status  scale,  Joseph  Farrell,  Ivy  League
named-chair-holder and distinguished expert on Augustan poetry, just about
the  most  canonical  enterprise  in  the  field  of  Classics,  expressed  similar
misgivings in his presidential address at the annual meeting of the Society
for  Classical  Studies.  The  founders  of  the  field,  he  observed,  “put  a  very
high value … on the unity of classical civilization,” insisting “on this unity
to  an  unusual  degree.  Every  aspect  of  ancient  Greek  and  Roman
civilization,  they  argued,  was  related  to  every  other  aspect  in  a  way  that
they thought simply untrue of other historical periods, and that could never
be  true  of  our  own,  radically  heterogeneous  modern  world.”  Thus  they
“constructed  our  object  of  study  as  something  that  is  the  opposite  of  the
modern  world,  and  as  something  that  possessed  characteristics  different
from those that any other period of human existence has ever known.”

The consequences for scholarly practice today are serious: “On one side,
we all have to thank our founders for constituting our discipline as one that
deserved  departmental  status,  because  if  we  didn’t  have  that  in  a  large

number of institutions, I wonder whether we would have survived this long.
On  the  other  side,  it  is  not  entirely  satisfactory  that  we  have  outlived  the
rationale on which our discipline is based. That is true both because, in my
opinion,  it  is  not  a  good  thing  for  a  discipline  to  justify  its  existence  on
principles that most of its practitioners would not care to defend; and also
in  other  disciplines
because  a  significant  number  of  colleagues
understandably  expect  us  to  defend  these  principles,  even  if  they  are
amazed that anyone would.”75

It is striking that in an entire book called Classics: Why It Matters, the
similarly  well-established  British  Classics  professor  Neville  Morley  is
unable or unwilling to make a case for the survival of the Classics template,
as opposed to the study of Greece and Rome as such. All he can muster is
“What holds it together is a shared commitment to the investigation of the
societies  of  classical  antiquity,  not  because  they  are  thought  to  be
intrinsically superior or more essential for ‘Western Civilization’ than other
societies, but simply because these different scholars have all developed an
interest in this fascinating topic rather than another.” That is probably true
but  does  not  require  the  field  to  exist  in  its  present  format.  Nor  does  his
equally plausible observation that “Classical antiquity remains an important
part  of  the  past  that  matters  in  Europe  and  North  America;  in  its  actual
impact  on  the  development  of  present-day  societies,  and  in  its  continuing
role as a powerful cultural idea, even a myth.”76

For  a  more  full-throated  defense  of  Classics  as  Classics,  consider  an
apologia  put  forth  by  the  then-Cambridge  classicist  David  Butterfield  in
2022.  In  his  telling,  the  dismantling  of  the  field  would  yield  three  major
negative consequences.77

First,  “the  loss  of  linguistic  expertise  would  necessarily  remove  depth
and technical sophistication from a large swathe of Classical research, and
blunt the effectiveness of future students and researchers of the subject.” (A
reminder,  prominently  placed,  of  the  hegemony  of  language  skills,  and  of
the  implicit  equation  of  those  skills  and  the  field  itself—a  welcome
reminder, lest I be accused of exaggerating the issue.)

Second,  “the  loss  of  faith  in  the  humanities,  and  in  the  wide  range  of
skills  that  Classics  cannot  but  instill,  would  reduce  uptake  of  the  subject,
which in the numbers-driven world of the modern university sector would
soon result in the loss of full-time academic posts.” (A cross between non
sequitur—how  would  the  administrative  reorganization  of  the  study  of
Greece and Rome undermine “faith in the humanities?”—and insistence on
self-preservation for self-preservation’s sake.)

And  third,  “the  loss  of  confidence  in  Classics  as  a  self-standing
discipline would result in the formal breakdown of the subject, first being
merged  with  others,  and  then  steadily  submerged  by  the  inevitable  weight
of  everything  else  that  is  accorded  equal  weight.”  (A  bold  return  to
Romantic-style  exceptionalism—after  all,  how  dare  one  accord  “equal
weight”  to  other  cultures?—served  up  with  a  heavy  dose  of  defeatism:  in
the  absence  of  special  protections,  the  study  of  Greece  and  Rome  would
become so devalued and delegitimated as to make its demise “inevitable.”)

Butterfield considers a level playing field the kiss of death, conjuring up
the  specter  of  “Classics  becoming  merged  as  an  indistinct  part  of  some
amorphous  megadiscipline  of  world  history,  where  no  discipline  is  given
resources ahead of any other.” Imagine an approach to the past in which no
one area is afforded a priori privilege. That, it seems, would be the end of
our  engagement  with  Greece  and  Rome,  unless  progressive  change
manages to kill it off first: after all, “the self-devouring nature of the strains
of activism we now see pose a collective danger to the health of Classics as
a whole.”

“Most importantly, Classicists worthy of the name should be driven by a
passion  for  keeping  the  subject  alive.”  Why  is  that  so  desirable?  Because
“to be serious—although the metaphor may seem unduly romantic to many
readers—once  snuffed  out,  the  flame  of  Classics  could  not  and  would  not
be  rekindled.”  This  takes  us  back—by  chance  or  design?—all  the  way  to
Friedrich  Jacobs’s  sermon  of  1807:  “With  renewed  love  we  returned  to
antiquity. The holy fire had not yet been extinguished under the overturned
altars; the columns of its temples still stood fast.”78

I  allow  for  the  possibility  of  design  not  only  because  of  Butterfield’s
explicit allusion to Romanticism but also because his centerpiece reads as if

it  had  been  lifted  from  Wolf  and  his  contemporaries,  updated  in  more
contemporary language: “Certainly it is true that to focus on such outcomes
inevitably  overlooks  what  may  be  the  greatest  advantages  of  studying  the
Greeks  and  Romans.  What  I  mean  by  this  is  that,  because  we  can  get  so
close to these peoples, and because their cultural output was so broad and
rich, we are given a kaleidoscope of insights into the human condition, at its
extremes  of  success  and  failure,  power  and  impotence,  freedom  and
servitude, and beyond. For those pursuing a humanities degree, who seek to
understand  what  can  be  taken  as  universal  human  values  (and  to  discover
those  that  cannot),  the  sheer  material  available  from  the  Greeks  and
Romans is of immense evidential value. This can only be put in grandiose
terms, of course, but exploring Classics holistically—combining literature,
history,  philosophy,  archaeology,  art,  linguistics,  sociology,  and  anything
else  that  can  help—forces  one  to  consider,  on  an  almost  unimaginably
broad  canvas,  the  biggest  questions  about  life  and  about  the  world.  These
may  prove  to  be  abstract  and  perhaps  insoluble  questions,  but  that  does
nothing  to  deprive  them  of  their  central  importance  to  researching
humanities subjects.”79

Classics,  in  short,  is  both  incomparably  valuable  and  incomparably
fragile. It is hard to imagine a more damning self-indictment: an academic
field  that  depends  on  the  resuscitation  of  premises  from  200  to  250  years
ago—language  fetishization,  crude  exceptionalism,  worship  of  Romantic
organicism—a  field  that  claims  a  special  place  at  the  trough  yet  betrays  a
morbid  pessimism  by  insisting  that  were  overt  privileges  withdrawn,  it
would be doomed to perish; such a field can only be declared intellectually
bankrupt and ought to be put out of its misery.

To  be  sure,  this  heady  mix  of  reactionary  politics,  fatalistic  doomsday
talk,  and  selfish  cynicism  might  readily  be  dismissed  as  an  unfortunate,
unrepresentative  outlier.  And  it  may  well  be  true  that  few  contemporary
classicists  would  embrace  this  vision  in  its  totality.  But  that  is  beside  the
point.  Butterfield  merely  distills  and  combines  with  extreme  concision
perfectly  conventional  elements  of  mainstream  preservationist  discourse,
most  notably  exceptionalism  (Classics  ultimately  deserves  its  position),
organicism (Classics can only survive as a separate field), and pragmatism

(let’s  not  rock  the  boat  or  we’ll  all  drown).  What  is  more,  in  so  fervently
condemning  both  progressive  critiques  and  globalizing  efforts,  Butterfield
shows  excellent  instinct—the  intuitive  realization,  all  too  rarely  if  ever
found  among  proponents  of  reform,  that  those  moves  are  not  really
upgrades but Trojan horses, perfectly capable of finishing off Classics as a
field, and indeed likely to do so if applied with vigor.

Where Does That Leave Ancient History?

Historians of the ancient world stand to benefit from Classics’ crisis without
alternative.  For
them,  exceptionalism,  organicism,  pragmatism,  and
progressivism cannot contribute to an adequate globalizing reorientation of
their own work. Only the fifth option, expansionism, may do so, at least if it
is  taken  far  enough.  “Far  enough”  means  three  things  above  all:  genuine
decentering; equal rights for words and things; and a truly global reach. By
the  time  these  conditions  are  met,  any  residue  of  the  Classics  template
would long be gone.

As Evan Jewell reminds us from the perspective of a scholar of Greece
and Rome who is based in a History department, “the discipline of classics
itself was created by and for another time and place—that is, 19th-century
Europe.” Our engagement with ancient history does and should not rely on
it. Wolf’s generation had put it in a box: “we are permitted to restrict even
the term antiquity in an exclusionary manner to the two peoples that were
refined by their intellectual culture, learning and art”—that is, to the ancient
Greek  and  Romans,  aka  GreeceandRome.  The  time  has  come  to  break  up
what remains of that box.80

Historians of Greece and Rome need not fret about this outcome. Thanks
to the ancient Mediterranean roots of many religious, political, and cultural
phenomena that are of global importance today, such scholars are bound to
play a major role in any future globalized study and teaching of the ancient
world.81

That said, although the dismantling of Classics as a field will remove a
big  obstacle  to  those  future  developments,  it  will  not  by  itself  bring  them
about. Much hinges on the question of how global ancient or foundational
history  can  be  organized  and  institutionalized.  History  programs  tend  to

marginalize even practitioners of Greco-Roman ancient history, and for the
most part restrict engagement with any other parts of the ancient world to
the shallowest of introductory world history surveys.

To some extent this imbalance may be attributed to the very existence of
Classics and other ancient area studies fields. At (usually better-resourced)
institutions that maintain such programs, it is perfectly rational for History
departments to minimize or eliminate investment in the ancient world and
reserve  precious  faculty  positions  for  other  specialisms.  The  abolition  of
separate  ancient  area  studies  programs  would  make  this  attitude  harder  to
justify.

Even so, Classics and cognate programs bear only part of the blame. The
demotion of the more distant past also stems from a more general tendency,
that of focusing research and (perhaps especially) teaching on more recent
periods of history that can be more readily and more directly connected to
current  conditions.  Such  presentist  undercurrents  can  never  be  fully
eliminated, nor should they be. It is perfectly reasonable to turn to whatever
history promises to throw the most light on the roads that were taken (or not
taken) in making our world, and on what was lost in the process. Ancient
historians must mobilize this curiosity for their own purposes by making the
strongest possible case for the profound relevance of their subject matter for
understanding the present. This, of course, is the whole point of reframing
“ancient” history as “foundational” history.

This is, however, easier said than done. I am not aware of any academic
system  that  fully  incorporates  the  entire  ancient  world  into  History
programs.  To  name  just  one  example,  in  Germany,  where  the  holistic
Classics template has been almost completely abandoned, only historians of
ancient  Greece  and  Rome  are  routinely  admitted  to  History  departments.
Worse still, in institutions that employ a sufficiently large number of these
scholars  they  tend  to  form  their  own  subunits  within  larger  History
programs. This encourages even greater fragmentation than that fostered by
Classics,  as Greco-Roman  historians  end up being  institutionally detached
from those who study other aspects of Greece and Rome, or other parts of
the ancient world, or more recent history—an outcome that is in a sense the

worst  of  all  possible  worlds.  Much  German  scholarship  in  this  area  bears
the scars of this multiple isolation.82

And  while  other  national  traditions  may  differ  in  specific  ways,  these
problems  tend  to  be  widespread.  The  disappearance  of  the  model  of
Altertumswissenschaft  from  continental  Europe  has  generally  failed  to
promote  more  inclusive  ancient  studies.  Thus,  while  the  dismantling  of
Classics  is  a  necessary  precondition  for  establishing  a  viable  field  of
planetary foundational history, it is clearly not sufficient.

German academia also highlights the challenges of teamwork as a means
of  globalizing  early  history.  There,  governmental  entities  and  foundations
have  long  been  providing  relatively  generous  funding  for  collaborative
projects  and  initiatives  in  the  humanities,  including  historical  fields.  Yet
none  of  this  has  led  to  any  discernible  reorientation  of  scholarly  practice.
The reason for this is not hard to find: such arrangements clash with deeply
entrenched  modes  of  training,  hiring,  evaluation,  and  promotion  that  are
predicated  on  and  promote  academic  individualism  across  the  humanities.
Efforts  to  support  collaboration  do  not  align  with  underlying  incentive
structures.  In  this  context,  teamwork  tends  to  remain  opportunistic  and
short-term, deployed as a means of bridging funding and employment gaps
between individualized training and individualized teaching and research.

This  raises  an  obvious  question:  what,  in  light  of  all  of  this,  can  we
actually  hope  to  accomplish?  It  is  all  well  and  good  to  criticize  current
practices, and to imagine how things might or should be done instead. But
we must also confront the thorny issue of feasibility.
